\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{i}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{j}\rVert\leq\kappa(\kappa(\alpha+1)\cdot\mathsf{OPT}+\kappa(\alpha+1)\cdot\mathsf{OPT})=2\kappa^{2}(\alpha+1)\cdot\mathsf{OPT}.

Thus, for the pairwise distance vector
\mathbf{d}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell^{2}}
computed in line 1 of Algorithm
3
, at least
(9/10)^{2}\cdot\ell^{2}\geq 8/10\cdot\ell^{2}
of the distances will be upper bounded by
2\kappa^{2}(\alpha+1)\cdot\mathsf{OPT}
. Thus, the threshold
\tau
computed in Line
1
, which is the
80^{th}
percentile of the distances, gives a lower bound
\tau\leq 2\kappa^{2}(\alpha+1)\cdot\mathsf{OPT}
. In Line
2
, we return any
\mathbf{x}_{i}
with
\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{i}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{j}\rVert\leq\tau
for at least
1/2\cdot\ell
vectors
\mathbf{x}_{j}
. First observe that at least one such
\mathbf{x}_{i}
must exist. Otherwise, at most
1/2\cdot\ell^{2}
of the pairwise distances would lie below
\tau
.

Additionally, observe that since at least
9/10\cdot\ell
of the
\mathbf{x}_{i}
are good, if
\mathbf{x}_{i}
is returned, it must have
\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{i}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{j}\rVert\leq\tau\leq 2\kappa^{2}(\alpha+1)\cdot\mathsf{OPT}
for at least one good
\mathbf{x}_{j}
. Since this good
\mathbf{x}_{j}
has
\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{j}-\mathbf{b}\rVert\leq\alpha\cdot\mathsf{OPT}
, by approximate triangle inequality, the returned
\mathbf{x}_{i}
must then satisfy

\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{i}-\mathbf{b}\rVert\leq\kappa(\alpha+2\kappa^{2}(\alpha+1))=(\kappa\alpha+2\kappa^{3}(\alpha+1))\cdot\mathsf{OPT}.

∎

3.2
Relative Error Approximation

We next show how to achieve a relative error
(1+\epsilon)
solution to Problem
1.1
. The relative error algorithm simply solves a Lewis weight sampled regression problem multiple times and outputs a boosted solution as done in Theorem
3.3
. Pseudocode is given in Algorithm
5
below.

Algorithm 4

Recursive relative error
\ell_{p}
regression

input:
Matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, measurement vector
\mathbf{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, number of measurements
m
.

output:
Approximate solution
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
to
\min_{\mathbf{x}}\|\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\|_{p}
.

1:

if

n\leq m

then

2:

Compute
\bar{\mathbf{x}}
with
\lVert\mathbf{A}\bar{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{b}\rVert_{p}\leq(1+\gamma)\cdot\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\rVert_{p}
for
\gamma\geq 0
.

3:

return

\bar{\mathbf{x}}

4:

end

if

5:

Let
\mathbf{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
be generated via Algorithm
1
.

6:

Run Algorithm
4
with inputs
\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{S}\mathbf{b}
to get
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}
.

7:

return

\tilde{\mathbf{x}}

Algorithm 5

High probability relative error
\ell_{p}
regression

input:
Matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, measurement vector
\mathbf{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, number of measurements
m
.

output:
Approximate solution
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
to
\min_{\mathbf{x}}\|\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\|_{p}
.

1:

Run Algorithm
4
with inputs
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{b}
independently
\ell
times for
\ell=O(\log\frac{1}{\delta})
.

2:

Run Algorithm
2
(and
3
) to find
\mathbf{x}_{c}
such that
\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{c}-\mathbf{b}\rVert_{p}\leq\alpha\min_{\mathbf{x}}\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\rVert_{p}
for
\alpha>0
.

3:

Discard the
1/10
fraction of trials with the largest
\left\lVert\mathbf{S}(\mathbf{b}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{c})\right\rVert_{p}^{p}
.

4:

Run Algorithm
3
on the remaining candidate solutions.

Theorem 3.4

(Main Result)
.

Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
,
\mathbf{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and
0<p<\infty
. Let

m=O(1)\frac{d^{1\lor(p/2)}}{\epsilon^{2\lor p}}\left[(\log d)^{2}\log n+\log\frac{1}{\delta}\right]\log\frac{1}{\delta}.

Then, with probability at least
1-\delta
, Algorithm
5
returns
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\tilde{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{p}\leq(1+\epsilon)\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{p}

and nonadaptively reads at most
m
entries of
\mathbf{b}
.

Our main result, Theorem
1.2
, follows directly from Theorem
3.4
, up to a
\log n
factor that is replaced by a
\log(d/\epsilon)
factor by results in Section
3.5
, as well as an optimization in the
\epsilon
dependence for
p\in(1,2)
as done in Section
3.4
.
The proof of Theorem
3.4
proceeds in 2 steps. We first show in Lemma
3.5
that any entries of the residual
\mathbf{z}=\mathbf{b}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}
whose contribution to
\lVert\mathbf{z}\rVert_{p}^{p}
is significantly larger than the corresponding Lewis weight in
\mathbf{A}
can be effectively ignored, since no
\mathbf{x}
achieving small error can accurately fit such entries. We then show in Lemma
3.8
that if we ignore these entries, sampling by the
\ell_{p}
Lewis weights approximately preserves
\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{z}\rVert_{p}
for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. The result follows by combining Lemmas
3.5
and
3.8
.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
and
\mathbf{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and let
0<p<\infty
. Let
\mathbf{z}=\mathbf{b}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Let

\mathcal{B}\coloneqq\left\{i\in[n]:\frac{|\mathbf{z}(i)|^{p}}{\mathsf{OPT}^{p}}\geq\frac{d^{\max(0,p/2-1)}\cdot\mathbf{w}_{i}^{p}(\mathbf{A})}{\epsilon^{p}}\right\}

Let
\bar{\mathbf{z}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be equal to
\mathbf{z}
but with all entries in
\mathcal{B}
set to
0
. Then for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{p}=O(\mathsf{OPT})
,

\left|\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{z}\rVert_{p}^{p}-\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\rVert_{p}^{p}-\left\lVert\mathbf{z}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}\right|=O(\epsilon)\cdot\mathsf{OPT}^{p}.

Proof.

For any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and any
i\in\mathcal{B}
, we have that

\displaystyle|[\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}](i)|^{p}

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{p}^{p}\cdot\mathbf{s}_{i}^{p}(\mathbf{A})

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{p}^{p}\cdot d^{\max(0,p/2-1)}\cdot\mathbf{w}^{p}_{i}(\mathbf{A})

Lemma
2.5

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{p}^{p}\cdot\epsilon^{p}\cdot\frac{|\mathbf{z}(i)|^{p}}{\mathsf{OPT}^{p}}=O(\epsilon^{p})\cdot|\mathbf{z}(i)|^{p},

where we use the assumption that
\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{p}^{p}=O(\mathsf{OPT}^{p})
.
From the above, we have that

\displaystyle|[\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}](i)-\mathbf{z}(i)|^{p}-|[\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}](i)-\bar{\mathbf{z}}(i)|^{p}

\displaystyle=|[\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}](i)-\mathbf{z}(i)|^{p}-|[\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}](i)|^{p}=(1\pm O(\epsilon))\cdot|\mathbf{z}(i)|^{p}

for
i\in\mathcal{B}
.
Since
\lVert\mathbf{z}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\rVert_{p}^{p}\leq\lVert\mathbf{z}\rVert_{p}^{p}=\mathsf{OPT}^{p}
,

\displaystyle\left|\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{z}\rVert_{p}^{p}-\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\rVert_{p}^{p}-\lVert\mathbf{z}\rVert_{p}^{p}\right|=O(\epsilon)\cdot\lVert\mathbf{z}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\rVert_{p}^{p}=O(\epsilon)\cdot\mathsf{OPT}^{p}.

∎

Lemma 3.6
.

Consider the setting of Lemma
3.5
. Let
\mathbf{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
be formed as in Line
5
of Algorithm
4
. With probability at least
99/100
,
\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{z}\rVert_{p}^{p}=O(\mathsf{OPT}^{p})
and further, for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{p}=O(\mathsf{OPT})
,

\left|\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{S}\mathbf{z}\rVert_{p}^{p}-\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{S}\bar{\mathbf{z}}\rVert_{p}^{p}-\lVert\mathbf{S}(\mathbf{z}-\bar{\mathbf{z}})\rVert_{p}^{p}\right|=O(\epsilon)\cdot\mathsf{OPT}^{p}.

Proof.

The proof follows that of Lemma
3.5
. We have, for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and any
i\in\mathcal{B}
sampled by
\mathbf{S}
,

\displaystyle|[\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}](i)-\mathbf{S}\mathbf{z}(i)|^{p}-|[\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}](i)-\mathbf{S}\bar{\mathbf{z}}(i)|^{p}

\displaystyle=|[\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}](i)-\mathbf{S}\mathbf{z}(i)|^{p}-|[\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}](i)|^{p}

\displaystyle=(1\pm O(\epsilon))\cdot|\mathbf{S}\mathbf{z}(i)|^{p},

where we have used that
\mathbf{S}
is a subspace embedding so
\left\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{p}=O(\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{p})=O(\mathsf{OPT})
. Now,
\E[\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{z}\rVert_{p}^{p}]=\lVert\mathbf{z}\rVert_{p}^{p}=O(\mathsf{OPT}^{p})
. Then by Markov’s inequality, with probability at least
99/100
,
\lVert\mathbf{S}(\mathbf{z}-\bar{\mathbf{z}})\rVert_{p}^{p}\leq\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{z}\rVert_{p}^{p}=O(\mathsf{OPT}^{p})
. We then have

\displaystyle\left|\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{S}\mathbf{z}\rVert_{p}^{p}-\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{S}\bar{\mathbf{z}}\rVert_{p}^{p}-\lVert\mathbf{S}(\mathbf{z}-\bar{\mathbf{z}})\rVert_{p}^{p}\right|=O(\epsilon)\cdot\lVert\mathbf{S}(\mathbf{z}-\bar{\mathbf{z}})\rVert_{p}^{p}=O(\epsilon)\cdot\mathsf{OPT}^{p}.

∎

Remark 3.7
.

While Lemma
3.6
only succeeds with constant probability, we may boost it by repeating the procedure
\ell=O(\log\frac{1}{\delta})
times, sorting the trials by
\left\lVert\mathbf{S}(\mathbf{b}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{c})\right\rVert_{p}^{p}
for a constant factor solution
\mathbf{x}_{c}
, and then discarding the top
1/10
of trials (see Line
3
). By Chernoff bounds, we will then select a trial satisfying

\left\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{z}\right\rVert_{p}\leq\left\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{x}^{*}-\mathbf{x}_{c})\right\rVert_{p}+\left\lVert\mathbf{S}(\mathbf{b}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{c})\right\rVert_{p}=O(\mathsf{OPT})

with probability at least
1-\delta
.

We now state the following lemma, which shows that Lewis weight sampling as in Line
5
of Algorithm
4
will approximately preserve the
\ell_{p}
norms of
\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\right\rVert_{p}
, provided that
\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{p}=O(\mathsf{OPT})
.

Lemma 3.8
.

Consider the setting of Lemma
3.5
. Let
\mathbf{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
be formed as in Line
5
of Algorithm
4
. Let

\Lambda\coloneqq\sup_{\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{p}\leq O(\mathsf{OPT})}\left\lvert\left\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{S}\bar{\mathbf{z}}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}-\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}\right\rvert.

Then,

\Pr\left\{\Lambda\geq\left[C\frac{1}{n}\frac{d^{1\lor(p/2)}}{\epsilon^{0\lor(p-2)}}\right]^{1/2}\left[(\log d)\sqrt{\log n}+z\right]\mathsf{OPT}^{p}\right\}\leq 2\exp(-z^{2})

We defer the proof of Lemma
3.8
to Section
3.3
and first show how to use it to prove Theorem
3.4
.

Proof of Theorem
3.4
.

Let
\mathbf{S}
be the sketching matrix generated as a result of the
O(\log n)
rounds of recursion. As in the proof of Theorem
2.11
, the algorithm makes at most
O(\log n)
levels of recursion with probability at least
1-\delta
, and generates a
\mathbf{S}
such that

\Pr\left\{\sup_{\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{p}\leq O(\mathsf{OPT})}\left\lvert\left\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{S}\bar{\mathbf{z}}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}-\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}\right\rvert\geq\epsilon\mathsf{OPT}^{p}\right\}\leq\frac{\delta}{\log\frac{1}{\delta}}

Consider
\ell=O(\log\frac{1}{\delta})
independent repetitions of Algorithm
4
. By a union bound over all
\ell
repetitions, the above guarantee holds simultaneously for all repetitions. Note also that with probability at least
1-\delta
, we only keep trials such that
\left\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{z}\right\rVert_{p}=O(\mathsf{OPT})
in Line
3
. Furthermore, with probability at least
1-\delta
, the boosting algorithm of Theorem
3.3
selects a run outputting a constant factor approximation. Let
\bar{\mathbf{x}}
be the solution that is output by the algorithm. Since
\bar{\mathbf{x}}
is a constant factor approximation, we have the guarantee that

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\bar{\mathbf{x}}\right\rVert_{p}\leq\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\bar{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{z}\right\rVert_{p}+\left\lVert\mathbf{z}\right\rVert_{p}=O(\mathsf{OPT}).

Similarly,
\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}\right\rVert_{p}=O(\mathsf{OPT})
, and thus
\left\lVert\mathbf{A}(\bar{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{x}^{*})\right\rVert_{p}=O(\mathsf{OPT})
. Then,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\bar{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\mathbf{A}(\bar{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{x}^{*})-\mathbf{z}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\mathbf{A}(\bar{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{x}^{*})-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}+\left\lVert\bar{\mathbf{z}}-\mathbf{z}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}+O(\epsilon)\cdot\mathsf{OPT}^{p}

Lemma
3.5

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}(\bar{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{x}^{*})-\mathbf{S}\bar{\mathbf{z}}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}+\left\lVert\bar{\mathbf{z}}-\mathbf{z}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}+O(\epsilon)\cdot\mathsf{OPT}^{p}

Lemma
3.8

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}(\bar{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{x}^{*})-\mathbf{S}\mathbf{z}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}-\left\lVert\mathbf{S}(\mathbf{z}-\bar{\mathbf{z}})\right\rVert_{p}^{p}+\left\lVert\bar{\mathbf{z}}-\mathbf{z}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}+O(\epsilon)\cdot\mathsf{OPT}^{p}

Lemma
3.6

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{S}\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}-\left\lVert\mathbf{S}(\mathbf{z}-\bar{\mathbf{z}})\right\rVert_{p}^{p}+\left\lVert\bar{\mathbf{z}}-\mathbf{z}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}+O(\epsilon)\cdot\mathsf{OPT}^{p}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}-\mathbf{S}\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}-\left\lVert\mathbf{S}(\mathbf{z}-\bar{\mathbf{z}})\right\rVert_{p}^{p}+\left\lVert\bar{\mathbf{z}}-\mathbf{z}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}+O(\epsilon)\cdot\mathsf{OPT}^{p}

near optimality of
\bar{\mathbf{x}}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}-\mathbf{S}\bar{\mathbf{z}}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}+\left\lVert\bar{\mathbf{z}}-\mathbf{z}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}+O(\epsilon)\cdot\mathsf{OPT}^{p}

Lemma
3.6

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}+\left\lVert\bar{\mathbf{z}}-\mathbf{z}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}+O(\epsilon)\cdot\mathsf{OPT}^{p}

Lemma
3.8

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}-\mathbf{z}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}+O(\epsilon)\cdot\mathsf{OPT}^{p}

\displaystyle\text{Lemma \ref{lem:clip}}\qed

3.3
Proof of Lemma
3.8

We return to proving Lemma
3.8
, which shows that Lewis weight sampling using the Lewis weights of
\mathbf{A}
can preserve the norm of
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}
. To do this, we will modify the proofs of the Lewis weight sampling subspace embeddings of
[
LT91
,
SZ01
]
. We return to the framework of Section
2.2.1
which considers sampling each row with probability
1/2
under the assumption that
\mathbf{A}
has
\ell_{p}
Lewis weights bounded by
O(d/n)
. However, this time, we must preserve the norm of
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}
rather than
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}
. We thus consider the quantity

\Lambda\coloneqq\sup_{\mathbf{y}\in T}\left\lvert\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}\left\lvert\mathbf{y}(i)\right\rvert^{p}\right\rvert=\sup_{\mathbf{y}\in T}\left\lvert\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{w}_{i}\sigma_{i}\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{y}](i)\right\rvert^{p}\right\rvert

where
T\coloneqq\left\{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}:\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{p}\leq O(\mathsf{OPT})\right\}
and
\mathbf{w}_{i}
are the
\ell_{p}
Lewis weights of
\mathbf{A}
which are bounded by
O(d/n)
. We now handle this by considering three different parameter regimes:
p\in(2,\infty)
,
p\in(1,2)
, and
p\in(0,1)
. Note that we exclude
p=1
and
p=2
, as they have already been handled by previous work
[
CP19
,
PPP21
,
CD21
]
. Throughout this section, we assume that
\mathsf{OPT}=O(1)
by scaling.

We first adapt the work of
[
LT91
]
to handle the cases of
p\in(1,2)
and
p\in(2,\infty)
. The following is an adaptation of Lemma 15.17 of
[
LT91
]
, which is essentially the statement that Lewis weights of
\mathbf{A}
uniformly bound the sensitivities over the set
T
, which is in turn a combination of Lemma
2.5
and the definition of
\mathcal{B}
in Lemma
3.5
.

Lemma 3.9

(Sensitivity Bounds)
.

Let
p\geq 1
and
\mathbf{y}\in T
. Then,

\left\lVert\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq O\left\lparen\frac{d^{0\lor(1/2-1/p)}}{\epsilon}\right\rparen\mathsf{OPT}

Proof.

Write
\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}
for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. For any coordinate
i\in[n]
, we have

\displaystyle\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{y}](i)\right\rvert^{p}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{w}_{i}^{-1}\left\lvert[\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}](i)\right\rvert^{p}

\displaystyle\leq O(2^{p})\mathbf{w}_{i}^{-1}(\left\lvert[\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}](i)\right\rvert^{p}+\left\lvert[\bar{\mathbf{z}}](i)\right\rvert^{p})

\displaystyle\leq O(2^{p})\mathbf{w}_{i}^{-1}\cdot\mathbf{w}_{i}d^{0\lor(p/2-1)}\left[\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}+\frac{1}{\epsilon^{p}}\left\lVert\bar{\mathbf{z}}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}\right]

\displaystyle=O(2^{p})\frac{d^{0\lor(p/2-1)}}{\epsilon^{p}}\mathsf{OPT}^{p}

Taking
p
th roots yields the desired result.
∎

We now separately handle indices
i\in[n]
such that the Lewis weights
\mathbf{w}_{i}
are less than some
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(\epsilon/n)
.

Definition 3.10
.

Define the set
J\subseteq[n]
by

J\coloneqq\left\{i\in[n]:\mathbf{w}_{i}\geq\frac{\epsilon^{p}d}{n^{2}}\right\}.

For indices not contained in
J
, we use the following simple bound:

Lemma 3.11
.

We have with probability
1
that

\sup_{\mathbf{y}\in T}\left\lvert\sum_{i\notin J}\mathbf{w}_{i}\sigma_{i}\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{y}](i)\right\rvert^{p}\right\rvert\leq O\left\lparen\frac{d^{1\lor(p/2)}}{n}\right\rparen\mathsf{OPT}^{p}

Proof.

For any
\mathbf{y}\in T
, we have that

\displaystyle\left\lvert\sum_{i\notin J}\mathbf{w}_{i}\sigma_{i}\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{y}](i)\right\rvert^{p}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i\notin J}\mathbf{w}_{i}\left\lVert\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{\infty}^{p}

triangle inequality

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i\notin J}\mathbf{w}_{i}O\left\lparen\frac{d^{0\lor(p/2-1)}}{\epsilon^{p}}\right\rparen\mathsf{OPT}^{p}

Lemma
3.9

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i\notin J}\frac{\epsilon^{p}d}{n^{2}}\cdot O\left\lparen\frac{d^{0\lor(p/2-1)}}{\epsilon^{p}}\right\rparen\mathsf{OPT}^{p}

i\notin J

\displaystyle\leq O\left\lparen\frac{d^{1\lor(p/2)}}{n}\right\rparen\mathsf{OPT}^{p}.\qed

For the indices contained in
J
, we use a comparison theorem for Rademacher and Gaussian averages, and then use Dudley’s entropy integral (Theorem
3.12
) to bound the corresponding Gaussian process.

Theorem 3.12

(Dudley’s entropy integral (Theorem 11.17,
[
LT91
]
))
.

Let
\{X_{t}\}_{t\in T}
be a Gaussian process. Define the pseudo-metric on
T
via

d_{X}(s,t)\coloneqq\left\lVert X_{s}-X_{t}\right\rVert_{2}.

Let
E(T,d_{X},u)
denote the minimal number of
d_{X}
-balls of radius
u
required to cover
T
. Then,

\E\sup_{t\in T}X_{t}\leq 24\int_{0}^{\infty}\sqrt{\log E(T,B_{X},u)}~du.

We first use that
\mathbf{w}_{i}\leq O(d/n)
to bound

\E\sup_{\mathbf{y}\in T}\left\lvert\sum_{i\in J}\mathbf{w}_{i}\sigma_{i}\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{y}](i)\right\rvert^{p}\right\rvert\leq\frac{O(1)}{\sqrt{n}}\cdot\E\sup_{\mathbf{y}\in T}\left\lvert\sum_{i\in J}(d\mathbf{w}_{i})^{1/2}\sigma_{i}\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{y}](i)\right\rvert^{p}\right\rvert

(4)

We now study the latter quantity, with Rademacher variables
\sigma_{i}
replaced by standard Gaussian variables
g_{i}
.

Definition 3.13
.

Let
\{X_{\mathbf{y}}\}_{\mathbf{y}\in T}
be the Gaussian process defined by

X_{\mathbf{y}}\coloneqq\sum_{i\in J}(d\mathbf{w}_{i})^{1/2}g_{i}\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{y}](i)\right\rvert^{p}.

Note that the pseudo-metric
d_{X}
associated with this Gaussian process (see Theorem
3.12
) is

\displaystyle d_{X}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\left\lVert X_{\mathbf{y}}-X_{\mathbf{y}^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\E\left[\sum_{i\in J}(d\mathbf{w}_{i})^{1/2}g_{i}\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{y}](i)\right\rvert^{p}-\sum_{i\in J}(d\mathbf{w}_{i})^{1/2}g_{i}\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{y}^{\prime}](i)\right\rvert^{p}\right]^{2}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\E\left[\sum_{i\in J}(d\mathbf{w}_{i})^{1/2}g_{i}(\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{y}](i)\right\rvert^{p}-\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{y}^{\prime}](i)\right\rvert^{p})\right]^{2}}

\displaystyle=O(1)\sqrt{\sum_{i\in J}d\mathbf{w}_{i}(\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{y}](i)\right\rvert^{p}-\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{y}^{\prime}](i)\right\rvert^{p})^{2}}

For tail bounds, we can use the same Rademacher contraction principle
[
LT91
, Theorem 4.12]
and Rademacher average comparison
[
LT91
, Equation 4.8]
theorems along with a standard comparison lemma due to Panchenko
[
Pan03
, Lemma 1]
.

3.3.1
Entropy Bounds

To apply Dudley’s entropy integral theorem, we now require estimates on the metric entropy
E(T,d_{X},u)
. For easier entropy calculations, we bound
d_{X}
by a more convenient metric, adapting Equations (15.18) and (15.19) in
[
LT91
]
to encorporate the change in Lemma
3.9
.

Lemma 3.14

(Bounds on
d_{X}
)
.

For
\mathbf{y},\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\in T
, we have the following:

•

if
2\leq p<\infty
, then

d_{X}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\leq O(p\sqrt{d})O\left\lparen\frac{d^{1/2-1/p}}{\epsilon}\right\rparen^{p/2-1}\left\lVert(\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{y}^{\prime})|_{J}\right\rVert_{\infty}

•

if
1<p<2
, then

d_{X}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\leq O(\sqrt{d})\left\lVert(\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{y}^{\prime})|_{J}\right\rVert_{\infty}^{p/2}

Proof.

We first handle
p\geq 2
. For
a,b\geq 0
, we have by convexity for
p>1
that

a^{p}-b^{p}\leq p(a^{p-1}+b^{p-1})\left\lvert a-b\right\rvert.

Then,

\displaystyle d_{X}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq O(d)\sum_{i\in J}\mathbf{w}_{i}\left\lparen\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{y}](i)\right\rvert^{p}-\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{y}^{\prime}](i)\right\rvert^{p}\right\rparen^{2}

\displaystyle\leq O(p^{2}d)\left\lVert(\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{y}^{\prime})|_{J}\right\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\sum_{i\in J}\mathbf{w}_{i}\max\left\{\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{y}](i)\right\rvert,\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{y}^{\prime}](i)\right\rvert\right\}^{2(p-1)}.

We then use Lemma
3.9
to bound

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in J}\mathbf{w}_{i}\max\left\{\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{y}](i)\right\rvert,\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{y}^{\prime}](i)\right\rvert\right\}^{2(p-1)}

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\max\left\{\left\lVert\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{\infty},\left\lVert\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{\infty}\right\}^{p-2}\sum_{i\in J}\mathbf{w}_{i}\left\lparen\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{y}](i)\right\rvert^{p}+\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{y}^{\prime}](i)\right\rvert^{p}\right\rparen

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle O\left\lparen\frac{d^{1/2-1/p}}{\epsilon}\mathsf{OPT}\right\rparen^{p-2}(\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}+\left\lVert\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{p}^{p})\leq O\left\lparen\frac{d^{1/2-1/p}}{\epsilon}\right\rparen^{p-2}

so combining the bounds and taking square roots gives the result.

For
1<p<2
, we have using the subadditivity of
|\cdot|^{p/2}
that

\displaystyle d_{X}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq O(d)\sum_{i\in J}\mathbf{w}_{i}\left\lparen\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{y}](i)\right\rvert^{p}-\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{y}^{\prime}](i)\right\rvert^{p}\right\rparen^{2}

\displaystyle=O(d)\sum_{i\in J}\mathbf{w}_{i}\left\lparen\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{y}](i)\right\rvert^{p/2}-\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{y}^{\prime}](i)\right\rvert^{p/2}\right\rparen^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 64.00003pt\left\lparen\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{y}](i)\right\rvert^{p/2}+\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{y}^{\prime}](i)\right\rvert^{p/2}\right\rparen^{2}

\displaystyle\leq O(d)\sum_{i\in J}\mathbf{w}_{i}\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{y}^{\prime}](i)\right\rvert^{p}\left\lparen\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{y}](i)\right\rvert^{p}+\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{y}^{\prime}](i)\right\rvert^{p}\right\rparen

\displaystyle\leq O(d)\left\lVert(\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{y}^{\prime})|_{J}\right\rVert_{\infty}^{p}(\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}+\left\lVert\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{p}^{p})

\displaystyle\leq O(d)\left\lVert(\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{y}^{\prime})|_{J}\right\rVert_{\infty}^{p}.\qed

Because
\mathbf{w}_{i}
is bounded below for
i\in J
, we may further bound

\left\lVert(\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{y}^{\prime})|_{J}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq O(1)\left\lVert\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\right\rVert_{\mathbf{w},q}

(5)

for
q=O(\log n)
, where

\left\lVert\mathbf{v}\right\rVert_{\mathbf{w},q}\coloneqq\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\mathbf{w}_{i}}{d}\left\lvert\mathbf{v}(i)\right\rvert^{q}\right]^{1/q}.

Indeed, if
i\in J
, then

\frac{1}{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(n)}\sum_{i\in J}\left\lvert\mathbf{v}(i)\right\rvert^{q}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\mathbf{w}_{i}}{d}\left\lvert\mathbf{v}(i)\right\rvert^{q}=\left\lVert\mathbf{v}\right\rVert_{\mathbf{w},q}^{q}

so
\left\lVert\mathbf{v}|_{J}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq O(1)\left\lVert\mathbf{v}\right\rVert_{\mathbf{w},q}
.

Now by the results of Lemma
3.14
and Equation (
5
), we can replace
d_{X}
balls by
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathbf{w},q}
balls of an appropriate radius. Furthermore, we may note that the set
T
is simply a translation of
\ell_{p}
ball of radius
O(\mathsf{OPT})=O(1)
by
\bar{\mathbf{z}}
, so it suffices to cover the unit
\ell_{p}
ball
B_{p}
by
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathbf{w},q}
balls. These are exactly the types of entropy estimates given by
[
BLM89
,
LT91
]
. Indeed, we have the following result:

Lemma 3.15

(Propositions 15.18 and 15.19,
[
LT91
]
)
.

Let
1<p<\infty
and
q=O(\log n)
. Let
E
be the subspace spanned by the columns of
\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{A}
and let
B_{\mathbf{w},p}\subseteq E
denote the unit
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathbf{w},p}
-ball in this subspace. Then, for some universal constant
C>0
, the following holds:

•

if
2\leq p<\infty
, then

\log E(B_{\mathbf{w},p},\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathbf{w},q},u)\leq C\frac{d\log n}{u^{2}}.

•

if
1<p\leq 2
, then

\log E(B_{\mathbf{w},p},\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathbf{w},q},u)\leq C\frac{d\log n}{u^{p}}.

For
u
tending to
0
, a standard volume argument gives a better bound:

Lemma 3.16
.

Consider the setting of Lemma
3.15
. Then, for some universal constant
C>0
we have that

\log E(B_{\mathbf{w},p},\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathbf{w},q},u)\leq Cd\log\frac{d}{u}.

Diameter Bounds.

Finally, we give diameter bounds for the application of the Dudley tail bound of Theorem
2.9
.

Lemma 3.17
.

The
d_{X}
-diameter of
T
is bounded by
[O(d^{p/2}/\epsilon^{p-2})]^{1/2}
for
2\leq p<\infty
and
O(\sqrt{d})
for
1<p<2
.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}
and
\mathbf{y}^{\prime}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}
be two points in
T
. Then,

\displaystyle\left\lVert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{y}^{\prime})]|_{J}\right\rVert_{\infty}^{p}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}^{\prime})]|_{J}\right\rVert_{\infty}^{p}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\right\rVert_{\infty}^{p}

\displaystyle\leq\max_{i=1}^{n}O(d^{0\lor(p/2-1)})\mathbf{w}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{w}_{i}^{-1}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\right\rVert_{p}^{p}

\displaystyle=O(d^{0\lor(p/2-1)})\left\lVert\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}.

It follows from the above calculation and Lemma
3.14
that the
d_{X}
-diameter of the set
T
is bounded by

\displaystyle\sup_{\mathbf{y},\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\in T}d_{X}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\mathbf{y},\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\in T}O(p\sqrt{d})O\left\lparen\frac{d^{1/2-1/p}}{\epsilon}\right\rparen^{p/2-1}\left\lVert(\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{y}^{\prime})|_{J}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\left[O(1)\frac{d^{p/2}}{\epsilon^{p-2}}\right]^{1/2}

for
2\leq p<\infty
and

\displaystyle\sup_{\mathbf{y},\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\in T}d_{X}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\mathbf{y},\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\in T}O(\sqrt{d})\left\lVert(\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{y}^{\prime})|_{J}\right\rVert_{\infty}^{p/2}\leq O(\sqrt{d})

for
1<p<2
.
∎

3.3.2
Proof for
2<p<\infty

Proof of Lemma
3.8
,
2<p<\infty
.

We will calculate Dudley’s entropy integral. As previously noted, we may replace
T
by the set

B_{p}\coloneqq\left\{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}:\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{p}\leq\mathsf{OPT}\right\}=\left\{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}:\left\lVert\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{\mathbf{w},p}\leq\frac{\mathsf{OPT}}{d^{1/p}}\right\}=\frac{\Theta(1)}{d^{1/p}}\mathbf{W}^{1/p}(B_{\mathbf{w},p})

since translations do not change cover numbers. Furthermore, we have by Lemma
3.14
and Equation (
5
) that for

\alpha=O(p\sqrt{d})O\left\lparen\frac{d^{1/2-1/p}}{\epsilon}\right\rparen^{p/2-1},

we have that

\displaystyle\log E(B_{p},d_{X},t)

\displaystyle\leq\log E(\Theta(d^{-1/p})B_{\mathbf{w},p},\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathbf{w},q},t/\alpha)

\displaystyle=\log E(B_{\mathbf{w},p},\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathbf{w},q},\Theta(d^{1/p}t/\alpha))

since linear transformations do not change cover numbers. Then, by Theorem
3.12
, we have that

\displaystyle\E\sup_{\mathbf{y}\in T}\left\lvert(d\mathbf{w}_{i})^{1/2}g_{i}\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{y}](i)\right\rvert^{p}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle 24\int_{0}^{\infty}\sqrt{\log E(T,d_{X},u)}~du

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\frac{O(\alpha)}{d^{1/p}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\sqrt{\log E(B_{\mathbf{w},p},\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathbf{w},q},u)}~du

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\frac{O(\alpha)}{d^{1/p}}\left[\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{\log E(B_{\mathbf{w},p},\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathbf{w},q},u)}~du+\int_{1}^{\infty}\sqrt{\log E(B_{\mathbf{w},p},\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathbf{w},q},u)}~du\right]

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\frac{O(\alpha)}{d^{1/p}}\left[\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{d\log\frac{d}{u}}~du+\int_{1}^{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(d)}\frac{\sqrt{d\log n}}{u}~du\right]

(Lemmas
3.15
and
3.16
)

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle O(1)\frac{(d^{p/2})^{1/2}(\log d)\sqrt{\log n}}{\epsilon^{p/2-1}}.

Combining the above bound with Equation (
4
) and Lemma
3.11
yields the result that

\E\Lambda\leq\left[O(1)\frac{1}{n}\frac{d^{p/2}(\log d)^{2}\log n}{\epsilon^{p-2}}\right]^{1/2}.

Furthermore, applying Theorem
2.9
and Lemma
3.17
yields the result that

\Pr\left\{\Lambda\geq C\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\left[\frac{d^{p/2}}{\epsilon^{p-2}}\right]^{1/2}\left[(\log d)\sqrt{\log n}+z\right]\right\}\leq 2\exp(-z^{2}).\qed

3.3.3
Proof for
1<p<2

Proof of Lemma
3.8
,
1<p<2
.

Our proof is very similar to that of
2<p<\infty
. By combining the reasoning of the proof for
2<p<\infty
with Lemma
3.14
and Equation (
5
), we obtain that

\displaystyle\log E(T,d_{X},t)

\displaystyle\leq\log E(\Theta(d^{-1/p})B_{\mathbf{w},p},\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathbf{w},q},(t/\sqrt{d})^{2/p})

\displaystyle=\log E(B_{\mathbf{w},p},\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathbf{w},q},\Theta(d^{1/p})(t/\sqrt{d})^{2/p})

\displaystyle=\log E(B_{\mathbf{w},p},\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathbf{w},q},\Theta(t^{2/p})).

Then by Theorem
3.12
, we have that

\displaystyle\E\sup_{\mathbf{y}\in T}\left\lvert(d\mathbf{w}_{i})^{1/2}g_{i}\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{y}](i)\right\rvert^{p}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle 24\int_{0}^{\infty}\sqrt{\log E(T,d_{X},u)}~du

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle O(1)\int_{0}^{\infty}\sqrt{\log E(B_{\mathbf{w},p},\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathbf{w},q},u^{2/p})}~du

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle O(1)\left[\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{\log E(B_{\mathbf{w},p},\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathbf{w},q},u^{2/p})}~du+\int_{1}^{\infty}\sqrt{\log E(B_{\mathbf{w},p},\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathbf{w},q},u^{2/p})}~du\right]

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle O(1)\left[\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{d\log\frac{d}{u}}~du+\int_{1}^{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(d)}\frac{\sqrt{d\log n}}{u}~du\right]

(Lemmas
3.15
and
3.16
)

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle O(1)\sqrt{d}(\log d)\sqrt{\log n}.

Combining the above bound with Equation (
4
) and Lemma
3.11
yields the result that

\E\Lambda\leq\left[O(1)\frac{1}{n}d(\log d)^{2}\log n\right]^{1/2}.

Furthermore, applying Theorem
2.9
and Lemma
3.17
yields the result that

\Pr\left\{\Lambda\geq C\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sqrt{d}\left[(\log d)\sqrt{\log n}+z\right]\right\}\leq 2\exp(-z^{2}).\qed

3.3.4
Proof for
0<p<1

Note that in the proof of
1<p<2
, essentially the only thing that changed between the active setting and the proof of the subspace embedding was the translation by a vector
\bar{\mathbf{z}}
with norm at most
O(\mathsf{OPT})=O(1)
, which did not affect the entropy integral bound. In the case of
0<p<1
, the proof of
[
SZ01
]
is structured similarly and can be easily checked to have essentially the same straightforward modifications.

3.4
Sharp Dependence on
\epsilon
for
1<p<2

We will now optimize our dependence on
\epsilon
for
1<p<2
.

3.4.1
Closeness of Near-Optimal Solutions

We first show that near-optimal solutions
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
are close to the optimal solution
\mathbf{x}^{*}
, using strong convexity with respect to the
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{p}
norm.

Theorem 3.18
.

Let
1<p\leq 2
. Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
and
\mathbf{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Then, for any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{p}\leq(1+\gamma)\mathsf{OPT}

with
\gamma\in(0,1)
, we have that

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}\right\rVert_{p}\leq O(\sqrt{\gamma})\mathsf{OPT},

where
\mathbf{x}^{*}\coloneqq\arg\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{p}

Proof.

For
p=2
, this follows from the Pythagorean theorem. Otherwise, note first that the KKT conditions require that

\left\langle(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}-\mathbf{b})^{p-1},\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rangle=0

for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, where the power to
p-1
denotes the signed entrywise power. Then by the strong convexity of
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{p}^{2}
with respect to
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{p}

[
BMN01
, Lemma 8.1]
, we have that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{p}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{p}^{2}+2\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{p}^{2-p}\left\langle(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}-\mathbf{b})^{p-1},\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}\right\rangle+\frac{p-1}{2}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}\right\rVert_{p}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{p}^{2}+\frac{p-1}{2}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}\right\rVert_{p}^{2}

which rearranges to

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}\right\rVert_{p}\leq\sqrt{\frac{\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{p}^{2}-\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{p}^{2}}{(p-1)/2}}\leq\sqrt{\frac{O(\gamma)\mathsf{OPT}^{2}}{(p-1)/2}}\leq O(\sqrt{\gamma})\mathsf{OPT}.\qed

3.4.2
Cost Difference

By Theorem
3.18
, if we first obtain a
(1+\gamma)
-approximate solution, then this solution must be within a distance of
O(\sqrt{\gamma})\mathsf{OPT}
from the optimum in the
\ell_{p}
norm. Thus, we may in fact just analyze the distortion of the sampling process when restricted to a ball of radius
O(\sqrt{\gamma})\mathsf{OPT}
. In this case, we will show that we obtain improved approximations.

We now consider sampling each row independently with probability
1/2
. This can be expressed as multiplying each row by
(1+\sigma_{i})
for independent Rademacher variables
\sigma_{i}\in\{\pm 1\}
for
i\in[n]
.w Suppose that we find
\hat{\mathbf{x}}\coloneqq\arg\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}}\hat{f}(\mathbf{x})
for

\hat{f}(\mathbf{x})\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{n}(1+\sigma_{i})\left\lvert[\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}](i)\right\rvert^{p}.

(6)

Then,

\displaystyle f(\hat{\mathbf{x}})-f(\mathbf{x}^{*})

\displaystyle=[f(\hat{\mathbf{x}})-\hat{f}(\hat{\mathbf{x}})]+[\hat{f}(\hat{\mathbf{x}})-\hat{f}(\mathbf{x}^{*})]+[\hat{f}(\mathbf{x}^{*})-f(\mathbf{x}^{*})]

\displaystyle\leq[f(\hat{\mathbf{x}})-\hat{f}(\hat{\mathbf{x}})]-[f(\mathbf{x}^{*})-\hat{f}(\mathbf{x}^{*})]

where we have used that
\hat{f}(\hat{\mathbf{x}})\leq\hat{f}(\mathbf{x}^{*})
. Thus, the difference in the quality of the two solutions
\hat{\mathbf{x}}
and
\mathbf{x}^{*}
depends on the
difference in
f-\hat{f}
between
\hat{\mathbf{x}}
and
\mathbf{x}^{*}
. We thus define the difference function

\bar{f}(\mathbf{x})\coloneqq f(\mathbf{x})-\hat{f}(\mathbf{x})

for our analyses. Note then that

\bar{f}(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}\left\lvert[\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}](i)\right\rvert^{p}

up to switching the signs on the Rademacher variables
\sigma_{i}
. The problem then is to bound
\left\lvert\bar{f}(\mathbf{x}_{1})-\bar{f}(\mathbf{x}_{2})\right\rvert
for
\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2}
such that
\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{1}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{2}\right\rVert_{p}\leq O(\sqrt{\gamma})\mathsf{OPT}
. We will do so by bounding

\left\lvert\bar{f}(\mathbf{x})-\bar{f}(0)\right\rvert=\left\lvert\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}\left\lparen\left\lvert[\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}](i)\right\rvert^{p}-\left\lvert\mathbf{b}(i)\right\rvert^{p}\right\rparen\right\rvert

for
\mathbf{x}
with
\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{p}\leq O(\sqrt{\gamma})\mathsf{OPT}
. We thus define

\Lambda\coloneqq\sup_{\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{p}\leq O(\sqrt{\gamma})\mathsf{OPT}}\left\lvert\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}\left\lparen\left\lvert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\right\rvert^{p}-\left\lvert\mathbf{b}(i)\right\rvert^{p}\right\rparen\right\rvert.

We will now bound this quantity.

3.4.3
Chaining Argument

For a subset
S\subseteq[n]
, define

\Lambda_{S}\coloneqq\sup_{\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{p}\leq O(\sqrt{\gamma})\mathsf{OPT}}\left\lvert\sum_{i\in S}\sigma_{i}\left\lparen\left\lvert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\right\rvert^{p}-\left\lvert\mathbf{b}(i)\right\rvert^{p}\right\rparen\right\rvert.

Via a Gaussian comparison theorem (see, e.g.,
[
CP15
,
CSS21
]
), one can show that

\displaystyle\E_{\Omega}\Lambda_{S}

\displaystyle\leq\E_{\Omega}\E_{\sigma}\sup_{\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{p}\leq O(\sqrt{\gamma})\mathsf{OPT}}\left\lvert\sum_{i\in S}\sigma_{i}\cdot\left\lparen\left\lvert[\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}](i)\right\rvert^{p}-\left\lvert\mathbf{b}(i)\right\rvert^{p}\right\rparen\right\rvert

(7)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2\pi}\E_{\Omega}\E_{g}\sup_{\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{p}\leq O(\sqrt{\gamma})\mathsf{OPT}}\left\lvert\sum_{i\in S}g_{i}\cdot\left\lparen\left\lvert[\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}](i)\right\rvert^{p}-\left\lvert\mathbf{b}(i)\right\rvert^{p}\right\rparen\right\rvert,

where
\sigma_{i}
are independent Rademacher variables and
g_{i}
are independent standard Gaussian variables.

By Lemma
3.5
, we may assume that
\left\lVert\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq O(1/\epsilon)\mathsf{OPT}
. Now as done previously, we first handle the coordinates whose Lewis weights are very small:

Lemma 3.19
.

Suppose that
\left\lVert\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq O(1/\epsilon)\mathsf{OPT}
. Let

R\coloneqq\left\{i\in[n]:\mathbf{w}_{i}^{p}(\mathbf{A})<\sqrt{\frac{\gamma d}{n}}\frac{\epsilon^{p}}{n}\right\}.

Then,
\Lambda_{R}\leq O(\sqrt{\gamma d/n})\mathsf{OPT}^{p}
almost surely.

Proof.

Using (
7
), we have that

\displaystyle\Lambda_{R}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{p}\leq O(\sqrt{\gamma})\mathsf{OPT}}\left\lvert\sum_{i\in R}\sigma_{i}\cdot\left\lparen\left\lvert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\right\rvert^{p}-\left\lvert\mathbf{b}(i)\right\rvert^{p}\right\rparen\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{p}\leq O(\sqrt{\gamma})\mathsf{OPT}}\sum_{i\in R}\left\lvert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\right\rvert^{p}+\left\lvert\mathbf{b}(i)\right\rvert^{p}

\displaystyle\leq O(1)\sup_{\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{p}\leq O(\sqrt{\gamma})\mathsf{OPT}}\sum_{i\in R}\mathbf{w}_{i}^{p}(\mathbf{A})\left[\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}+\frac{\sqrt{\gamma}^{p}}{\epsilon^{p}}\mathsf{OPT}^{p}\right]

\displaystyle\leq O(1)\cdot n\cdot\sqrt{\frac{\gamma d}{n}}\frac{\epsilon^{p}}{n}\cdot\frac{1}{\epsilon^{p}}\mathsf{OPT}^{p}

\displaystyle\leq O(\sqrt{\gamma d/n})\mathsf{OPT}^{p}.\qed

For the remaining coordinates, we partition into two sets: those with large coordinates of
\mathbf{b}
and those with small coordinates. Define the sets

\displaystyle L_{\mathbf{b}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{i\in[n]\setminus R:\left\lvert\mathbf{b}(i)\right\rvert\geq C\sqrt{\gamma}\cdot\mathbf{w}_{i}^{p}(\mathbf{A})^{1/p}\mathsf{OPT}\right\}

\displaystyle S_{\mathbf{b}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{i\in[n]\setminus R:\left\lvert\mathbf{b}(i)\right\rvert<C\sqrt{\gamma}\cdot\mathbf{w}_{i}^{p}(\mathbf{A})^{1/p}\mathsf{OPT}\right\}

where
R
is as defined in Lemma
3.19
and
C>0
is a sufficiently large constant. We then bound
\E\Lambda_{[n]\setminus R}
as

\displaystyle\E\Lambda_{[n]\setminus R}

\displaystyle\leq\E\sup_{\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{p}\leq O(\sqrt{\gamma})\mathsf{OPT}}\left\lvert\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus R}\sigma_{i}\cdot\left\lparen\left\lvert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\right\rvert^{p}-\left\lvert\mathbf{b}(i)\right\rvert^{p}\right\rparen\right\rvert

(8)

\displaystyle\leq\E\sup_{\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{p}\leq O(\sqrt{\gamma})\mathsf{OPT}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\left\lvert\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus R}(d\mathbf{w}_{i})^{1/2}\sigma_{i}\cdot\left\lparen\left\lvert\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b})\right\rvert^{p}-\left\lvert\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{b}(i)\right\rvert^{p}\right\rparen\right\rvert

as done previously, and bound the latter term by bounding the corresponding terms for
L_{\mathbf{b}}
and
S_{\mathbf{b}}
. We now bound the corresponding Gaussian processes given by

X_{\mathbf{y}}\coloneqq C_{S}\sum_{i\in S}(d\mathbf{w}_{i})^{1/2}g_{i}\cdot\left\lparen\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{b})](i)\right\rvert^{p}-\left\lvert\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{b}(i)\right\rvert^{p}\right\rparen

for
\mathbf{y}\in\left\{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}:\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{p}\leq O(\sqrt{\gamma})\mathsf{OPT}\right\}
, where
S
can be either
L_{\mathbf{b}}
or
S_{\mathbf{b}}
, and
C_{S}
is a normalizing constant associated with
S
. Our first task is to bound the associated metric
d_{X}
.

Lemma 3.20

(Metric for
S_{\mathbf{b}}
)
.

Let
C_{S}\coloneqq 1/\sqrt{\gamma}^{p}\mathsf{OPT}^{p}
. Consider the metric

\displaystyle d_{X}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\lVert X_{\mathbf{y}}-X_{\mathbf{y}^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\gamma}^{p}\mathsf{OPT}^{p}}\left[\sum_{i\in S_{\mathbf{b}}}(d\mathbf{w}_{i})\left\lparen\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{b})](i)\right\rvert^{p}-\left\lvert\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}(\mathbf{y}^{\prime}-\mathbf{b}(i))\right\rvert^{p}\right\rparen^{2}\right]^{1/2}

for
\mathbf{y},\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\in\left\{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}:\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{p}\leq O(\sqrt{\gamma})\mathsf{OPT}\right\}
. Then,

d_{X}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\leq O(\sqrt{d})\frac{\left\lVert(\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{y}^{\prime})|_{S_{\mathbf{b}}}\right\rVert_{\infty}^{p/2}}{\sqrt{\gamma}^{p/2}\mathsf{OPT}^{p/2}}

Proof.

The proof follows from a straightforward adaptation of Lemma
3.14
.
∎

Bounding the metric for
L_{\mathbf{b}}
requires a different approach than the metric used in
[
LT91
]
.

Lemma 3.21

(Metric for
L_{\mathbf{b}}
)
.

Let
C_{S}\coloneqq 1/\sqrt{\gamma}\mathsf{OPT}^{p}
. Consider the metric

\displaystyle d_{X}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\lVert X_{\mathbf{y}}-X_{\mathbf{y}^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\gamma}\mathsf{OPT}^{p}}\left[\sum_{i\in L_{\mathbf{b}}}(d\mathbf{w}_{i})\left\lparen\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{b})](i)\right\rvert^{p}-\left\lvert\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}(\mathbf{y}^{\prime}-\mathbf{b}(i))\right\rvert^{p}\right\rparen^{2}\right]^{1/2}

for
\mathbf{y},\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\in\left\{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}:\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{p}\leq O(\sqrt{\gamma})\mathsf{OPT}\right\}
. Then,

d_{X}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\leq O(\sqrt{d})\frac{\left\lVert(\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{y}^{\prime}))|_{L_{\mathbf{b}}}\right\rVert_{\infty}^{p/2}}{\sqrt{\gamma}^{p/2}\mathsf{OPT}^{p/2}}

Proof.

Note that

\displaystyle\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{b})](i)\right\rvert^{p}

\displaystyle=\left\lparen\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}(\mathbf{y}^{\prime}-\mathbf{b})](i)\right\rvert\pm\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{y}^{\prime})](i)\right\rvert\right\rparen^{p}

\displaystyle=\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}(\mathbf{y}^{\prime}-\mathbf{b})](i)\right\rvert^{p}\pm O(1)\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{y}^{\prime})](i)\right\rvert\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}(\mathbf{y}^{\prime}-\mathbf{b})](i)\right\rvert^{p-1}

since
i\in L_{\mathbf{b}}
. Thus,

\displaystyle\left\lvert\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{b})](i)\right\rvert^{p}-\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}(\mathbf{y}^{\prime}-\mathbf{b})](i)\right\rvert^{p}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq O(1)\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{y}^{\prime})](i)\right\rvert\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}(\mathbf{y}^{\prime}-\mathbf{b})](i)\right\rvert^{p-1}

so we have that

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in L_{\mathbf{b}}}(d\mathbf{w}_{i})\left\lparen\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{b})](i)\right\rvert^{p}-\left\lvert\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}(\mathbf{y}^{\prime}-\mathbf{b}(i))\right\rvert^{p}\right\rparen^{2}

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle O\left\lparen d\right\rparen\left\lVert(\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{y}^{\prime}))|_{L_{\mathbf{b}}}\right\rVert_{\infty}^{p}\sum_{i\in L_{\mathbf{b}}}\mathbf{w}_{i}\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{y}^{\prime})](i)\right\rvert^{2-p}\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}(\mathbf{y}^{\prime}-\mathbf{b})](i)\right\rvert^{2p-2}.

Now note that
\frac{p}{2-p}>1
and
\frac{p}{2p-2}>1
are Hölder conjugates for
1<p<2
. Then, Hölder’s inequality on the sum yields

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in L_{\mathbf{b}}}\mathbf{w}_{i}\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{y}^{\prime})](i)\right\rvert^{2-p}\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}(\mathbf{y}^{\prime}-\mathbf{b})](i)\right\rvert^{2p-2}

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\left\lparen\sum_{i\in L_{\mathbf{b}}}\mathbf{w}_{i}\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{y}^{\prime})](i)\right\rvert^{p}\right\rparen^{\frac{2-p}{p}}\left\lparen\sum_{i\in L_{\mathbf{b}}}\mathbf{w}_{i}\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}(\mathbf{y}^{\prime}-\mathbf{b})](i)\right\rvert^{p}\right\rparen^{1-\frac{2-p}{p}}

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{p}^{2-p}\left\lVert\mathbf{y}^{\prime}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{p}^{2p-2}\leq O(\sqrt{\gamma}^{2-p})\mathsf{OPT}^{p}.

Combining these bounds yields the claim.
∎

Now given the bounds on the metrics, we can finish by using the entropy bounds from Section
3.3.1
and then calculating Dudley’s entropy integral as done in Section
3.3.3
.

Lemma 3.22
.

We have that

\E\Lambda\leq O(1)\left[\gamma\frac{d}{n}(\log d)^{2}(\log n)\right]^{1/2}\mathsf{OPT}^{p}

and

\Pr\left\{\Lambda\geq C\sqrt{\frac{\gamma d}{n}}\left[(\log d)\sqrt{\log n}+z\right]\mathsf{OPT}^{p}\right\}\leq 2\exp(-z^{2}).

Proof.

Our proof is very similar to that of Lemma
3.8
for
1<p<2
. Let

T=\left\{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}:\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{p}\leq O(\sqrt{\gamma})\mathsf{OPT}\right\}.

Then, as reasoned in Lemma
3.8
for
1<p<2
, for the Gaussian process
X_{\mathbf{y}}
associated with
S_{\mathbf{b}}
(see Lemma
3.20
),

\displaystyle\log E(T,d_{X},t)

\displaystyle\leq\log E(\Theta(\sqrt{\gamma}d^{-1/p}\mathsf{OPT})B_{\mathbf{w},p},\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathbf{w},q},(t/\sqrt{d})^{2/p}\sqrt{\gamma}\mathsf{OPT})

\displaystyle=\log E(B_{\mathbf{w},p},\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathbf{w},q},\Theta(t^{2/p})).

Similarly, for the Gaussian process
X_{\mathbf{y}}
associated with
L_{\mathbf{b}}
(see Lemma
3.21
),

\log E(T,d_{X},t)\leq\log E(B_{\mathbf{w},p},\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathbf{w},q},\Theta(t^{2/p})).

Then by Theorem
3.12
, following the calculation in Lemma
3.8
for
1<p<2
, we have that

\displaystyle\E\sup_{\mathbf{y}\in T}\left\lvert X_{\mathbf{y}}\right\rvert\leq O(1)\sqrt{d}(\log d)\sqrt{\log n}

for both of the Gaussian processes. We combine this with (
8
) to obtain

\displaystyle\E\Lambda_{[n]\setminus R}

\displaystyle\leq O(1)\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\left[\frac{1}{C_{L_{\mathbf{b}}}}\sqrt{d}(\log d)\sqrt{\log n}+\frac{1}{C_{S_{\mathbf{b}}}}\sqrt{d}(\log d)\sqrt{\log n}\right]

\displaystyle\leq O(1)\frac{1}{n}\sqrt{d}(\log d)\sqrt{\log n}[\sqrt{\gamma}\mathsf{OPT}^{p}+\sqrt{\gamma}^{p}\mathsf{OPT}^{p}]

\displaystyle\leq O(1)\left[\gamma\frac{d}{n}(\log d)^{2}(\log n)\right]^{1/2}\mathsf{OPT}^{p}

where
C_{L_{\mathbf{b}}}=1/\sqrt{\gamma}\mathsf{OPT}^{p}
and
C_{S_{\mathbf{b}}}=1/\sqrt{\gamma}^{p}\mathsf{OPT}^{p}
are the
C_{S}
constants for
S=L_{\mathbf{b}}
and
S=S_{\mathbf{b}}
(see Lemmas
3.21
and
3.20
). Finally, together with Lemma
3.19
, we conclude that

\E\Lambda\leq O(1)\left[\gamma\frac{d}{n}(\log d)^{2}(\log n)\right]^{1/2}\mathsf{OPT}^{p}.

In a similar manner as Lemma
3.17
, the
d_{X}
-diameter of
T
is
O(\sqrt{d})
so Theorem
2.9
gives us that

\Pr\left\{\Lambda\geq C\sqrt{\frac{\gamma d}{n}}\left[(\log d)\sqrt{\log n}+z\right]\mathsf{OPT}^{p}\right\}\leq 2\exp(-z^{2}).\qed

3.4.4
Iterative Size Reduction

We will now assemble the previous lemmas into an iterative size reduction argument.

Because Lemma
3.22
differs from Lemma
3.8
only by a factor of
\sqrt{\gamma}
, one can show as done in Theorem
3.4
that
m
entries need to be read to produce a
(1+\epsilon)
-approximation, for

m=O(1)\frac{\gamma d}{\epsilon^{2}}\left[(\log d)^{2}\log n+\log\frac{1}{\delta}\right],

as long as it can be shown that reading
m
entries gave a
(1+\gamma)
-approximation. Now let

C=O(1)\left[(\log d)^{2}\log n+\log\frac{1}{\delta}\right]

and suppose that reading
Cd/\epsilon^{\beta}
is sufficient to obtain a
(1+\epsilon)
-approximation (for instance, Theorem
3.4
applied directly gives that
\beta\leq 2
). Then, if we set
\gamma=\epsilon^{2/(1+\beta)}
, then making
Cd/\gamma^{\beta}
gives a
(1+\gamma)
-approximation. Furthermore, note that

C\frac{\gamma d}{\epsilon^{2}}=Cd\epsilon^{\frac{2}{1+\beta}-2}=Cd\epsilon^{\frac{2-2(1+\beta)}{1+\beta}}=Cd\epsilon^{-\frac{2\beta}{1+\beta}}=C\frac{d}{\gamma^{\beta}}

so reading
Cd/\gamma^{\beta}=C\gamma d/\epsilon^{2}
entries is in fact sufficient to obtain a
(1+\epsilon)
-approximation for this setting of
\gamma
. We may now iterate this argument. Consider the sequence
\beta_{i}
given by

\beta_{1}=2,\qquad\beta_{i+1}\to\frac{2\beta_{i}}{1+\beta_{i}}.

It can be checked that the solution is

\beta_{i}=\frac{2^{i}}{2^{i}-1}=1+\frac{1}{2^{i}-1},

so applying this argument
O(\log\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
times yields that
\beta_{i}\leq 1+O(1/\log(\frac{1}{\epsilon}))
which means that reading only
O(1)Cd/\epsilon
entries suffices.

We have shown the following:

Theorem 3.23

(Main Result for
1<p<2
)
.

Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
,
\mathbf{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and
1<p<2
. Let

m=O(1)\frac{d}{\epsilon}\left[(\log d)^{2}\log n+\log\frac{1}{\delta}\right]\log\frac{1}{\delta}.

Then, with probability at least
1-\delta
, Algorithm
4
returns
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\tilde{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{p}\leq(1+\epsilon)\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{p}

and reads at most
m
entries of
\mathbf{b}
.

3.5
Removing the
\log n

As shown in Section
3
, Algorithm
4
gives a relative error approximation to
\min\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\rVert_{p}
using
\epsilon^{-2}d^{\max(1,p/2)}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(\log d,\log n)
samples from
\mathbf{b}
. However, the dependence on
\log n
is undesirable, and we now show how to replace the
\log n
dependence with a
\log(d/\epsilon)
dependence.

3.5.1
Bounding
n

Due to the use of the sophisticated chaining argument, Theorem
3.4
has an undesirable dependence on
\log n
. By replacing this net construction with a standard
\epsilon
-net, we can still show that Algorithm
4
solves
\ell_{p}
regression to
(1+\epsilon)
error, albeit using
\tilde{O}(d^{\max(1,p/2)+1}/\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(\epsilon))
samples (without dependence on
\log n
). This bound is loose by a
d
factor as compared to Theorem
3.4
due to the simpler net construction.
It can, however, be used as a preprocessing step for Theorem
3.4
, to ensure that
n
is only
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(d/\epsilon)
and so that the
\log n
dependence is removed — after sampling by Lewis weights, we are left with a problem where
n=\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(d,1/\epsilon)
. We can then apply Theorem
3.4
to further subsample this problem, achieving
\tilde{O}(d^{\max(1,p/2)}/\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(\epsilon))
sample complexity. See Algorithm
6
for pseudocode for the full approach.

We use a more standard Lewis weight sampling algorithm for this result:

Definition 3.24
.

Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
. Let
\tilde{\mathbf{w}}_{i}^{p}(\mathbf{A})\geq\mathbf{w}_{i}^{p}(\mathbf{A})
be Lewis weight upper bounds, and let
m
be a parameter. Define probabilities

\mathbf{p}_{i}\coloneqq\min\{1,m\cdot d^{0\lor(p/2-1)}\tilde{\mathbf{w}}_{i}^{p}(\mathbf{A})\}

Then,
\mathbf{S}
is a Lewis weight sampling matrix if for each
i\in[n]
,
\mathbf{S}(i,i)=\mathbf{p}_{i}^{-1/p}
independently with probability
\mathbf{p}_{i}
and
0
otherwise.

Algorithm 6

Relative error
\ell_{p}
regression – No Assumptions

input:
Matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, measurement vector
\mathbf{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

output:
Approximate solution
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
to
\min_{\mathbf{x}}\|\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\|_{p}
.

1:

Let
\mathbf{S}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{1}\times n}
be generated via Def.
3.24
for
m_{1}=O\left(d^{\max(1,p/2)+1}\cdot\frac{\log(1/\epsilon\delta)}{\epsilon^{2+p}}\right)
.

2:

Run Algorithm
4
with inputs
\mathbf{S}_{1}\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{S}_{1}\mathbf{z}
to obtain
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}
.

3:

return

\tilde{\mathbf{x}}

Theorem 3.25

(Main Result)
.

Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
,
\mathbf{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and
0<p<\infty
. Let

m=\begin{cases}O(1)\frac{d}{\epsilon^{2}}\left[(\log d)^{2}\log(d/\epsilon)+\log\frac{1}{\delta}\right]\log\frac{1}{\delta}&p\in(0,1)\\
O(1)\frac{d}{\epsilon}\left[(\log d)^{2}\log(d/\epsilon)+\log\frac{1}{\delta}\right]\log\frac{1}{\delta}&p\in(1,2)\\
O(1)\frac{d^{p/2}}{\epsilon^{p}}\left[(\log d)^{2}\log(d/\epsilon)+\log\frac{1}{\delta}\right]\log\frac{1}{\delta}&p\in(2,\infty)\end{cases}

Then, with probability at least
1-\delta
, Algorithm
6
returns
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\tilde{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{p}\leq(1+\epsilon)\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{p}

and nonadaptively reads at most
m
entries of
\mathbf{b}
.

Theorem
3.25
will follow from an analog of Lemma
3.8
, when
m=O\left(d^{\max(1,p/2)+1}\cdot\frac{\log(1/\epsilon\delta)}{\epsilon^{2+p}}\right)
. In particular:

Lemma 3.26
.

Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
,
\mathbf{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and
0<p<\infty
. Let
\mathbf{z}=\mathbf{b}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, with
\lVert\mathbf{z}\rVert_{p}=\mathsf{OPT}
. Let

\mathcal{B}\coloneqq\left\{i\in[n]:\frac{|\mathbf{z}(i)|^{p}}{\mathsf{OPT}^{p}}\geq\frac{d^{\max(0,p/2-1)}\cdot\mathbf{w}_{i}^{p}(\mathbf{A})}{\epsilon^{p}}\right\}

Let
\bar{\mathbf{z}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be equal to
\mathbf{z}
but with all entries in
\mathcal{B}
set to
0
. Let
\mathbf{S}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{1}\times n}
be formed as in Line
1
of Algorithm
6
. With probability at least
1-\delta
, for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{p}=O(\mathsf{OPT})
,

\displaystyle\left|\left\|\mathbf{S}_{1}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{S}_{1}\bar{\mathbf{z}}\right\|_{p}^{p}-\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\rVert_{p}^{p}\right|=O(\epsilon)\cdot\mathsf{OPT}^{p}.

We first use Lemma
3.26
to prove Theorem
3.25
. Then we prove the lemma.

Proof of Theorem
3.25
.

By restricting to the
m_{1}
rows sampled by Line
1
of Algorithm
6
, we have a
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(d/\epsilon)\times d
matrix
\mathbf{S}_{1}\mathbf{A}
such that

\displaystyle\left|\left\|\mathbf{S}_{1}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{S}_{1}\bar{\mathbf{z}}\right\|_{p}^{p}-\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\rVert_{p}^{p}\right|=O(\epsilon)\cdot\mathsf{OPT}^{p}

by Lemma
3.26
. In combination with Lemmas
3.5
and
3.6
, Theorem
3.2
and Lemma
3.26
give that with probability at least
1-O(\delta)
, there is some fixed
C\geq 0
with
C=O(\mathsf{OPT}^{p})
such that for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{p}=O(\mathsf{OPT})
,

\displaystyle|\lVert\mathbf{S}_{1}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{S}_{1}\mathbf{z}\rVert_{p}^{p}-\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{z}\rVert_{p}^{p}-C|=O(\epsilon)\cdot\mathsf{OPT}^{p}.

Following the proof of Theorem
3.4
, we then have that if
\bar{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
satisfies
\lVert\mathbf{S}_{1}\mathbf{A}\bar{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{S}_{1}\mathbf{z}\rVert_{p}\leq(1+\epsilon/2)\cdot\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lVert\mathbf{S}_{1}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{S}_{1}\mathbf{z}\rVert_{p}
then
\lVert\mathbf{A}\bar{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{z}\rVert_{p}\leq(1+\epsilon)\cdot\mathsf{OPT}
. We can verify that
\bar{\mathbf{x}}
as computed in Line
2
of Algorithm
6
does indeed satisfy this near optimality condition with probability
1-O(\delta)
by Theorem
3.4
. Overall, with probability at least
1-O(\delta)
, we have that
\bar{\mathbf{x}}
as computed in Line
2
of Algorithm
6
satisfies
\lVert\mathbf{A}\bar{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{z}\rVert_{p}\leq(1+\epsilon)\cdot\mathsf{OPT}
.

∎

Proof of Lemma
3.26
.

For simplicity, we assume via scaling that
\lVert\mathbf{z}\rVert_{p}\leq 1
and
\mathsf{OPT}=\Theta(1)
. By a standard argument (see, e.g., Lemma 2.5 of
[
BLM89
]
), it suffices to show that with high probability, for all
\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{\epsilon}
,

\displaystyle\left|\left\|\mathbf{S}_{1}\mathbf{y}^{\prime}-\mathbf{S}_{1}\bar{\mathbf{z}}\right\|_{p}^{p}-\lVert\mathbf{y}^{\prime}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\rVert_{p}^{p}\right|\leq\epsilon,

(9)

where
\mathcal{N}_{\epsilon}
is an
\epsilon
-net in the
p
-norm over
\{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}:\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{p}\leq 1\}
. By a standard volume argument (see, e.g., Lemma 2.4 of
[
BLM89
]
), it is known that one can construct this net such that
\log|\mathcal{N}_{\epsilon}|=O(d\log(1/\epsilon))
.

We prove (
9
) via a Bernstein inequality and union bound. We have
\E[\lVert\mathbf{S}_{1}\mathbf{y}^{\prime}-\mathbf{S}_{1}\bar{\mathbf{z}}\rVert_{p}^{p}]=\lVert\mathbf{y}^{\prime}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\rVert_{p}^{p}=O(1)
. Additionally, by definition,
|\bar{\mathbf{z}}(i)|^{p}\leq\frac{d^{\max(0,p/2-1)}\cdot\mathbf{w}_{i}^{p}(\mathbf{A})}{\epsilon^{p}}
for all
i
. Similarly, by Lemma
2.5
which bounds the
\ell_{p}
sensitivities by the Lewis weights,
|\mathbf{y}^{\prime}(i)|^{p}\leq d^{\max(0,p/2-1)}\cdot\mathbf{w}_{i}^{p}(\mathbf{A})
. Overall,

\displaystyle|\mathbf{y}^{\prime}(i)-\bar{\mathbf{z}}(i)|^{p}=O\left(\frac{d^{\max(0,p/2-1)}\cdot\mathbf{w}_{i}^{p}(\mathbf{A})}{\epsilon^{p}}\right)

By the construction of
\mathbf{S}_{1}
(Definition
3.24
), this gives

|[\mathbf{S}_{1}\mathbf{y}^{\prime}-\mathbf{S}_{1}\bar{\mathbf{z}}](i)|^{p}\leq\frac{d}{m\cdot\mathbf{w}_{i}^{p}(\mathbf{A})}\cdot\frac{d^{\max(0,p/2-1)}\cdot\mathbf{w}_{i}^{p}(\mathbf{A})}{\epsilon^{p}}=O\left(\frac{d^{\max(1,p/2)}}{m\cdot\epsilon^{p}}\right),

and so applying a Bernstein bound

\displaystyle\Pr\left[|\lVert\mathbf{S}_{1}\mathbf{y}^{\prime}-\mathbf{S}_{1}\bar{\mathbf{z}}\rVert_{p}^{p}-\lVert\mathbf{y}^{\prime}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\rVert_{p}^{p}|\leq\epsilon\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2\exp\left(-\Omega\left(\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{{d^{\max(0,p/2-1)}}/{m\cdot\epsilon^{p}}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=2\exp\left(-\Omega\left(\frac{\epsilon^{2+p}\cdot m}{d^{\max(0,p/2-1)}}\right)\right).

Setting

m=O\left(\frac{d^{\max(1,p/2)}}{\epsilon^{2+p}}\cdot\log\frac{|\mathcal{N}_{\epsilon}|}{\delta}\right)\leq O\left\lparen\frac{d^{\max(1,p/2)+1}}{\epsilon^{2+p}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon\delta}\right\rparen

and applying a union bound gives that this bound holds with high probability for all
\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{\epsilon}
. This completes the proof.
∎

3.6
Optimal Dependence on
\delta
with Additive Error

In this section, we show that with a knowledge of some overestimate
E
of
\mathsf{OPT}
, we can obtain an optimal dependence on
\delta
with an additive error of
\epsilon E
. Our approach is as follows. We first use Theorem
3.3
to compute a constant factor approximation
\mathbf{x}_{c}
. Now with the knowledge of
E
as well as
\mathbf{x}_{c}
, we can
explicitly
clip the large entries of
\mathbf{b}
as done in Lemma
3.5
, where
\mathbf{z}
can be replaced by
\mathbf{z}=\mathbf{b}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{c}
for a
\mathbf{x}_{c}
which can be found with high probability by Theorem
3.3
. In this case, we can explicitly approximate the cost of this clipped vector by sampling, which means we can collect
O(\log\frac{1}{\delta})
runs of a constant probability solution, and output the best solution among
O(\log\frac{1}{\delta})
many repetitions. This achieves an optimal dependence on
\delta
up to constant factors due to a lower bound of
[
PPP21
, Theorem 3.5]
, as this hard instance has an
\mathsf{OPT}
that is fixed up to a
\Theta(1)
factor, with high probability.

Note first that in Lemma
3.5
, we may replace
\mathsf{OPT}
by
E
,
\mathbf{z}=\mathbf{b}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}
by
\mathbf{z}=\mathbf{b}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{c}
, and
\bar{\mathbf{z}}
by the explicitly clipped
\bar{\mathbf{z}}
using
E
instead of
\mathsf{OPT}
, to obtain the following:

Lemma 3.27

(Lemma
3.5
– Explicit Version)
.

Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
and
\mathbf{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and let
0<p<\infty
. Let
\mathbf{z}=\mathbf{b}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{c}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
satisfy
\left\lVert\mathbf{z}\right\rVert_{p}=O(\mathsf{OPT})
. Let
E\geq\mathsf{OPT}
and let

\mathcal{B}\coloneqq\left\{i\in[n]:\frac{|\mathbf{z}(i)|^{p}}{E^{p}}\geq\frac{d^{\max(0,p/2-1)}\cdot\mathbf{w}_{i}^{p}(\mathbf{A})}{\epsilon^{p}}\right\}

Let
\bar{\mathbf{z}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be equal to
\mathbf{z}
but with all entries in
\mathcal{B}
set to
0
. Then for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{p}=O(E)
,

\left|\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{z}\rVert_{p}^{p}-\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\rVert_{p}^{p}-\left\lVert\mathbf{z}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}\right|=O(\epsilon)\cdot E^{p}.

With this modification, the following version of Lemma
3.8
holds with the same proof:

Lemma 3.28

(Lemma
3.8
– Explicit Version)
.

Consider the setting of Lemma
3.27
. Let
\mathbf{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
be formed as in Line
5
of Algorithm
4
. Let

\Lambda\coloneqq\sup_{\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{p}\leq O(E)}\left\lvert\left\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{S}\bar{\mathbf{z}}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}-\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}\right\rvert.

Then,

\Pr\left\{\Lambda\geq\left[C\frac{1}{n}\frac{d^{1\lor(p/2)}}{\epsilon^{0\lor(p-2)}}\right]^{1/2}\left[(\log d)\sqrt{\log n}+z\right]E^{p}\right\}\leq 2\exp(-z^{2})

We then obtain the following theorem, which has an improved dependence on
\delta
.

Theorem 3.29
.

Let
E\geq\mathsf{OPT}=\min_{\mathbf{x}}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{p}
be known. There is an algorithm which, with probability at least
1-\delta
, reads only
M
entries of
\mathbf{b}
and outputs
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}
satisfying

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\tilde{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{p}\leq\mathsf{OPT}+\epsilon E

where

m=\begin{cases}O(1)\frac{d}{\epsilon^{2}}(\log d)^{2}\log(d/\epsilon)\log\frac{1}{\delta}&p\in(0,2)\\
O(1)\frac{d^{p/2}}{\epsilon^{p}}(\log d)^{2}\log(d/\epsilon)\log\frac{1}{\delta}&p\in(2,\infty)\end{cases}

Proof.

We first prepare
\mathbf{z}
as required in Lemma
3.27
using Theorem
3.3
. This requires reading only
O(d(\log d)^{3}\log\frac{1}{\delta})
entries. Then by using our knowledge of
E
and
\mathbf{z}
, we may replace
\mathbf{b}
by the clipped residual
\bar{\mathbf{z}}
at a cost of only an additive
\epsilon\cdot E
error by Lemma
3.28
. Next, we sample
\ell=O(\log\frac{1}{\delta})
independent trials of
\mathbf{S}^{(i)}
for
i\in[\ell]
each with a constant probability success guarantee and solve
\min_{\mathbf{x}}\lVert\mathbf{S}^{(i)}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{S}^{(i)}\bar{\mathbf{z}}\rVert_{p}
to obtain
\ell
candidate solutions
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}^{(i)}
. Note that each
\mathbf{S}^{(i)}
only requires reading

O(1)\frac{d^{1\lor(p/2)}}{\epsilon^{2\lor p}}(\log d)^{2}\log(d/\epsilon)

entries of
\mathbf{b}
, so we only require
O(m)
entries altogether. Then by Chernoff bounds, with probability at least
1-\delta
, at least
9/10
of these solutions satisfy

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\tilde{\mathbf{x}}^{(i)}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{p}\leq\mathsf{OPT}+\epsilon\cdot E

and satisfy
\lVert\mathbf{A}\tilde{\mathbf{x}}^{(i)}\rVert_{p}\leq O(E)
, and we may discard any solution which fails to have
\lVert\mathbf{A}\tilde{\mathbf{x}}^{(i)}\rVert_{p}\leq O(E)
by using our knowledge of
E
. Finally, we sample a single high probability sketch
\mathbf{S}
which satisfies the guarantee of Lemma
3.28
with probability
1-\delta
. This single sketch requires only reading
O(m)
entries of
\mathbf{b}
. Then, up to an
\epsilon E
additive error we may approximately evaluate the cost of each of the
\ell
solutions
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}
such that
\lVert\mathbf{A}\tilde{\mathbf{x}}^{(i)}\rVert_{p}\leq O(E)
and select the best candidate using
\mathbf{S}
, by using
\left\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\tilde{\mathbf{x}}^{(i)}-\mathbf{S}\bar{\mathbf{z}}\right\rVert_{p}
as the proxy cost. We conclude by adjusting
\delta
and
\epsilon
by constant factors.
∎

4
Sensitivity Bounds for
M
-Estimators

In this section, we present our new sensitivity bounds. In Section
4.1
, we collect basic definitions and lemmas concerning
M
-estimators. Section
4.2
develops basic notions for sensitivity sampling for
M
-estimators. In Section
4.3
, we describe our efficient algorithm for computing sensitivities for a broad class of
M
-estimators. In Section
4.4
, we show that a variation on our efficient algorithm can be used to show an existential bound of
O(d^{\max\{1,p_{M}/2\}}\log n)
total sensitivity for the same class of
M
-estimators. Finally, in Section
4.5
, we show that the Tukey loss has a total sensitivity of
\Omega(d\log n)
, and that the Huber loss has a total sensitivity of
\Omega(d\log\log n)
.

4.1
Geometry of
M
-norms

In this section, we define
M
-norms and collect some of their geometric properties. This is a slight generalization of Section 4.1 of
[
CW15a
]
which allows for a broader class of
M
-norms (namely with a relaxed polynomial lower bound condition). With applications to active regression in mind, we also slightly generalize the results to handle translations by a single vector
\mathbf{b}
, which can be taken to be
0
to retrieve the original results.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
M:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
be increasing. If there exist constants
p>0
and
c_{U}\geq 1
such that for all
y>x
,

\frac{M(y)}{M(x)}\leq c_{U}\left\lparen\frac{y}{x}\right\rparen^{p},

then we say that
M
is
polynomially bounded above with degree
p
and constant
c_{U}
. Similarly, if there exists constants
q>0
and
c_{L}\geq 1
such that for all
y>x
,

\frac{M(y)}{M(x)}\geq c_{L}\left\lparen\frac{y}{x}\right\rparen^{q},

then we say that
M
is
polynomially bounded below with degree
q
and constant
c_{L}
.

Remark 4.2
.

As noted in
[
CW15a
]
, it can be shown that convex functions are polynomially bounded below with degree
1
.

Remark 4.3
.

Throughout this work, we will consider the constants
p,q,c_{U},c_{L}
in Definition
4.1
to be absolute constants that don’t depend on other parameters under consideration.

We define the
M
-norm as follows. Note that despite our abuse of notation and terminology, the
M
-norm need not be an actual norm.

Definition 4.4

(
M
-norm)
.

Let
M:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
be such that

•

M(0)=0

•

M
is nondecreasing

•

M
is polynomially bounded above with degree
p_{M}
and constant
c_{U}
(see Definition
4.1
)

Let
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a set of weights such that

\mathbf{w}_{i}\geq 1

for all
i\in[n]
. Then, we define the
M
-norm of a vector
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
as

\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}\coloneqq\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{w}_{i}M(\left\lvert\mathbf{x}_{i}\right\rvert)\right]^{1/p_{M}}.

If
\mathbf{w}
is the vector of all ones, we simple write
\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{M}
for
\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}
. If
M(x)=\left\lvert x\right\rvert^{p}
for some
p>0
, then we write
\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{p,\mathbf{w}}
for
\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}
.

Definition 4.5

(
M
balls and spheres)
.

Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
and let
\mathcal{V}=\Span(\mathbf{A})
. Let
M:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
satisfy the conditions of Definition
4.4
, and let
\mathbf{w}\geq\mathbf{1}_{n}
be a set of weights. Define the ball
\mathcal{B}^{M,\mathbf{w}}_{\rho}
of radius
\rho>0
as

\mathcal{B}^{M,\mathbf{w}}_{\rho}\coloneqq\left\{\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{V}:\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}\leq\rho\right\}.

Similarly define the sphere
\mathcal{S}^{M,\mathbf{w}}_{\rho}
of radius
\rho>0
as

\mathcal{S}^{M,\mathbf{w}}_{\rho}\coloneqq\left\{\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{V}:\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}=\rho\right\}.

If
\mathbf{w}=\mathbf{1}_{n}
, then we simply write
\mathcal{B}_{\rho}^{M}
and
\mathcal{S}_{\rho}^{M}
, respectively.

Additional useful properties that we will need are included in Appendix
C
, including sufficient conditions for triangle inequality and net constructions.

4.2
Sensitivities for
M
-Estimators

Because
M
-estimators are defined as coordinate-wise sums, one can naturally define analogues of sensitivities, just as was done for
\ell_{p}
norms.

Definition 4.6

(
M
-sensitivity)
.

Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
and let
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{M}
be an
M
-norm. Then, the
i
th
M
-sensitivity
is defined as

\mathbf{s}_{i}^{M}(\mathbf{A})\coloneqq\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\neq 0}\frac{M(\left\lvert[\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}](i)\right\rvert)}{\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{M}^{p_{M}}}

and the
total
M
-sensitivity
is defined as

\mathcal{T}^{M}(\mathbf{A})\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{s}_{i}^{M}(\mathbf{A}).

Let
\mathbf{w}\geq\mathbf{1}_{n}
be a set of weights. Then, the
i
th weighted
M
-sensitivity
is defined as

\mathbf{s}_{i}^{M,\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{A})\coloneqq\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\neq 0}\frac{\mathbf{w}_{i}M(\left\lvert[\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}](i)\right\rvert)}{\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}^{p_{M}}}

and the
total weighted
M
-sensitivity
is defined as

\mathcal{T}^{M,\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{A})\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{s}_{i}^{M,\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{A}).

When
M(x)=\left\lvert x\right\rvert^{p}
, i.e. for the case of
\ell_{p}
norms, it is known that sampling with probabilities proportional to upper bounds on sensitivities yields subspace embeddings
[
BLM89
,
DDH+09
,
CP15
]
. Analogous results are known as well for
M
-estimators
[
CW15b
,
CW15a
,
CWW19
]
and Orlicz norms
[
SWY+19
]
.

Definition 4.7

(Sensitivity Sampling for
M
-Estimators)
.

Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, let
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{M}
be an
M
-norm, and let
\mathbf{w}\geq\mathbf{1}_{n}
be a set of weights. Let
m
be an oversampling parameter. Then, a random set of weights
\mathbf{w}^{\prime}
is sampled according to sensitivity upper bounds
\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{M,\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{A})\geq\mathbf{s}_{i}^{M,\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{A})
(see Definition
4.6
) if

\mathbf{w}_{i}^{\prime}\coloneqq\begin{cases}\mathbf{w}_{i}/\mathbf{p}_{i}&\text{w.p. $\mathbf{p}_{i}$}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

where
\mathbf{p}_{i}\coloneqq\min\{1,m\cdot\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{M,\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{A})\}
.

Note that in the case of
M
-estimators, the lack of scale invariance means that we get norm preservation guarantees for spheres rather than for entire subspaces. That is, we can get the following lemma, similar Lemma 43 of
[
CW15a
]
:

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
. Let
\epsilon\in(0,1)
,
\delta>0
, and let
\rho\geq 1
. Let
M:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
satisfy the conditions of Definition
4.4
, and furthermore that

•

M^{1/p_{M}}
is subadditive

•

M
is polynomially bounded below with degree
q_{M}
and constant
c_{L}
(see Definition
4.1
)

Let
\mathbf{w}\geq\mathbf{1}_{n}
be a set of weights. Let
\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{M,\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{A})\geq\mathbf{s}_{i}^{M,\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{A})
be sensitivity upper bounds. Let
m\geq m_{0}
be an oversampling parameter larger than some

m_{0}=O\left\lparen\frac{d}{\epsilon^{2}}\left\lparen\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}\right\rparen\left\lparen\log\frac{1}{\delta}\right\rparen\right\rparen.

Let
\mathbf{w}^{\prime}\geq\mathbf{1}_{n}
be sampled according to Definition
4.7
. Then with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}^{p_{M}}=(1\pm\epsilon)\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}^{p_{M}}

for all
\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{S}_{\rho}^{M}
. Furthermore,

\E\nnz(\mathbf{w}^{\prime})\leq m\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{M,\mathbf{w}}=m\tilde{\mathcal{T}}^{M,\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{A})

Proof.

The proof is by a standard Bernstein bound, and is included in Appendix
C
.
∎

4.3
Efficient Sensitivity Bounds

We first show that algorithmically, one can compute upper bounds to the
M
-estimator sensitivities that sum to at most
O(d^{\max\{1,p_{M}/2\}}\log^{2}n+\tau)
in time

O\left\lparen\nnz(\mathbf{A})\log^{3}n+\frac{nT}{\tau}\log n\right\rparen,

where
T=T(n,d)
is such that constant factor
\ell_{p_{M}}
Lewis weight approximation for an
n\times d
matrix
\mathbf{B}
takes
O(\nnz(\mathbf{B})\log n+T)
time. For example, it is known that
\ell_{p}
Lewis weights for
0<p<4
can be approximated up to constant factors in
O(\nnz(\mathbf{A})\log n+d^{\omega})
time, so for
\tau=T=d^{\omega}
, we obtain a nearly input sparsity time algorithm that computes upper bounds to
M
-estimator sensitivities that sum to at most
O(d^{\max\{1,p_{M}/2\}}\log^{2}n+d^{\omega})
. Note that in applications (see applications to active regression (Section
5
), Orlicz norm regression (Section
10
), and robust subspace approximation (Section
11
) in this work), this is enough to compute a set of
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(d)\log^{2}n
rows that approximates the original matrix well, at which point we can compute sensitivities that sum to only
O(d\log^{2}n)
in an additional
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(d\log n)
time.

The algorithm draws ideas from a theorem of
[
CWW19
, Theorem 3.4]
, which shows an input sparsity time algorithm for locating “heavy entries” for the Tukey loss, which is equivalent to finding coordinates with high Tukey sensitivity.

Algorithm 7

Sensitivity upper bounds

input:
Matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
,
M
-norm
M
, parameter
1\leq\tau\leq n
.

output:
Upper bounds
\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{M}(\mathbf{A})
on
\mathbf{s}_{i}^{M}(\mathbf{A})
.

1:

Initialize
\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{M}(\mathbf{A})\leftarrow 2\tau/n
.

2:

for

r\in[\lceil\log_{2}(n/\tau)\rceil]

do

3:

for

t\in[O(\log n)]

do

4:

Hash the rows of
\mathbf{A}
into
B=10\cdot 2^{r}
buckets
S_{1},S_{2},\dots,S_{B}
.

5:

Compute
O(1)
-approximate
\ell_{p}
Lewis weights of each of the
B
buckets
\mathbf{A}\mid_{S_{1}},\mathbf{A}\mid_{S_{2}},\dots,\mathbf{A}\mid_{S_{B}}
.

6:

For any row
i
with an
\ell_{p_{M}}
Lewis weight of at least
\Omega(1)
, set
\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{M}(\mathbf{A})\leftarrow\max\{2/2^{r},\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{M}(\mathbf{A})\}
.

7:

end

for

8:

end

for

Theorem 4.9

(Main Result for Sensitivity Bounds)
.

Let
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{M}
be an
M
-norm. Let
1\leq\tau\leq n
be a parameter. Then, with probability at least
99/100
, Algorithm
7
computes sensitivity upper bounds
\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{M}(\mathbf{A})\geq\mathbf{s}_{i}^{M}(\mathbf{A})
that sum to at most

\tilde{\mathcal{T}}^{M}(\mathbf{A})\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{M}(\mathbf{A})=O(d^{\max\{1,p_{M}/2\}}\log^{2}n+\tau).

If constant factor Lewis weight approximation takes for an
n\times d
matrix
\mathbf{B}
takes
O(\nnz(\mathbf{B})\log n+T)
time (see Theorem
2.7
), then the total running time is

O\left\lparen\nnz(\mathbf{A})\log^{3}n+\frac{nT}{\tau}\log n\right\rparen.

Proof.

We first show correctness of the algorithm, then show the sensitivity bound, and finally the running time guarantee.

Correctness.

Let
r\in[\lceil\log_{2}(n/\tau)\rceil]
. Consider a coordinate
i\in[n]
that has
M
-sensitivity between
1/2^{r}
and
2/2^{r}
and let
\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}
be a corresponding vector which satisfies

\frac{M(|\mathbf{y}_{i}|)}{\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{M}^{p_{M}}}\in\left[\frac{1}{2^{r}},\frac{2}{2^{r}}\right].

Note then that there are at most
2^{r}-1
entries
j
of
\mathbf{y}
such that
\left\lvert\mathbf{y}_{j}\right\rvert>\left\lvert\mathbf{y}_{i}\right\rvert
. In our algorithm (line
4
), we randomly hash the
n
rows of
\mathbf{A}
into
B=10\cdot 2^{r}
buckets. Then, the probability that any one of these
2^{r}-1
entries is hashed to the same bucket as
i
is
1/B
, so by a union bound, the probability that
\mathbf{y}_{i}
has the largest absolute value in its hash bucket is at least
1-2^{r}/B\geq 9/10
. Call this event
\mathcal{E}
.

Now let
S
be the set of indices which hash to the same bucket as row
i
and let
S^{\prime}=S\setminus\{i\}
. By Markov’s inequality, with probability at least
9/10
,
i
is hashed to a bucket such that the
M
-norm of all other entries is most
\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\mid_{S^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{M}^{p_{M}}\leq\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{M}^{p_{M}}/2^{r}
, where
\mathbf{y}\mid_{S^{\prime}}
is the restriction of
\mathbf{y}
to the indices in
S^{\prime}
. Call this event
\mathcal{F}
.

Condition on
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{F}
. We have by Lemma
C.3
that
