\frac{|\mathbf{y}_{i}|^{p_{M}}}{\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\mid_{S}\right\rVert_{p_{M}}^{p_{M}}}\geq\frac{M(|\mathbf{y}_{i}|^{p_{M}})}{\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\mid_{S}\right\rVert_{M}^{p_{M}}}\geq\frac{M(|\mathbf{y}_{i}|^{p_{M}})}{M(|\mathbf{y}_{i}|^{p_{M}})+\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\mid_{S^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{M}^{p_{M}}}\geq\frac{1/2^{r}}{2/2^{r}+1/2^{r}}=\frac{1}{3}.

The above holds with probability at least
4/5
. Thus, by repeating the hashing process
O(\log n)
times, with probability at least
1-1/(100n\log_{2}n)=1-1/\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(n)
, there exists some trial where the
\ell_{p_{M}}
sensitivity of the
i
th row
in the matrix
\mathbf{A}\mid_{S}
is at least
1/3
. In this trial, our algorithm will correctly set
\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{M}(\mathbf{A})\geq 2/2^{r}
(line
6
). By a union bound over
O(\log(n/\tau))
levels
r
and the
n
rows, our algorithm succeeds with probability at least
99/100
.

Sensitivity Bound.

By Lemma
B.4
, the
\ell_{p_{M}}
sensitivities sum to at most
d^{\max\{1,{p_{M}}/2\}}
. Thus, each time we compute
O(1)
-approximate
\ell_{p_{M}}
Lewis weights (line
5
), we find at most
O(d^{\max\{1,{p_{M}}/2\}})
entries with
M
-sensitivity at least
2/2^{r}
. Thus, for each
r
and each iteration, we increase the sum of our upper bounds on
M
-sensitivities by a total of at most

B\cdot O(d^{\max\{1,{p_{M}}/2\}})\cdot\frac{2}{2^{r}}=O(d^{\max\{1,{p_{M}}/2\}}).

This occurs at most
O((\log n)(\log(n/\tau)))
times, and we start at a sensitivity bound of

\frac{2\tau}{n}\cdot n=O(\tau)

so our upper bounds on the sensitivities sum to at most

O(d^{\max\{1,{p_{M}}/2\}}(\log n)(\log(n/\tau))+\tau)\leq O(d^{\max\{1,{p_{M}}/2\}}\log^{2}n+\tau).

Running Time.

For a given
r
, the dominating running time cost of the inner-most loop of Algorithm
7
is the computation of
\ell_{p_{M}}
Lewis weights for
O(2^{r})
matrices whose sparsities sum to
\nnz(\mathbf{A})
. Thus, if Lewis weight computation for an
n\times d
matrix
\mathbf{B}
takes
O(\nnz(\mathbf{B})\log n+T)
time, then the total running time is

\sum_{r=1}^{\lceil\log_{2}(n/\tau)\rceil}O(\log n)\cdot O\left\lparen\nnz(\mathbf{A})\log n+2^{r}T\right\rparen=O\left\lparen\nnz(\mathbf{A})\log^{3}n+\frac{nT}{\tau}\log n\right\rparen.\qed

Remark 4.10
.

As noted by
[
TMF20
]
, if we can control the sensitivities of functions
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
, then it is straightforward to control the sensitivities of the sum of these two functions, i.e.,
M=M_{1}+M_{2}
. This applies to our algorithm as well. Suppose that
\mathbf{s}_{i}^{M}(\mathbf{A})\in[1/2^{r},2/2^{r}]
and let
\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}
be such that

\frac{M_{1}(\left\lvert\mathbf{y}_{i}\right\rvert)+M_{2}(\left\lvert\mathbf{y}_{i}\right\rvert)}{\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{M_{1}}^{p_{M_{1}}}+\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{M_{2}}^{p_{M_{2}}}}\in\left[\frac{1}{2^{r}},\frac{2}{2^{r}}\right].

Then,

\frac{M_{1}(\left\lvert\mathbf{y}_{i}\right\rvert)}{\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{M_{1}}^{p_{M_{1}}}}+\frac{M_{2}(\left\lvert\mathbf{y}_{i}\right\rvert)}{\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{M_{2}}^{p_{M_{2}}}}\geq\frac{1}{2^{r}}

so then there is some
j\in\{1,2\}
such that

\frac{M_{j}(\left\lvert\mathbf{y}_{i}\right\rvert)}{\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{M_{j}}^{p_{M_{j}}}}\geq\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{2^{r}},

so the
\ell_{p_{j}}
Lewis weight of the
i
th coordinate must be large by using a similar proof as Theorem
4.9
. Thus, we can obtain similar sensitivity upper bounds up to a constant factor loss. Similarly, if
M_{2}
is a “flat” sensitivity function in the sense of
[
TMF20
]
, that is, if

\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{M_{2}(\left\lvert[\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}](i)\right\rvert)}{\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{M_{2}}^{p_{M_{2}}}}=O\left\lparen\frac{1}{n}\right\rparen

for all
i\in[n]
, then this just means that either the
M_{1}
sensitivity is large, or the
M
sensitivity is at most
O(1/n)
, in which case we still get the same bounds.

Although Theorem
4.9
only handles unweighted
M
-estimators, this result can be generalized to weighted
M
-estimators by splitting into level sets, similarly to Lemma 39 of
[
CW15a
]
.

Lemma 4.11
.

Let
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{M}
be an
M
-norm. Let
\mathbf{w}\geq\mathbf{1}_{n}
be a set of weights. Let
N\coloneqq\left\lceil\log_{2}(1+\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{\infty})\right\rceil
. For
j\in[N]
, let

T_{j}\coloneqq\left\{i\in[n]:2^{j-1}\leq\mathbf{w}_{i}<2^{j}\right\},

and let
\mathbf{A}\mid_{T_{j}}
denote the restriction of
\mathbf{A}
to the rows of
T_{j}
. Then,

\mathbf{s}_{i}^{M,\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{A})\leq 2\cdot\mathbf{s}_{i}^{M}(\mathbf{A}\mid_{T_{j}})

for
i\in T_{j}
.

Proof.

Let
i\in T_{j}
for some
j\in[N]
. We have that

\displaystyle\mathbf{s}_{i}^{M,\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{A})

\displaystyle=\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\neq 0}\frac{\mathbf{w}_{i}M([\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}](i))}{\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}^{p_{M}}}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\neq 0}\frac{\mathbf{w}_{i}M([\mathbf{A}\mid_{T_{j}}\mathbf{x}](i))}{\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mid_{T_{j}}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}^{p_{M}}}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\neq 0}\frac{2^{j}M([\mathbf{A}\mid_{T_{j}}\mathbf{x}](i))}{2^{j-1}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mid_{T_{j}}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{M}^{p_{M}}}

\displaystyle=2\cdot\mathbf{s}_{i}^{M}(\mathbf{A}\mid_{T_{j}})

as desired.
∎

This leads to an algorithm that achieves guarantees similar to Theorem
4.9
for weighted
M
-sensitivities, up to a loss of a factor of
N
in the running time and sensitivity bound.

Corollary 4.12
.

Let
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{M}
be an
M
-norm. Let
\mathbf{w}\geq\mathbf{1}_{n}
be a set of weights. Define
N
as in Lemma
4.11
. There is an algorithm that computes weighted
M
-estimator sensitivities that sum to at most

O(Nd^{\max\{1,p_{M}/2\}}\log^{2}n+N\tau)

in time

O\left\lparen\nnz(\mathbf{A})\log^{3}n+N\frac{nT}{\tau}\log n\right\rparen,

where
T
is such that constant factor Lewis weight approximation for an
n\times d
matrix
\mathbf{B}
takes
O(\nnz(\mathbf{B})+T)
time (see Theorem
2.7
).

Proof.

This is simply the result of applying Theorem
4.9
on the
N
matrices
\mathbf{A}\mid_{T_{j}}
as defined in Lemma
4.11
. Note that the
\nnz(\mathbf{A}\mid_{T_{j}})
terms add up to
\nnz(\mathbf{A})
in the running time.
∎

4.4
Sharper Sensitivity Bounds

We show that we may modify the proof of our input sparsity time algorithm to show that the sum of sensitivities is at most
O(d^{\max\{1,p_{M}/2\}}\log n)
, if we do not need to efficient algorithms for constructing these sensitivities.

Theorem 4.13
.

Let
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{M}
be an
M
-norm. Then, the total
M
-sensitivity of
\mathbf{A}
is at most

O(d^{\max\{1,p_{M}/2\}}\log n).

Proof.

Our idea is essentially to run Algorithm
7
with
\tau=d
without the
O(\log n)
repetitions of the hashing process.

Let
r\in[\lceil\log_{2}n\rceil]
and let
I_{r}
be the set of coordinates with
M
-sensitivity in
[1/2^{r},2/2^{r}]
. Suppose we hash the rows of
\mathbf{A}
into
B=10\cdot 2^{r}
buckets. Then, as in the proof of Theorem
4.9
, for each
i\in I_{r}
, there is at least a
9/10
probability that
i
has
\ell_{p_{M}}
Lewis weight at least
1/3
in its hash bucket. Thus, the number of such
i
is
(9/10)\left\lvert I_{r}\right\rvert
in expectation, so there exists some hashing such that at least
(9/10)\left\lvert I_{r}\right\rvert
of the indices
i\in I_{r}
have
\ell_{p}
Lewis weight at least
1/3
in its hash bucket. However, there can be at most
B\cdot d^{\max\{1,p/2\}}
such indices, so we must have that

\frac{9}{10}\left\lvert I_{r}\right\rvert\leq B\cdot d^{\max\{1,p/2\}}

so

\left\lvert I_{r}\right\rvert=O(B\cdot d^{\max\{1,p/2\}})=O(2^{r}\cdot d^{\max\{1,p/2\}}).

By summing over the
r
, we obtain a bound of

\sum_{r=1}^{\lceil\log_{2}n\rceil}\frac{2}{2^{r}}\left\lvert I_{r}\right\rvert\leq\sum_{r=1}^{\lceil\log_{2}n\rceil}\frac{2}{2^{r}}O(2^{r}\cdot d^{\max\{1,p/2\}})=O(d^{\max\{1,p/2\}}\log n)=O(d^{\max\{1,p/2\}}\log n)

on the total
M
-sensitivity, as claimed.
∎

4.5
Sensitivity Lower Bounds

Finally, we show that our sensitivity upper bounds are tight by showing that the Tukey loss can have a total sensitivity as large as
\Omega(d\log(n/d))
. We also show a weaker lower bound of
\Omega(d\log\log(n/d))
for the Huber loss. This is in contrast to sensitivities for the
\ell_{p}
loss for
0<p<\infty
, which is always at most
d^{\max\{1,p/2\}}
due to the existence of Lewis bases
[
Lew78
,
SZ01
]
, and thus has no dependence on
n
. The necessity for a dependence on
n
can be attributed to the lack of scale invariance for these
M
-estimator losses. A similar observation has been made previously in
[
SWZ19
, Theorem 1.3]
, which shows that the column subset selection problem with the entrywise Huber loss exhibits a lower bound of
\Omega(\sqrt{\log n})
columns, also attributed to the lack of scale invariance.

We simultaneously handle the Tukey and Huber losses by analyzing the
\ell_{2}
-
\ell_{p}
loss for
p\in[0,1]
, which grows quadratically near the origin and as
\ell_{p}
away from the origin, and is polynomially bounded above with degree
2
.

Lemma 4.14

(Sensitivity Lower Bound for the
\ell_{2}
-
\ell_{p}
Loss)
.

Define the
\ell_{2}
-
\ell_{p}
loss of width
\tau
to be

M(x)=\begin{cases}x^{2}&\left\lvert x\right\rvert\leq\tau\\
(\tau^{2}/\tau^{p})\cdot x^{p}&\left\lvert x\right\rvert>\tau\end{cases}.

For
d\geq 1
and
n\geq d
, there exists an
n\times d
matrix
\mathbf{A}
with total
M
-sensitivity that is at least

\mathcal{T}^{T}(\mathbf{A})\geq\begin{cases}\Omega\left\lparen d\log\frac{n}{d}\right\rparen&\text{if $p\in[0,1)$}\\
\Omega\left\lparen d\log\log\frac{n}{d}\right\rparen&\text{if $p=1$}.\end{cases}

Proof.

Let
\ell=\left\lfloor\log_{2}n\right\rfloor
and let
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a vector with
2^{i}
coordinates of value
\tau/2^{i}
for
i\in[\ell]
. We will show a sensitivity lower bound of
\Omega(\ell)=\Omega(\log n)
for the
n\times 1
matrix formed by the vector
\mathbf{x}
. By considering
d
disjoint copies of this vector, each on
n/d
coordinates, this implies a lower bound of
\Omega(d\log(n/d))
.

Let
j\in[\ell]
. Then,

\displaystyle\left\lVert 2^{j}\cdot\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{M}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}2^{i}\cdot M\left\lparen\tau\frac{2^{j}}{2^{i}}\right\rparen

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\tau^{2}}{\tau^{p}}\sum_{i=1}^{j}2^{i}\cdot\left\lparen\tau\frac{2^{j}}{2^{i}}\right\rparen^{p}+\sum_{i=j+1}^{\ell}2^{i}\cdot\left\lparen\tau\frac{2^{j}}{2^{i}}\right\rparen^{2}

\displaystyle=\tau^{2}2^{pj}\sum_{i=1}^{j}2^{(1-p)i}+\tau^{2}2^{2j}\sum_{i=j+1}^{\ell}\frac{1}{2^{i}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}O(\tau^{2}\cdot 2^{j})&\text{if $p\in[0,1)$}\\
O(\tau^{2}\cdot j2^{j})&\text{if $p=1$}\\
\end{cases}

so for each
j\in[\ell]
, there are
2^{j}
coordinates
i
such that

\displaystyle\frac{M(2^{j}\cdot\mathbf{x}_{i})}{\left\lVert 2^{j}\cdot\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{M}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\Omega\left\lparen\frac{\tau^{2}}{\tau^{2}\cdot 2^{j}}\right\rparen&\text{if $p\in[0,1)$}\\
O\left\lparen\frac{\tau^{2}}{\tau^{2}\cdot j2^{j}}\right\rparen&\text{if $p=1$}\\
\end{cases}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\Omega\left\lparen\frac{1}{2^{j}}\right\rparen&\text{if $p\in[0,1)$}\\
O\left\lparen\frac{1}{j2^{j}}\right\rparen&\text{if $p=1$}\\
\end{cases}.

Thus, the sum of sensitivities for the Tukey loss for this matrix is at least

\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}2^{j}\cdot\Omega\left\lparen\frac{1}{2^{j}}\right\rparen=\Omega\left\lparen\ell\right\rparen=\Omega\left\lparen\log n\right\rparen

for
p\in[0,1)
and

\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}2^{j}\cdot\Omega\left\lparen\frac{1}{j2^{j}}\right\rparen=\Omega\left\lparen\log\ell\right\rparen=\Omega\left\lparen\log\log n\right\rparen

for
p=1
.
∎

5
Applications: Active Regression for
M
-Estimators

We now show that our algorithmic ideas for
\ell_{p}
active regression may be applied to handle active regression for a general class of
M
-estimators, using our sensitivity results in Section
4
.

Throughout this section, we assume the following. Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
and let
\mathcal{V}=\Span(\mathbf{A})
. Let
M:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
satisfy the conditions of Definition
4.4
, and furthermore that

•

M^{1/p_{M}}
is subadditive

•

M
is polynomially bounded below with degree
q_{M}
and constant
c_{L}
(see Definition
4.1
)

In the linear regression problem with the
M
-loss, we are given a matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
and a vector
\mathbf{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
we must solve the following optimization problem:

\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{M}.

In the active setting, we wish to solve the above problem while querying as few entries of
\mathbf{b}
as possible. We will also consider the weighted version, i.e.

\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}

(10)

for a set of weights
\mathbf{w}\geq\mathbf{1}_{n}
. Throughout this section, let for a given weighted
M
-norm
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}
, let

\mathsf{OPT}\coloneqq\min_{\mathbf{x}}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}.

5.1
Constant Factor Approximation

We adapt Lemmas 7 and 8 of
[
DDH+09
]
to first obtain a constant factor solution
\mathbf{x}_{c}
to the regression problem.

Lemma 5.1

(Constant Factor Approximation)
.

Let
\mathbf{w}\geq\mathbf{1}_{n}
be a set of weights. Let
\mathbf{w}^{\prime}\geq\mathbf{1}_{n}
be a random set of weights that satisfies the following:

•

\E_{\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}=\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}
for any fixed
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}

•

\mathbf{w}^{\prime}
is a
1/8
-subspace embedding for any
\mathcal{S}_{\rho}^{M}
for any fixed
\rho>0
with probability at least
9/10
, that is,

\Pr\left\{\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}=\left\lparen 1\pm\frac{1}{8}\right\rparen\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}},\forall\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{S}_{\rho}^{M}\right\}\geq\frac{9}{10}

Let
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}
satisfy

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\tilde{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}\leq\kappa\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}.

Then, with constant probability,

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\tilde{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}\leq O(\kappa)\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}.

Proof.

Let

\mathsf{OPT}\coloneqq\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}

and let
\mathbf{x}^{*}
be the minimizer achieving this value. Then by assumption, we have that

\E_{\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}=\mathsf{OPT}

so by Markov’s inequality, with probability at least
9/10
,
\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}\leq 10\cdot\mathsf{OPT}
. Condition on this event. Note then that

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\tilde{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}\leq\kappa\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}\leq 10\kappa\cdot\mathsf{OPT}.

(11)

Now suppose for contradiction that

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\tilde{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}>\frac{25\kappa}{c_{L}^{1/p_{M}}}\cdot\mathsf{OPT}.

Then,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\tilde{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}

\displaystyle\geq\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\tilde{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}-\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}

triangle inequality

\displaystyle>\frac{25\kappa}{c_{L}^{1/p_{M}}}\cdot\mathsf{OPT}-\mathsf{OPT}=\frac{24\kappa}{c_{L}^{1/p_{M}}}\cdot\mathsf{OPT}.

We now apply the subspace embedding property, with
\rho=(24\kappa/c_{L}^{1/p_{M}})\cdot\mathsf{OPT}
so that

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}\geq\left\lparen 1\pm\frac{1}{8}\right\rparen\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}

for all
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}
with
\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}=(24\kappa/c_{L}^{1/p_{M}})\cdot\mathsf{OPT}
. We then apply Lemma
C.12
to see that

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}\geq c_{L}^{1/p_{M}}\frac{7}{8}\cdot\frac{24\kappa}{c_{L}^{1/p_{M}}}\cdot\mathsf{OPT}=21\kappa\cdot\mathsf{OPT}

for all
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}
with
\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}\geq(24\kappa/c_{L}^{1/p_{M}})\cdot\mathsf{OPT}
. Thus,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\tilde{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\geq\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\tilde{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}-\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}

triangle inequality

\displaystyle>21\kappa\cdot\mathsf{OPT}-10\kappa\cdot\mathsf{OPT}=11\kappa\cdot\mathsf{OPT}.

This contradicts Equation (
11
), so we conclude that

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\tilde{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}\leq\frac{25\kappa}{c_{L}^{1/p_{M}}}\cdot\mathsf{OPT}=O(\kappa)\mathsf{OPT}.\qed

5.2
Relative Error Approximation

After an initial constant factor approximation, we show that sensitivity sampling on the residual yields a relative error approximation.

We first reduce to considering only
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}
with
\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}=O(\mathsf{OPT})
.

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
and let
\mathbf{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Let
\mathbf{w}\geq\mathbf{1}_{n}
and let
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{M}
be an
M
-norm that satisfies an approximate triangle inequality, that is,

M^{1/p_{M}}(a+b)\leq O(1)(M^{1/p_{M}}(a)+M^{1/p_{M}}(b)).

Suppose that
\mathbf{x}_{c}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
satisfies

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{c}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}\leq O(1)\min_{\mathbf{x}}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}=O(\mathsf{OPT})

and let
\mathbf{b}^{\prime}=\mathbf{b}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{c}
. Let
\bar{\mathbf{x}}
satisfy

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\bar{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{b}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}\leq O(1)\min_{\mathbf{x}}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}=O(\mathsf{OPT}).

Then,
\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\bar{\mathbf{x}}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}=O(\mathsf{OPT})
.

Proof.

We have by approximate triangle inequality that

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\bar{\mathbf{x}}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}\leq O(1)(\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\bar{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{b}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}+\left\lVert\mathbf{b}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}})\leq O(1)(O(\mathsf{OPT})+O(\mathsf{OPT}))=O(\mathsf{OPT}).

∎

Next, we show that we only need to preserve the cost on a certain subset of coordinates where the entries of
\mathbf{b}^{\prime}
are relatively small.

Lemma 5.3
.

Consider the setting of Lemma
5.2
. Further suppose that either

(1)

for any
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
with
M(a)\leq\epsilon^{p_{M}}M(b)
,

M(a+b)=(1\pm O(\epsilon))M(b)

(2)

M^{1/p_{M}}
is subadditive

Let
\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{M,\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{A})\geq\mathbf{s}_{i}^{M,\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{A})
be upper bounds on the weighted
M
-sensitivities of
\mathbf{A}
. Let

\mathcal{B}\coloneqq\left\{i\in[n]:\mathbf{w}_{i}M(\mathbf{b}_{i}^{\prime})>\frac{\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{M,\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{A})\mathsf{OPT}^{p_{M}}}{\epsilon^{p_{M}}}\right\}

Let
\mathbf{w}^{\prime}
be a set of random weights such that for any fixed
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
\E_{\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}^{p_{M}}=\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}^{p_{M}}
, and such that with probability at least
1-\delta
satisfies

\left\lVert(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}^{\prime})\mid_{\overline{\mathcal{B}}}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}^{p_{M}}=\left\lVert(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}^{\prime})\mid_{\overline{\mathcal{B}}}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}^{p_{M}}\pm O(\epsilon)\mathsf{OPT}^{p_{M}}

(12)

for every
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}
with
\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}=O(\mathsf{OPT})
. Then with probability at least
1-\delta
, there is a
C
with
\left\lvert C\right\rvert\leq O(\mathsf{OPT}^{p_{M}}/\delta)
such that

\displaystyle\left\lvert\left\lVert\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{b}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}^{p_{M}}-\left\lVert\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{b}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}^{p_{M}}-C\right\rvert=O(\epsilon/\delta)\mathsf{OPT}^{p_{M}}.

Proof.

First note that assumption (2) implies assumption (1), since that implies that

M^{1/p_{M}}(a+b)=M^{1/p_{M}}(b)\pm M^{1/p_{M}}(a)=M^{1/p_{M}}(b)\pm\epsilon M^{1/p_{M}}(b)

and raising both sides to the
p_{M}
th power gives assumption (1).

Now note that for any
i\in\mathcal{B}
and
\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}
with
\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}=O(\mathsf{OPT})
, we have that

\mathbf{w}_{i}M([\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}](i))\leq\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{M,\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{A})\cdot\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}^{p_{M}}=O(1)\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{M,\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{A})\cdot\mathsf{OPT}^{p_{M}}\leq O(\epsilon^{p_{M}})\mathbf{w}_{i}M(\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(i)).

It follows by assumption (1) that

M(\left\lvert\mathbf{y}_{i}-\mathbf{b}^{\prime}_{i}\right\rvert)=(1\pm O(\epsilon))M(\left\lvert\mathbf{b}^{\prime}_{i}\right\rvert)

Then, we have that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{b}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}^{p_{M}}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{b}^{\prime})\mid_{\overline{\mathcal{B}}}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}^{p_{M}}+\left\lVert(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{b}^{\prime})\mid_{\mathcal{B}}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}^{p_{M}}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{b}^{\prime})\mid_{\overline{\mathcal{B}}}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}^{p_{M}}+\left\lVert\mathbf{b}^{\prime}\mid_{\mathcal{B}}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}^{p_{M}}\pm O(\epsilon)\left\lVert\mathbf{b}^{\prime}\mid_{\mathcal{B}}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}^{p_{M}}

and similarly,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{b}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}^{p_{M}}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{b}^{\prime})\mid_{\overline{\mathcal{B}}}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}^{p_{M}}+\left\lVert(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{b}^{\prime})\mid_{\mathcal{B}}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}^{p_{M}}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{b}^{\prime})\mid_{\overline{\mathcal{B}}}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}^{p_{M}}+\left\lVert\mathbf{b}^{\prime}\mid_{\mathcal{B}}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}^{p_{M}}\pm O(\epsilon)\left\lVert\mathbf{b}^{\prime}\mid_{\mathcal{B}}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}^{p_{M}}

Now using that
\left\lVert\mathbf{b}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}^{p_{M}}=O(1/\delta)\left\lVert\mathbf{b}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}^{p_{M}}
with probability at least
1-\delta
by Markov’s inequality, we set
C=\left\lVert\mathbf{b}^{\prime}\mid_{\mathcal{B}}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}^{p_{M}}-\left\lVert\mathbf{b}^{\prime}\mid_{\mathcal{B}}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}^{p_{M}}
to see that

\displaystyle\left\lvert\left\lVert\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{b}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}^{p_{M}}-\left\lVert\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{b}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}^{p_{M}}-C\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\left\lVert(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{b}^{\prime})\mid_{\overline{\mathcal{B}}}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}^{p_{M}}-\left\lVert(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{b}^{\prime})\mid_{\overline{\mathcal{B}}}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}^{p_{M}}\right\rvert\pm O(\epsilon/\delta)\left\lVert\mathbf{b}^{\prime}\mid_{\mathcal{B}}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}^{p_{M}}

\displaystyle\leq O(\epsilon/\delta)\left\lVert\mathbf{b}^{\prime}\mid_{\mathcal{B}}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}^{p_{M}}=O(\epsilon/\delta)\mathsf{OPT}^{p_{M}}

as desired.
∎

We next show how to guarantee Equation (
12
) using Bernstein’s inequality. The proof closely follows that of Lemma
4.8
, but needs a slight modification to handle the coordinates of
\mathbf{b}^{\prime}
in
\overline{\mathcal{B}}
.

Lemma 5.4
.

Consider the setting of Lemma
5.3
. Let
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}
have
\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}=O(\mathsf{OPT})
and let
\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}^{\prime}
. Let
m\geq 1
be a parameter and let
\mathbf{w}^{\prime}
be obtained from weights
\mathbf{w}
according to Definition
4.7
. Then,

\Pr\left\{\left\lvert\left\lVert(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}^{\prime})\mid_{\overline{\mathcal{B}}}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}^{p_{M}}-\left\lVert(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}^{\prime})\mid_{\overline{\mathcal{B}}}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}^{p_{M}}\right\rvert\geq\epsilon\mathsf{OPT}^{p_{M}}\right\}\leq\exp\left\lparen-\Theta(1)m\epsilon^{2+p_{M}}\right\rparen

Proof.

Let
G\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{B}}
be the subset of coordinates of
\overline{\mathcal{B}}
such that
\mathbf{p}_{i}<1
. Define the random variable

W_{i}\coloneqq\mathbf{w}_{i}^{\prime}M(\mathbf{y}(i))

for each
i\in G
. Then,

\E\left[\sum_{i\in G}W_{i}\right]=\sum_{i\in G}\frac{\mathbf{w}_{i}}{\mathbf{p}_{i}}M(\mathbf{y}(i))\cdot\mathbf{p}_{i}=\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\mid_{G}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}^{p_{M}}.

Note that

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbf{w}_{i}}{\mathbf{p}_{i}}M(\mathbf{y}(i))

\displaystyle\leq O(1)\frac{1}{m\cdot\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{M,\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{A})}(\mathbf{w}_{i}M([\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}](i))+\mathbf{w}_{i}M(\mathbf{b}^{\prime}(i)))

\displaystyle\leq O(1)\frac{1}{m\cdot\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{M,\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{A})}\left\lparen\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{M,\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{A})\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}^{p_{M}}+\frac{\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{M,\mathbf{w}}\mathsf{OPT}^{p_{M}}}{\epsilon^{p_{M}}}\right\rparen

\displaystyle\leq O(1)\frac{1}{m\cdot\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{M,\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{A})}\left\lparen\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{M,\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{A})\mathsf{OPT}^{p_{M}}+\frac{\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{M,\mathbf{w}}\mathsf{OPT}^{p_{M}}}{\epsilon^{p_{M}}}\right\rparen

\displaystyle\leq O(1)\frac{\mathsf{OPT}^{p_{M}}}{m\cdot\epsilon^{p_{M}}}

We next bound the variance:

\displaystyle\operatorname{Var}\left[\sum_{i\in G}W_{i}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in G}\operatorname{Var}[W_{i}]\leq\sum_{i\in G}\frac{\mathbf{w}_{i}^{2}}{\mathbf{p}_{i}^{2}}M(\mathbf{y}(i))^{2}\cdot\mathbf{p}_{i}=\sum_{i\in G}\frac{\mathbf{w}_{i}}{\mathbf{p}_{i}}M(\mathbf{y}(i))\cdot\mathbf{w}_{i}M(\mathbf{y}(i))

\displaystyle\leq O(1)\frac{\mathsf{OPT}^{p_{M}}}{m\cdot\epsilon^{p_{M}}}\sum_{i\in G}\mathbf{w}_{i}M(\mathbf{y}(i))=O(1)\frac{\mathsf{OPT}^{2p_{M}}}{m\cdot\epsilon^{p_{M}}}

Then by Bernstein’s inequality,

\displaystyle\Pr\left\{\left\lvert\sum_{i\in G}W_{i}-\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\mid_{G}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}^{p_{M}}\right\rvert>t\right\}

\displaystyle\leq 2\exp\left\lparen-\Theta(1)\frac{t^{2}}{\frac{1}{m\epsilon^{p_{M}}}\mathsf{OPT}^{2p_{M}}+\frac{1}{m\epsilon^{p_{M}}}\mathsf{OPT}^{p_{M}}t}\right\rparen

\displaystyle=2\exp\left\lparen-\Theta(1)\frac{mt^{2}\epsilon^{p_{M}}}{\mathsf{OPT}^{p_{M}}(\mathsf{OPT}^{p_{M}}+t)}\right\rparen.

For
t=\epsilon\mathsf{OPT}^{p_{M}}
, this gives a bound of

2\exp\left\lparen-\Theta(1)\frac{m(\epsilon\mathsf{OPT}^{p_{M}})^{2}\epsilon^{p_{M}}}{\mathsf{OPT}^{2p_{M}}}\right\rparen=2\exp\left\lparen-\Theta(1)m\epsilon^{2+p_{M}}\right\rparen.

∎

Lemma 5.5
.

Consider the setting of Lemma
5.4
. Recall that
\mathbf{x}_{c}
satisfies
\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{c}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}=O(\mathsf{OPT})
and
\mathbf{b}^{\prime}=\mathbf{b}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{c}
. Let

m=O\left\lparen\frac{d}{\epsilon^{2+p_{M}}}\left\lparen\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}\right\rparen\left\lparen\log\frac{1}{\delta}\right\rparen\right\rparen

and let
\mathbf{w}^{\prime}
be as defined in Lemma
5.4
with the above choice of
m
. Let
\bar{\mathbf{x}}
satisfy
\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\bar{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{b}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}^{p_{M}}\leq(1+\epsilon)\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}^{p_{M}}
. Then, with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{x}_{c}+\bar{\mathbf{x}})-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}\leq(1+O(\epsilon/\delta))\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.2
, we have that
\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\tilde{\mathbf{x}}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}\leq O(\mathsf{OPT})
. We may thus restrict our attention to the ball
\mathcal{B}_{\rho}^{M,\mathbf{w}}\subseteq\mathcal{V}
of radius
\rho=O(\mathsf{OPT})
, since

\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}=\min_{\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{B}_{\rho}^{M,\mathbf{w}}}\left\lVert\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{b}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}

by the above.

Next, we show Equation (
12
) for every
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{B}_{\rho}^{M,\mathbf{w}}
. By Lemma
C.11
, it suffices to show the approximation guarantee for every
\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{N}
and
\mathcal{N}-\mathbf{b}^{\prime}
, for an
O(\epsilon^{p_{M}/q_{m}})\rho
-cover
\mathcal{N}
of
\mathcal{B}_{\rho}^{M,\mathbf{w}}
. Let
\mathcal{N}
be such a net over
\mathcal{B}_{\rho}^{M,\mathbf{w}}
, which has size at most
\left\lvert\mathcal{N}\right\rvert\leq O(d\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
by Lemma
C.8
. We may then use Lemma
5.4
and a union bound over the net
\mathcal{N}
to get Equation (
12
) for every
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{B}_{\rho}^{M,\mathbf{w}}
.

Finally, note that we now have the conclusion of Lemma
5.3
, since we have satisfied its assumption. Then,

\displaystyle\lVert\mathbf{A}\bar{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{b}^{\prime}\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}^{p_{M}}

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\mathbf{A}\bar{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{b}^{\prime}\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}^{p_{M}}-C+O(\epsilon/\delta)\cdot\mathsf{OPT}^{p_{M}}

\displaystyle\leq(1+\epsilon)\cdot\min_{\mathbf{x}}\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}^{\prime}\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}^{p_{M}}-C+O(\epsilon/\delta)\cdot\mathsf{OPT}^{p}

\displaystyle\leq(1+\epsilon)\min_{\mathbf{x}}(\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}^{\prime}\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}^{p_{M}}+C)-C+O(\epsilon/\delta)\cdot\mathsf{OPT}^{p}

\displaystyle\leq\min_{\mathbf{x}}\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}^{\prime}\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}^{p_{M}}+O(\epsilon/\delta)\cdot\mathsf{OPT}^{p}

∎

5.3
Nearly Input Sparsity Time Algorithm

We now combine the active regression algorithms obtained in this section with the sensitivity bounds of Section
4
.

Algorithm 8

Input sparsity time, constant factor
M
-estimator active regression

input:
Matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, measurement vector
\mathbf{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

output:
Constant factor approximate solution
\mathbf{x}_{c}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
to
\min_{\mathbf{x}}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{M}
.

1:

Run Algorithm
4.9
to obtain approximate sensitvities
\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{M}(\mathbf{A})
with
\tau=T
10
10

10

See Theorem
5.6
for the definition of this setting.

2:

Given weights
\mathbf{1}_{n}
, obtain
\mathbf{w}
according to Definition
4.7

3:

Run Algorithm
4.9
to obtain approximate sensitvities
\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{M,\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{A})
with
\tau=d

4:

Given weights
\mathbf{w}
, obtain
\mathbf{w}^{\prime}
according to Definition
4.7

5:

Let
\mathbf{x}_{c}
be an approximate solution
\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{c}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}\leq O(1)\min_{\mathbf{x}}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}

6:

return

\mathbf{x}_{c}

Algorithm 9

Input sparsity time, relative error
M
-estimator active regression

input:
Matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, measurement vector
\mathbf{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

output:
Approximate solution
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
to
\min_{\mathbf{x}}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{M}
.

1:

Run Algorithm
8
to obtain a constant factor solution
\mathbf{x}_{c}

2:

Set
\mathbf{b}^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathbf{b}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{c}

3:

Run Algorithm
4.9
to obtain approximate sensitvities
\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{M}(\mathbf{A})
with
\tau=T

4:

Given weights
\mathbf{1}_{n}
, obtain
\mathbf{v}
according to Definition
4.7

5:

Run Algorithm
4.9
to obtain approximate sensitvities
\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{M,\mathbf{v}}(\mathbf{A})
with
\tau=d

6:

Given weights
\mathbf{v}
, obtain
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}
according to Definition
4.7

7:

Let
\bar{\mathbf{x}}
be an approximate solution
\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\bar{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{v}^{\prime}}\leq(1+\epsilon)\min_{\mathbf{x}}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{v}^{\prime}}

8:

Let
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}\leftarrow\mathbf{x}_{c}+\bar{\mathbf{x}}

9:

return

\tilde{\mathbf{x}}

Theorem 5.6
.

There is an algorithm, Algorithm
9
, which with probability at least
99/100
computes
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}
such that

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\tilde{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{M}\leq(1+\epsilon)\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{M}

in time

O\left\lparen\nnz(\mathbf{A})\log^{3}n+\frac{(Td^{\max\{1,p_{M}/2\}}+T^{2})\log^{4}n}{\epsilon^{2+p_{M}}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}\right\rparen

where constant factor
\ell_{p_{M}}
Lewis weight approximation for an
n\times d
matrix
\mathbf{B}
takes time
O(\nnz(\mathbf{B})+T))
time. Furthermore, the algorithm only queries

O\left\lparen\frac{d^{\max\{2,p_{M}/2+1\}}\log^{3}n}{\epsilon^{2+p_{M}}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}\right\rparen

entries of
\mathbf{b}
.

Proof.

In order to obtain nearly input sparsity time algorithms, we will need to apply the sensitivity sampling algorithms of Theorem
4.9
twice, once to reduce the number of rows to
O(\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(d,\log n))
rows in nearly input sparsity time, and once to reduce the number of rows all the way down to
O(d^{\max\{2,p/2+1\}}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}\log(n))
rows in an additional
O(\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(d,\log n))
time.

Constant Factor Approximation.

We will first obtain a constant factor solution
\mathbf{x}_{c}
such that

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{c}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{M}\leq O(1)\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{M}.

To do this, we first obtain sensitivity upper bounds
\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{M}(\mathbf{A})
by Theorem
4.9
with
\tau=T
, so that they sum to

\tilde{\mathcal{T}}^{M}(\mathbf{A})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{M}(\mathbf{A})=O\left\lparen d^{\max\{1,p_{M}/2\}}\log^{2}n+T\right\rparen

and can be computed in time

O\left\lparen\nnz(\mathbf{A})\log^{2}n+n\log^{2}n\right\rparen=O\left\lparen\nnz(\mathbf{A})\log^{2}n\right\rparen.

By Lemma
5.1
, we can perform sensitivity sampling with these sensitivity estimates to obtain a set of weights
\mathbf{w}
such that if
\mathbf{x}_{c}
satisfies

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{c}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}\leq O(1)\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}},

then

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{c}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{M}\leq O(1)\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{M}.

Next, we obtain
weighted
sensivitity upper bounds
\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{M,\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{A})
by Corollary
4.12
with
\tau=d
. Note that
\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{\infty}=O(n)
since the sensitivity upper bounds are all at least
1/n
, so the weighted sensitivities sum to at most

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{M,\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{A})=O(d^{\max\{1,p_{M}/2\}}\log^{3}n).

Furthermore, the weights
\mathbf{w}
only have

\nnz(\mathbf{w})=O\left\lparen d\cdot\tilde{\mathcal{T}}^{M}(\mathbf{A})\right\rparen=O(d^{\max\{2,p_{M}/2+1\}}\log^{2}n+dT)

nonzero entries with constant probability by Markov’s inequality, so the running time is

\displaystyle O\left\lparen\nnz(\mathbf{A})\log^{3}(\nnz(\mathbf{w}))+\log n\frac{\nnz(\mathbf{w})T}{d}\log(\nnz(\mathbf{w}))\right\rparen

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle O\left\lparen\nnz(\mathbf{A})\log^{3}n+(Td^{\max\{1,p_{M}/2\}}+T^{2})\log^{4}n\right\rparen.

By Lemma
5.1
, we can perform sensitivity sampling with these sensitivity estimates to obtain a set of weights
\mathbf{w}^{\prime}
. We can now define our
\mathbf{x}_{c}
to be the minimizer of

\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}},

which means that

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{c}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}\leq O(1)\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}},

which in turn implies that

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{c}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{M}\leq O(1)\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{M},

as desired. Computing
\mathbf{x}_{c}
only required
\nnz(\mathbf{w}^{\prime})
entries of
\mathbf{b}
to sample, which is only
O(d^{\max\{2,p_{M}/2+1\}}\log^{3}n)
entries.

Relative Error Approximation.

With the constant factor solution
\mathbf{x}_{c}
in hand, we can now apply Lemma
5.5
to refine this to a relative error approximation. Following Lemma
5.5
, let

\mathbf{b}^{\prime}=\mathbf{b}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{c}

Recall the sensitivity upper bounds
\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{M}(\mathbf{A})
constructed earlier. This time, we obtain a different set of weights
\mathbf{v}\geq\mathbf{1}_{n}
by following Lemma
5.5
, so that the expected number of rows sampled is now

\displaystyle\E[\nnz(\mathbf{v})]\leq O\left\lparen\frac{d}{\epsilon^{2+p_{M}}}\left\lparen\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}\right\rparen\cdot\tilde{\mathcal{T}}^{M}(\mathbf{A})\right\rparen

\displaystyle=O\left\lparen\frac{d(d^{\max\{1,p_{M}/2\}}\log^{2}n+T)}{\epsilon^{2+p_{M}}}\left\lparen\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}\right\rparen\right\rparen

\displaystyle=O\left\lparen\frac{d^{\max\{2,p_{M}/2+1\}}\log^{2}n+dT}{\epsilon^{2+p_{M}}}\left\lparen\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}\right\rparen\right\rparen.

By a Markov bound, with constant probability, the number of rows sampled has the same bound, up to constant factors. We are then guaranteed by Lemma
5.5
that if
\bar{\mathbf{x}}
satisfies

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\bar{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{b}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{v}}\leq(1+\epsilon)\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{v}},

then

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\bar{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{b}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{M}\leq(1+O(\epsilon))\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{M}.

Then, as done in the previous constant factor approximation step, we perform another round of weighted sensitivity sampling. Let
\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{M,\mathbf{v}}(\mathbf{A})
be weighted sensitivity upper bounds given by Corollary
4.12
with
\tau=d
, which sum to at most
O(d\log^{3}n)
and be computed in time at most

\displaystyle O\left\lparen\nnz(\mathbf{A})\log^{3}(\nnz(\mathbf{v}))+\log n\frac{\nnz(\mathbf{v})T}{d}\log(\nnz(\mathbf{v}))\right\rparen

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle O\left\lparen\nnz(\mathbf{A})\log^{3}n+\frac{(Td^{\max\{1,p_{M}/2\}}+T^{2})\log^{4}n}{\epsilon^{2+p_{M}}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}\right\rparen.

We can then again do weighted sensitivity sampling according to the
\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{M,\mathbf{v}}(\mathbf{A})
as in Lemma
5.5
to obtain a set of weights
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}
such that if
\bar{\mathbf{x}}
satisfies

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\bar{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{b}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{v}^{\prime}}\leq(1+\epsilon)\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{v}^{\prime}},

then

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\bar{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{b}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{v}}\leq(1+O(\epsilon))\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{v}}.

It then follows from the previous discussion that

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\bar{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{b}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{M}\leq(1+O(\epsilon))\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{M}.

Furthermore, computing
\bar{\mathbf{x}}
only required
\nnz(\mathbf{v}^{\prime})
entries of
\mathbf{b}^{\prime}
to sample, which is only

O\left\lparen\frac{d\cdot\tilde{\mathcal{T}}^{M,\mathbf{v}}(\mathbf{A})}{\epsilon^{2+p_{M}}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}\right\rparen=O\left\lparen\frac{d^{\max\{2,p_{M}/2+1\}}\log^{3}n}{\epsilon^{2+p_{M}}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}\right\rparen

samples.
∎

6
Applications: Active Regression for the Huber Loss

Note that our sampling bound for general
M
-estimators from Section
5
is loose by a factor of
d
compared to the corresponding bounds for
\ell_{p}
. This is due to the reliance on a naïve net argument rather than using the more efficient construction of
[
BLM89
,
SZ01
,
LT91
]
. A natural question then is whether this can be improved or not, for loss functions other than the
\ell_{p}
loss. We answer this in the affirmative for the important special case of the Huber loss.

More specificially, in this section, we obtain a sampling bound of

O(d^{4-2\sqrt{2}}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(\epsilon^{-1},\log n))

for subspace embeddings as well as active regression for the Huber loss, where
4-2\sqrt{2}\approx 1.17157
. This bound is substantially better compared to the
O(d^{2}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(\epsilon^{-1},\log n))
bound for other losses, which is obtained by a naïve Bernstein bound over a net, as well as a previous subspace embedding result of
[
CW15b
]
which achieved roughly
O(d^{4})
rows.

In what follows, we will first develop our recursive sampling algorithm for obtaining subspace embeddings for the Huber loss with the above sampling complexity, which can be used to obtain constant factor solutions in the active setting by Lemma
5.1
. In Section
6.6
, we then show how to modify our subspace embedding construction in order to get relative error bounds, again following Section
5
.

6.1
Properties of the Huber Loss

We first collect properties of the Huber loss needed to apply previous results for
M
-estimators. It is known that the Huber loss is polynomially bounded above with degree
2
and constant
1
, and polynomially bounded below with degree
1
and constant
1
.

Lemma 6.1

(Lemma 2.2 of
[
CW15b
]
)
.

The Huber loss satisfies

\frac{y}{x}\leq\frac{H(y)}{H(x)}\leq\frac{y^{2}}{x^{2}}

for all
y>x\geq 0
.

This in turn implies that
H^{1/2}
is subadditive, since it is known that polynomial growth with degree
1
and constant
1
implies subadditivity:

Lemma 6.2

(Theorem 103 of
[
HLP73
]
)
.

Let
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
. Suppose that
x\mapsto x^{-1}f(x)
is decreasing, that is,

\frac{f(y)}{f(x)}\leq\frac{y}{x}

for all
y>x
. Then,
f
is subadditive.

The following is a corollary that discretizes Lemma
1.10
:

Corollary 6.3

(Huber Inequality ver. 4)
.

Let
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and let
\alpha\in[0,1/2]
and
\gamma=n^{-\alpha}
with
2/n\leq\gamma\leq 1
. Let

T\supseteq\left\{i\in[n]:H(\mathbf{y}_{i})\leq\gamma\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H}^{2}\right\},

\epsilon=c/\log n
for some sufficiently small constant
c
, and let

\mathcal{I}=\left\{1,1+\epsilon,1+2\epsilon,\dots,2-\epsilon,2\right\}=\left\{1+\epsilon\cdot i:i\in\{0\}\cup[1/\epsilon]\right\}.

Then, for some constant
c>0
, either

\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\mid_{T}\right\rVert_{H}^{2}\geq c\frac{1}{(\gamma n)^{3-2\sqrt{2}}}\min_{p\in\mathcal{I}}\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\mid_{T}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}

or

\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H}^{2}\geq c\gamma\min_{p\in\{1,2\}}\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}

where
3-2\sqrt{2}\approx 0.17157
.

Proof.

It was shown in the proof of Lemma
1.10
that if
\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{1}\geq 2n
, then
\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H}^{2}=\Theta(\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{1})
, and if
\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 1
, then
\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H}^{2}=\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
. Thus, we may assume that all the entries of
\mathbf{y}
are bounded by
O(n)
, and
\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{\infty}\geq 1
. Note then that the entries that are at most
1/2n
can make up at most an
\ell_{p}
mass of
1/2
, and thus only affects the claimed inequality by a constant factor for any
p
. For all other entries, the magnitude is bounded below by
1/\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(n)
and above by
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(n)
, so an additive
O(1/\log n)
difference in the exponent can only affect the bounds by a constant factor. We thus conclude the desired result.
∎

6.2
Net Arguments for the Huber Loss

When constructing subspace embeddings for norms, it suffices to preserve the lengths of vectors on the unit normed sphere, by the scale invariance/homogeneity of norms. On the other hand, general
M
-estimators, including the Huber norm, do not necessarily satisfy scale invariance, which means that this strategy, as is, does not work. For the Huber norm, we show that for sufficiently small and sufficiently large scales, we have at least an
(1\pm\epsilon)
-approximate scale invariance, which allows us to obtain subspace embeddings by union bounding over finitely many scales. For all scales near
0
, this is because the Huber norm coincides with the
\ell_{2}
norm. For larger scales, we show that the Huber norm is the
\ell_{1}
norm up to
(1\pm\epsilon)
factors. Then, the following result then shows that in order to prove subspace embeddings, it suffices to preserve the Huber norms of Huber balls at approximately
\log n
radii.

The following lemma shows that for extremely large radii, the Huber norm essentially coincides with the
\ell_{1}
norm.

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a set of weights. Suppose
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
has weighted Huber norm at least
\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}^{2}\geq n\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{\infty}/\epsilon
. Then,

\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}^{2}=(1\pm\epsilon)\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{1,\mathbf{w}}.

Proof.

Suppose that
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
has Huber norm at least
\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H}^{2}\geq n\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{\infty}/\epsilon
. Now define the set

S\coloneqq\left\{i\in[n]:\left\lvert\mathbf{y}_{i}\right\rvert\leq 1\right\}.

Then,
\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\mid_{S}\right\rVert_{1,\mathbf{w}}\leq n\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{\infty}
so

\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{1,\mathbf{w}}=\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\mid_{\overline{S}}\right\rVert_{1,\mathbf{w}}+\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\mid_{S}\right\rVert_{1,\mathbf{w}}\leq\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\mid_{\overline{S}}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}^{2}+n\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq(1+\epsilon)\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\mid_{\overline{S}}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}^{2}.

On the other hand, we have
\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{1,\mathbf{w}}\geq\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}^{2}
, so we conclude.
∎

We now show the nets we need to obtain subspace embeddings for the Huber norm.

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
and let
\mathcal{V}=\Span(\mathbf{A})
. Let
\mathbf{w},\mathbf{w}^{\prime}\geq\mathbf{1}_{n}
be two sets of weights. Let
\mathbf{w}^{\prime}\geq\mathbf{1}_{n}
be another set of weights. Let
\epsilon>0
. Let

\ell\coloneqq\log_{2}\sqrt{\frac{n\left\lVert\mathbf{w}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{\infty}}{\epsilon}}=O\left\lparen\log\frac{n\left\lVert\mathbf{w}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{\infty}}{\epsilon}\right\rparen.

For each
k\in[\ell]
, let
\rho=2^{k}
and suppose that

\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}=\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}\pm\epsilon\rho

for every
\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{B}_{\rho}^{H}
, for every
k\in[\ell]
. Then,

\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}=(1\pm O(\epsilon))\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}

for all
\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{V}
.

Proof.

We handle three cases: scales near
0
, scales near infinity, and all scales in between.

Scales near
0
.

Note that if the weighted Huber norm of
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
is at most
1
, then all entries of
\mathbf{y}
are at most
1
in absolute value, so
\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}^{2}=\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{2,\mathbf{w}}^{2}
, and thus the Huber norm is scale invariant at these scales. Thus,
(1\pm\epsilon)
-approximations for the unit Huber sphere implies
(1\pm\epsilon)
-approximations for all vectors with
\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}^{2}\leq 1
.

Scales near infinity.

Suppose that we have the guarantee that

\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}^{2}=(1\pm\epsilon)\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}^{2}

for every
\mathbf{x}
in the Huber sphere
\mathcal{S}_{n\left\lVert\mathbf{w}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{\infty}/\epsilon}^{H,\mathbf{w}}
of radius
n\left\lVert\mathbf{w}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{\infty}/\epsilon
. Then, if
\mathbf{x}
has any Huber norm at least
n\left\lVert\mathbf{w}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{\infty}/\epsilon
, then

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}^{2}

\displaystyle=(1\pm\epsilon)\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1,\mathbf{w}}

Lemma
6.4

\displaystyle=(1\pm\epsilon)\frac{\epsilon}{n\left\lVert\mathbf{w}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{\infty}}\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1,\mathbf{w}}\cdot\left\lVert\frac{n\left\lVert\mathbf{w}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{\infty}}{\epsilon}\frac{\mathbf{x}}{\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1,\mathbf{w}}}\right\rVert_{1,\mathbf{w}}

Scale invariance

\displaystyle=(1\pm\epsilon)^{2}\frac{\epsilon}{n\left\lVert\mathbf{w}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{\infty}}\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1,\mathbf{w}}\cdot\left\lVert\frac{n\left\lVert\mathbf{w}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{\infty}}{\epsilon}\frac{\mathbf{x}}{\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1,\mathbf{w}}}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}^{2}

Lemma
6.4

\displaystyle=(1\pm\epsilon)^{3}\frac{\epsilon}{n\left\lVert\mathbf{w}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{\infty}}\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1,\mathbf{w}}\cdot\left\lVert\frac{n\left\lVert\mathbf{w}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{\infty}}{\epsilon}\frac{\mathbf{x}}{\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1,\mathbf{w}}}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}^{2}

Approximation guarantee

\displaystyle=(1\pm\epsilon)^{4}\frac{\epsilon}{n\left\lVert\mathbf{w}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{\infty}}\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1,\mathbf{w}}\cdot\left\lVert\frac{n\left\lVert\mathbf{w}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{\infty}}{\epsilon}\frac{\mathbf{x}}{\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1,\mathbf{w}}}\right\rVert_{1,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}

Lemma
6.4

\displaystyle=(1\pm\epsilon)^{4}\cdot\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}^{2}

Scale invariance

\displaystyle=(1\pm\epsilon)^{5}\cdot\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}^{2}

Lemma
6.4

Thus, it suffices to prove approximation guarantees for the Huber sphere
\mathcal{S}_{n\left\lVert\mathbf{w}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{\infty}/\epsilon}^{H,\mathbf{w}}
to handle all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with Huber norms at least
n\left\lVert\mathbf{w}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{\infty}/\epsilon
.

Scales in between.

For the remaining scales between
1
and
n\left\lVert\mathbf{w}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{\infty}/\epsilon
, we work in powers of
2
so that there are approximately

\ell\coloneqq\log_{2}\sqrt{\frac{n\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{\infty}}{\epsilon}}=O\left\lparen\log\frac{n\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{\infty}}{\epsilon}\right\rparen

total scales. Then for any
\rho\in[1,\sqrt{n\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{\infty}/\epsilon}]
, there exists a
\rho\leq\rho_{k}\leq 2\rho
such that we can get an additive error of
O(\epsilon)\rho_{k}=O(\epsilon)\rho
for every
\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{B}_{\rho_{k}}
, which in particular contains
\mathcal{B}_{\rho}^{H}
.
∎

6.3
Compact Rounding for the Huber Loss

In Section
6.2
, we have reduced our task to proving approximation guarantees for a small number of Huber spheres. We now focus on showing small sampling bounds for a single Huber sphere.

The following rounding lemma is implicit in the proof of Theorems 7.3 and 7.4 of
[
BLM89
]
, which uses these net constructions.

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
1\leq p<\infty
and let

\mathcal{B}_{p}\coloneqq\left\{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}:\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{p}\leq 1\right\}.

Let
\epsilon\geq\gamma>0
and let
\mathcal{N}_{\gamma}
be a standard
\gamma
-net on
\mathcal{B}_{p}
with the
\ell_{p}
norm, which has

\log\left\lvert\mathcal{N}_{\gamma}\right\rvert\leq O\left\lparen d\log\frac{1}{\gamma}\right\rparen

by Lemma
C.7
. Let
\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{N}_{\gamma}
. Let
\ell=\left\lceil\log_{1+\epsilon}(2^{1/p}d^{1/p\lor 1/2})\right\rceil
. Then, for
k\in[\ell]
, there exists a rounding

\tilde{\mathbf{y}}=\sum_{k=0}^{\ell}\tilde{\mathbf{y}}^{(k)}

such that:

•

\left\lvert\tilde{\mathbf{y}}(i)-\mathbf{y}(i)\right\rvert\leq 3\epsilon\left\lvert\mathbf{y}(i)\right\rvert
for all
i\in[n]

•

\left\lvert\tilde{\mathbf{y}}^{(k)}(i)\right\rvert\leq\mathbf{W}_{i,i}^{1/p}(1+\epsilon)^{k}

•

\tilde{\mathbf{y}}^{(k)}
for
0\leq k\leq\ell
have disjoint supports

•

each
\tilde{\mathbf{y}}^{(k)}
is drawn from a set of vectors
\mathcal{D}_{k}
with size at most

\log\left\lvert\mathcal{D}_{k}\right\rvert\leq c(p)\frac{d}{\epsilon^{1+\beta}(1+\epsilon)^{\beta k}}\left\lparen\log\frac{n}{\epsilon}+\log\frac{1}{\gamma}\right\rparen

where
\beta=(2\land p)
and
c(p)=O\left\lparen\max\left\{p,\frac{1}{p-1}\right\}\right\rparen
.

The lemma above is used to prove the following similar result for the Huber loss.

Lemma 6.7
.

Let
\epsilon\in(0,1)
and
\rho\geq 1
. Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
and let
\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{B}_{\rho}^{H}\subseteq\Span(\mathbf{A})
. Let
\mathcal{I}\subset[1,2]
be the set in the statement of Corollary
6.3
and suppose that
\mathbf{y}
satisfies

\frac{\min_{p\in\mathcal{I}}\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}}{\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H}^{2}}\leq\kappa.

Then, there exists a
p\in\mathcal{I}
,
\ell=O((\log d)/\epsilon^{2})
, and a rounding

\tilde{\mathbf{y}}=\sum_{k=0}^{\ell}\tilde{\mathbf{y}}^{(k)}

such that:

•

\left\lVert\tilde{\mathbf{y}}-\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H}\leq O(\epsilon)\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H}

•

H\left\lparen\left\lvert\tilde{\mathbf{y}}^{(k)}(i)\right\rvert\right\rparen\leq O(\kappa)\left\lparen\frac{\mathbf{w}_{i}^{p}(\mathbf{A})}{d}+\frac{1}{n}\right\rparen(1+\epsilon^{2})^{pk}\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H}^{2}

•

\tilde{\mathbf{y}}^{(k)}
for
0\leq k\leq\ell
have disjoint supports

•

each
\tilde{\mathbf{y}}^{(k)}
is drawn from a set of vectors
\mathcal{D}_{k}
with size at most

\log\left\lvert\mathcal{D}_{k}\right\rvert\leq O(1)\frac{d}{\epsilon^{2(1+p)}(1+\epsilon^{2})^{pk}}(\log n)\left\lparen\log\frac{n}{\epsilon}\right\rparen.

Proof.

Let
\gamma=\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(\epsilon/n)
and let
\mathcal{N}_{\gamma}^{p}
be a
\gamma
-net for the
\ell_{p}
unit ball for
p\in\mathcal{I}
. We apply the results of Lemma
6.6
to each of these nets to obtain sets of vectors
\mathcal{D}_{k}^{p}
, corresponding to the set of vectors
\mathcal{D}_{k}
in the lemma statement when we apply the result with
p
.

Now let
\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{B}_{\rho}^{H}
. By assumption, we may find a
p\in\mathcal{I}
so that

\rho^{2}\leq\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}\leq\kappa\rho^{2}.

We then let
L\leq O(\log\kappa)\leq O(\log n)
be such that

\rho^{2}\cdot 2^{L}\leq\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}\leq\rho^{2}\cdot 2^{L+1}.

Let

\beta\coloneqq(\rho^{2}\cdot 2^{L+1})^{1/p}=\Theta(\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{p}).

Then,
\left\lVert\mathbf{y}/\beta\right\rVert_{p}\leq 1
so there exists a
\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{\gamma}^{p}
such that

\left\lVert\mathbf{y}^{\prime}-\mathbf{y}/\beta\right\rVert_{p}\leq\gamma

so

\left\lVert\beta\mathbf{y}^{\prime}-\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H}^{2}\leq\left\lVert\beta\mathbf{y}^{\prime}-\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}\leq\gamma^{p}\beta^{p}\leq 2\gamma^{p}\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}\leq 2\gamma^{p}\kappa\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H}^{2}\leq\epsilon^{2}\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H}^{2}

for an appropriate choice of
\gamma
.

We now let

\tilde{\mathbf{y}}=\sum_{k=0}^{\ell}\tilde{\mathbf{y}}^{(k)}

be the rounding given in Lemma
6.6
for
\mathbf{y}^{\prime}
, which is in the
\ell_{p}
unit ball, with the
\epsilon
in the lemma set to our
\epsilon^{2}
. Then,

\left\lvert\beta\mathbf{y}^{\prime}(i)-\beta\tilde{\mathbf{y}}(i)\right\rvert\leq 3\epsilon^{2}\left\lvert\beta\mathbf{y}^{\prime}(i)\right\rvert

for all
i\in[n]
so

\displaystyle\left\lVert\beta\mathbf{y}^{\prime}-\beta\tilde{\mathbf{y}}\right\rVert_{H}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}H(\left\lvert\beta\mathbf{y}^{\prime}(i)-\beta\tilde{\mathbf{y}}(i)\right\rvert)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}H(3\epsilon^{2}\left\lvert\beta\mathbf{y}^{\prime}(i)\right\rvert)

\displaystyle\leq 3\epsilon^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}H(\left\lvert\beta\mathbf{y}^{\prime}(i)\right\rvert)

\displaystyle=3\epsilon^{2}\left\lVert\beta\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{H}^{2}

\displaystyle=O(\epsilon^{2})\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H}^{2}.

Thus by the triangle inequality for the Huber norm,

\left\lVert\beta\tilde{\mathbf{y}}-\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H}\leq O(\epsilon)\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H}.

Futhermore,

\left\lvert\beta\tilde{\mathbf{y}}^{(k)}(i)\right\rvert\leq\mathbf{W}_{i,i}^{1/p}(1+\epsilon^{2})^{k}\beta

so

\displaystyle H\left\lparen\left\lvert\beta\tilde{\mathbf{y}}^{(k)}(i)\right\rvert\right\rparen

\displaystyle\leq O(1)\mathbf{W}_{i,i}(1+\epsilon^{2})^{pk}\beta^{p}

H(y)\leq O\left\lparen\min_{p\in[1,2]}y^{p}\right\rparen

\displaystyle\leq O(1)\left\lparen\frac{\mathbf{w}_{i}^{p}(\mathbf{A})}{d}+\frac{1}{n}\right\rparen(1+\epsilon^{2})^{pk}\cdot 2\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}

\displaystyle\leq O(\kappa)\left\lparen\frac{\mathbf{w}_{i}^{p}(\mathbf{A})}{d}+\frac{1}{n}\right\rparen(1+\epsilon^{2})^{pk}\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H}^{2}.

We conclude as desired by rescaling the
\tilde{\mathbf{y}}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{y}}^{(k)}
by
\beta
.
∎

6.4
Sampling Bounds

With the above net results in hand, we can now complete the argument for one step of the sampling recursion. In order to implement a recursive sampling scheme, note that we need to handle weighted Huber norms. These weighted Huber norms will be handled by handling the weights in a small number of groups such that the weights are within constant factors of each other.

6.4.1
Sampling Guarantees for a Single Weight Class and a Single Radius

We start with a Bernstein bound using the net results, for a single group of weights and at a single radius.

Lemma 6.8

(Bernstein bounds for Huber sampling)
.

Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
and let
\mathcal{V}=\Span(\mathbf{A})
. Let
\epsilon\in(0,1)
be an accuracy parameter,
\delta>0
a failure rate parameter,
\rho\geq 1
a Huber radius, and
\kappa\geq 1
a distortion parameter. Let

m\coloneqq\frac{d}{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(\epsilon)}(\log^{2}d)\left\lparen\log^{2}\frac{n}{\epsilon}\right\rparen\left\lparen\log\frac{\log d}{\delta\epsilon}\right\rparen.

Let
\mathcal{I}
be as in the statement of Corollary
6.3
. Let
\mathbf{w}\geq\mathbf{1}_{n}
be a set of weights such that

\frac{\max_{i\in[n]}\mathbf{w}_{i}}{\min_{i\in[n]}\mathbf{w}_{i}}\leq 2

(13)

and let
w=\min_{i\in[n]}\mathbf{w}_{i}
. Let
\mathbf{w}^{\prime}
be chosen randomly so that

\mathbf{w}_{i}^{\prime}\coloneqq\begin{cases}\mathbf{w}_{i}/\mathbf{p}_{i}&\text{w.p. $\mathbf{p}_{i}$}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

where

\mathbf{p}_{i}\geq\min\left\{1,m\cdot\kappa\left[\frac{1}{n}+\sum_{p\in\mathcal{I}}\frac{\mathbf{w}_{i}^{p}(\mathbf{A})}{d}\right]\right\}

Let

S_{\kappa}\coloneqq\left\{\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{V}:\frac{\min_{p\in\mathcal{I}}\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}}{\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H}^{2}}\leq\kappa\right\}

Then with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}=\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}\pm\epsilon w\rho

for all
\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{N}\cap S_{\kappa}
, where
\mathcal{N}
is an
\epsilon^{2}\rho
-net over
\mathcal{B}_{\rho}^{H}
given by Lemma
6.7
, by setting
\epsilon
in the lemma to
\epsilon^{2}
.

Proof.

Let
\tilde{\mathbf{y}}\in\mathcal{N}\cap S_{\kappa}
be a net vector in the result of Lemma
6.7
and let
p
be the corresponding
p\in\mathcal{I}
. Note that

\left\lvert\left\lVert\tilde{\mathbf{y}}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}^{2}-\left\lVert\tilde{\mathbf{y}}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}^{2}\right\rvert\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\ell}\left\lvert\left\lVert\tilde{\mathbf{y}}^{(k)}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}^{2}-\left\lVert\tilde{\mathbf{y}}^{(k)}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}^{2}\right\rvert

by the disjointness of the supports of the
\tilde{\mathbf{y}}^{(k)}
. Thus, it suffices to bound each term in the sum by
\epsilon\rho^{2}/(\ell+1)
.

Fix a
0\leq k\leq\ell
and define the random variable

W_{i}\coloneqq\mathbf{w}_{i}^{\prime}H(\tilde{\mathbf{y}}^{(k)}(i))

for each
i\in[n]
. Then,

\E\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}W_{i}\right]=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\mathbf{w}_{i}}{\mathbf{p}_{i}}H(\tilde{\mathbf{y}}^{(k)}(i))\cdot\mathbf{p}_{i}=\left\lVert\tilde{\mathbf{y}}^{(k)}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}^{2}.

We next bound the variance:

\displaystyle\operatorname{Var}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}W_{i}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\operatorname{Var}[W_{i}]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\mathbf{w}_{i}^{2}}{\mathbf{p}_{i}^{2}}H(\tilde{\mathbf{y}}^{(k)}(i))^{2}\cdot\mathbf{p}_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{\mathbf{p}_{i}}\mathbf{w}_{i}H(\tilde{\mathbf{y}}^{(k)}(i))\cdot\mathbf{w}_{i}H(\tilde{\mathbf{y}}^{(k)}(i))

Note that
W_{i}
is almost surely bounded by

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathbf{p}_{i}}\mathbf{w}_{i}H(\tilde{\mathbf{y}}^{(k)}(i))

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{w}_{i}\frac{O(\kappa)\left\lparen\frac{\mathbf{w}_{i}^{p}(\mathbf{A})}{d}+\frac{1}{n}\right\rparen(1+\epsilon^{4})^{pk}\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H}^{2}}{m\cdot O(\kappa)\left\lparen\frac{\mathbf{w}_{i}^{p}(\mathbf{A})}{d}+\frac{1}{n}\right\rparen}

Lemma
6.7

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{m}(1+\epsilon^{4})^{pk}\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}^{2}

Equation (
13
)

so the variance is bounded by

\operatorname{Var}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}W_{i}\right]\leq\frac{2}{m}(1+\epsilon^{4})^{pk}\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{w}_{i}H(\tilde{\mathbf{y}}^{(k)}(i))=\frac{2}{m}(1+\epsilon^{4})^{pk}\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}^{4}.

Then by Bernstein’s inequality,

\displaystyle\Pr\left\{\left\lvert\sum_{i=1}^{n}W_{i}-\left\lVert\tilde{\mathbf{y}}^{(k)}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}^{2}\right\rvert>t\right\}

\displaystyle\leq 2\exp\left\lparen-\Theta(1)\frac{t^{2}}{\frac{1}{m}(1+\epsilon^{4})^{pk}\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}^{4}+\frac{1}{m}(1+\epsilon^{4})^{pk}\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}^{2}t}\right\rparen

\displaystyle=2\exp\left\lparen-\Theta(1)\frac{mt^{2}}{(1+\epsilon^{4})^{pk}\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}^{2}(\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}^{2}+t)}\right\rparen.

For
t=\epsilon w^{2}\rho^{2}/(\ell+1)
, this gives a bound of

2\exp\left\lparen-\Theta(1)\frac{m(\epsilon w^{2}\rho^{2}/(\ell+1))^{2}}{(1+\epsilon^{4})^{pk}w^{4}\rho^{4}}\right\rparen=2\exp\left\lparen-\Theta(1)\frac{m\epsilon^{2}}{(\ell+1)^{2}(1+\epsilon^{4})^{pk}}\right\rparen.

We then set

\displaystyle m

\displaystyle=O(1)\frac{d}{\epsilon^{4(1+p)+2}}(\ell+1)^{2}\log\frac{n}{\epsilon}\log\frac{\ell}{\delta}

\displaystyle=O(1)\frac{d}{\epsilon^{4p+10}}(\log^{2}d)\left\lparen\log^{2}\frac{n}{\epsilon}\right\rparen\left\lparen\log\frac{\log d}{\delta\epsilon}\right\rparen

which is enough to union bound over the set
\mathcal{D}_{k}
(see Lemma
6.7
) of log size at most

O(1)\frac{d}{\epsilon^{4(1+p)}(1+\epsilon^{4})^{pk}}\log^{2}\frac{n}{\epsilon}

with failure probability at most
\delta/(\ell+1)
. We union bound over the
\ell+1
choices of
k
to obtain that

\left\lvert\left\lVert\tilde{\mathbf{y}}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}^{2}-\left\lVert\tilde{\mathbf{y}}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}^{2}\right\rvert\leq\epsilon w^{2}\rho^{2}.

Setting
\epsilon
in the above result to
\epsilon^{2}
, we get

\left\lvert\left\lVert\tilde{\mathbf{y}}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}^{2}-\left\lVert\tilde{\mathbf{y}}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}^{2}\right\rvert\leq\epsilon^{2}w^{2}\rho^{2}.

Now note that if both
\left\lVert\tilde{\mathbf{y}}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}
and
\left\lVert\tilde{\mathbf{y}}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}
are at most
\epsilon w\rho/2
, then we have that

\left\lvert\left\lVert\tilde{\mathbf{y}}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}-\left\lVert\tilde{\mathbf{y}}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}\right\rvert\leq\epsilon w\rho

by the triangle inequality. Otherwise, we have that

\left\lvert\left\lVert\tilde{\mathbf{y}}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}-\left\lVert\tilde{\mathbf{y}}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}\right\rvert=\frac{\left\lvert\left\lVert\tilde{\mathbf{y}}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}^{2}-\left\lVert\tilde{\mathbf{y}}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}^{2}\right\rvert}{\left\lvert\left\lVert\tilde{\mathbf{y}}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}+\left\lVert\tilde{\mathbf{y}}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}\right\rvert}\leq\frac{\epsilon^{2}w^{2}\rho^{2}}{\epsilon w\rho}=\epsilon w\rho.\qed

We can now apply the result twice, once on all of the rows and once on only the set of rows with small Huber sensitivity, in order to obtain a sampling bound for preserving a Huber sphere at a single scale.

Lemma 6.9
.

Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
and let
\mathcal{V}=\Span(\mathbf{A})
. Let
\epsilon\in(0,1)
be an accuracy parameter,
\delta>0
a failure rate parameter, and
\rho\geq 1
a Huber radius. Let
\beta=3-2\sqrt{2}
,
\gamma=n^{-\beta/(\beta+1)}
, and let
T
be a subset

T\subseteq\left\{i\in[n]:\mathbf{s}_{i}^{H}(\mathbf{A})\leq\gamma\right\}

Let
\kappa\coloneqq O((\gamma n)^{\beta})=O(\gamma^{-1})
. Let
\mathbf{w}\geq\mathbf{1}_{n}
be a set of weights such that

\frac{\max_{i\in[n]}\mathbf{w}_{i}}{\min_{i\in[n]}\mathbf{w}_{i}}\leq 2

and let
w=\min_{i\in[n]}\mathbf{w}_{i}
. Let
\mathbf{p}
be the sampling probabilities given by Lemma
6.8
for
\mathbf{A}
and let
\mathbf{q}
be the sampling probabilities given by Lemma
6.8
for
\mathbf{A}\mid_{T}
. We then consider sampling probabilities
\mathbf{r}
such that

\mathbf{r}_{i}\coloneqq\begin{cases}1&\text{if $i\in\overline{T}$}\\
\min\left\{\mathbf{p}_{i}+\mathbf{q}_{i},1\right\}&\text{if $i\in T$}\end{cases}

Let
\mathbf{w}^{\prime}\geq\mathbf{1}_{n}
be a set of weights chosen randomly so that

\mathbf{w}_{i}^{\prime}\coloneqq\begin{cases}\mathbf{w}_{i}/\mathbf{r}_{i}&\text{w.p. $\mathbf{r}_{i}$}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

Then,

\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}=\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}\pm\epsilon w\rho.

for all
\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{B}_{\rho}^{H}
with probability at least
1-\delta
. Furthermore,

\displaystyle\E\nnz(\mathbf{w}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=O(\kappa m\log n)=O\left\lparen\kappa d\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}\left\lparen\frac{\log n}{\epsilon}\right\rparen\log\frac{1}{\delta}\right\rparen

\displaystyle\nnz(\mathbf{w}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=O\left\lparen\E\nnz(\mathbf{w}^{\prime})\right\rparen

with probability at least
1-\delta
, and
\left\lVert\mathbf{w}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq O(n)\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{\infty}
.

Proof.

Note that for our choice of
\gamma
, we have that
\gamma=(\gamma n)^{-\beta}
. Then by our choice of
\kappa
and Corollary
6.3
, any vector
\mathbf{y}
either has at least one of

\min_{p\in\mathcal{I}}\frac{\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}}{\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H}^{2}}\leq\kappa\qquad\mbox{ or }\qquad\min_{p\in\mathcal{I}}\frac{\left\lVert\mathbf{y}|_{T}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}}{\left\lVert\mathbf{y}|_{T}\right\rVert_{H}^{2}}\leq\kappa.

(14)

Now let
\mathcal{N}
and
\mathcal{N}_{T}
denote the nets obtained by applying Lemma
6.8
on
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{A}|_{T}
, respectively, with radius
\rho
. Let
\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{B}_{\rho}^{H}
. Then, clearly,
\mathbf{y}
is within
\epsilon^{2}\rho
Huber distance of some
\tilde{\mathbf{y}}
belonging to either
\mathcal{N}
or
\mathcal{N}_{T}
, so
\mathcal{N}\cup\mathcal{N}_{T}
is an
\epsilon^{2}\rho
-cover of
\mathcal{B}_{\rho}^{H}
. Furthermore, by Lemma
6.8
,

\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}=\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}\pm\epsilon w\rho

for every
\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{N}\cup\mathcal{N}_{T}
with probability at least
1-2\delta
. Then by Lemma
C.11
we have that

\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}=\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}\pm O(\epsilon)w\rho

for all
\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{B}_{\rho}^{H}
, with probability at least
1-2\delta
.

Finally, letting
m
be as in the statement of Lemma
6.8
,

\displaystyle\E\nnz(\mathbf{w}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{p}_{i}+\mathbf{q}_{i}

\displaystyle\leq O(\kappa m)\left\lparen\sum_{p\in\mathcal{I}}\left[\frac{1}{n}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\mathbf{w}_{i}^{p}(\mathbf{A})}{d}\right]+\sum_{p\in\mathcal{I}}\left[\frac{1}{n}+\sum_{i\in T}\frac{\mathbf{w}_{i}^{p}(\mathbf{A}\mid_{T})}{d}\right]\right\rparen

\displaystyle=O(\kappa m\left\lvert\mathcal{I}\right\rvert)=O(\kappa m\log n)

since Lewis weights sum to
d
. Furthermore, by Bernstein’s inequality,

\nnz(\mathbf{w}^{\prime})=\Theta(\E\nnz(\mathbf{w}^{\prime}))

with probability at least
1-\delta
. By rescaling
\epsilon
and
\delta
by constant factors, we conclude.
∎

6.4.2
Sampling Guarantees for a Single Step

We now remove the assumption of bounded weights and fixed radius by union bounding over their various levels.

Lemma 6.10
.

Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
and let
\mathcal{V}=\Span(\mathbf{A})
. Let
\epsilon\in(0,1)
be an accuracy parameter,
\delta>0
a failure rate parameter. Let
\mathbf{w}\geq\mathbf{1}_{n}
be a set of weights and for each
j\in[\left\lceil\log_{2}(\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{\infty})\right\rceil+1]
define the sets

T_{j}\coloneqq\left\{i\in[n]:\mathbf{w}_{i}\in[2^{j-1},2^{j})\right\}.

Let
\mathbf{w}^{\prime}
be obtained by applying Lemma
6.9
on each
\mathbf{A}|_{T_{j}}
with weights
\mathbf{w}|_{T_{j}}
, with
\delta
set to

\Theta\left\lparen\frac{\delta}{\log(n\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{\infty}/\epsilon}\right\rparen.

Then, for all
\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{V}
,

\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}=(1\pm\epsilon)\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}.

Furthermore, for
\beta=3-2\sqrt{2}
and
\kappa=n^{\beta/(\beta+1)}
,

\displaystyle\E\nnz(\mathbf{w}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=O\left\lparen\kappa d\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}\left\lparen\frac{\log n}{\epsilon}\right\rparen\log\frac{\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{\infty}}{\delta}\right\rparen

\displaystyle\nnz(\mathbf{w}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=O\left\lparen\E\nnz(\mathbf{w}^{\prime})\right\rparen

with probability at least
1-\delta
.

Proof.

For any
\rho\geq 1
, by a union bound over the
\left\lceil\log_{2}\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{\infty}\right\rceil+1
weight classes
T_{j}
, the approximation guarantee from Lemma
6.9
holds for all classes with probability at least
\Theta(\delta/\log(n\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{\infty}/\epsilon))
. We then union bound over the
\Theta(\log(n\left\lVert\mathbf{w}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{\infty}/\epsilon))=\Theta(\log(n\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{\infty}/\epsilon))
radius levels
\rho
required by Lemma
6.5
so that

\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}=(1\pm\epsilon)\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}

for all
\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{V}
.
∎

We provide the algorithm for Lemma
6.10
Algorithm
10
, whose guarantee is proven in Theorem
6.11
.

Algorithm 10

Huber subspace embedding

input:
Matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, weights
\mathbf{w}
.

output:
Weights
\mathbf{w}^{\prime}
.

1:

\beta\leftarrow 3-2\sqrt{2}
,
\gamma\leftarrow n^{-\beta/(1+\beta)}
,
m\leftarrow d\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}((\log(n\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{\infty}/\delta))/\epsilon),W\leftarrow\left\lceil\log_{2}\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{\infty}\right\rceil+1

2:

T_{j}\leftarrow\left\{i\in[n]:\mathbf{w}_{i}\in[2^{j-1},2^{j})\right\}
for
j\in[W]

3:

\mathcal{I}\leftarrow\{1+(c/\log n)\cdot j:j\in[(\log n)/c]\cup\{0\}\}

4:

for

j\in[W]

do

5:

Compute approximate Huber sensitivities
\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{H}(\mathbf{A}|_{T_{j}})
with Algorithm
7
with
\tau=\gamma n

6:

S_{j}\leftarrow\{i\in[n]:\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{H}(\mathbf{A}|_{T_{j}})\leq\gamma\}

7:

Set
\mathbf{w}_{i}^{\prime}=\mathbf{w}_{i}
for every

Compute approximate Lewis weights
\tilde{\mathbf{w}}_{i}^{p}(\mathbf{A})
with Theorem
2.7
for every
p\in\mathcal{I}

Compute approximate Lewis weights
\tilde{\mathbf{w}}_{i}^{p}(\mathbf{A}|_{S_{j}\cap T_{j}})
with Theorem
2.7
for every
p\in\mathcal{I}

for

i\in S_{j}

do

11:

Let
\mathbf{p}_{i}=\min\{1,\gamma^{-1}m(1/n+\sum_{p\in\mathcal{I}}\tilde{\mathbf{w}}_{i}^{p}(\mathbf{A})/d)+\sum_{p\in\mathcal{I}}\tilde{\mathbf{w}}_{i}^{p}(\mathbf{A}\mid_{T})/d)\}
