Set
\mathbf{w}_{i}^{\prime}=\mathbf{w}_{i}/\mathbf{p}_{i}
with probability
\mathbf{p}_{i}
and
0
otherwise.

13:

end

for

14:

end

for

15:

return

\mathbf{w}^{\prime}

Theorem 6.11
.

Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
and let
\mathcal{V}=\Span(\mathbf{A})
. Let
\epsilon>0
and
\delta>0
. Let
\mathbf{w}\geq\mathbf{1}_{n}
be a set of weights. Let
\mathbf{w}^{\prime}
be the weights returned by Algorithm
10
. Then,

\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}^{2}=(1\pm O(\epsilon))\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H}^{2}

for all
\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{V}
and

\displaystyle\E\nnz(\mathbf{w}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=O\left\lparen\gamma^{-1}d\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}\left\lparen\frac{\log(n\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{\infty})}{\delta\epsilon}\right\rparen\right\rparen

\displaystyle\nnz(\mathbf{w}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=O\left\lparen\E\nnz(\mathbf{w}^{\prime})\right\rparen

with probability at least
1-\delta
. Furthermore, Algorithm
10
runs in time

O\left\lparen(\nnz(\mathbf{A})+d^{(1+1/\beta)\omega})(\log^{2}n)\log\frac{1}{\delta}\right\rparen.

Proof.

We first identify the rows with Huber sensitivity at least
\gamma
. By running Theorem
4.9

\log(1/\delta)
times with
\tau=\gamma n=n^{1/(\beta+1)}
, we can boost the success probability to
1-\delta
to find sensitivity upper bounds that sum to

O\left\lparen d(\log^{2}n)\log\frac{1}{\delta}\right\rparen

in time

O\left\lparen\left[\nnz(\mathbf{A})\log^{2}n+n^{1/(\beta+1)}T\log n\right]\log\frac{1}{\delta}\right\rparen.

for
T=O(d^{\omega}\log^{2}d)
by Theorem
2.7
. Note that in the above running time, the term inside the square brackets is
O(\nnz(\mathbf{A})\log^{2}n)
time for
T\leq n^{\beta/(\beta+1)}
and
O((\nnz(\mathbf{A})+T^{1+1/\beta})\log^{2}n)
time for
T\geq n^{\beta/(\beta+1)}
. Then, we may find a superset of rows with Huber sensitivity at least
\gamma
of size at most
O\left\lparen\gamma^{-1}d(\log^{2}n)\log\frac{1}{\delta}\right\rparen
. We may also approximate the
\ell_{p}
Lewis weights in time
O([\nnz(\mathbf{A})+T](\log n)\log\frac{1}{\delta})
by Theorem
2.7
, for each
p\in\mathcal{I}
. The guarantees on
\nnz(\mathbf{w})
follow from Lemma
6.9
.
∎

6.5
Subspace Embeddings via Recursive Sampling

For large
n
, a bound of
\tilde{O}(n^{\beta/(\beta+1)}d)
with
\beta=3-2\sqrt{2}
of Theorem
6.11
is much worse than the previous
O(d^{2}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}\log(n))
bound. However, by applying Theorem
6.11

after
reducing the number of rows to
O(d^{2}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}\log(n))
, we can obtain a sampling bound of roughly
O(d^{2\beta/(\beta+1)}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}\log(n))
, where
1+2\beta/(\beta+1)\approx 1.29289
. Furthermore, by recursively applying this procedure, we can improve the dependence on
d
to
d^{1+\beta}=d^{4-2\sqrt{2}}
after only
O(\log\log(d\log n))
many iterations, where
4-2\sqrt{2}\approx 1.17157
.

Note that as long as we apply this recursion at most
\log n
times, the number of rows decreases as
m\to O(m^{\beta/(\beta+1)}d\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}((\log n)/\epsilon))
at each iteration, since
\log\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{\infty}
increases by at most
O(1)
at each iteration. The closed form solution to this can be found via the following recurrence:

Lemma 6.12
.

Suppose that
(a_{i})_{i=0}^{\infty}
satisfies the recurrence

a_{i+1}=\lambda a_{i}+b

for some
b>0
and
\lambda\in(0,1)
. Then,

a_{i}=\frac{1}{1-\lambda}\left\lparen b-\lambda^{i}\left\lparen b-(1-\lambda)a_{0}\right\rparen\right\rparen

Proof.

Note that

\frac{1}{1-\lambda}\left\lparen b-\lambda^{0}\left\lparen b-(1-\lambda)a_{0}\right\rparen\right\rparen=\frac{1}{1-\lambda}\left\lparen b-b+(1-\lambda)a_{0}\right\rparen=a_{0}

and

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1-\lambda}\left\lparen b-\lambda^{i+1}\left\lparen b-(1-\lambda)a_{0}\right\rparen\right\rparen

\displaystyle=\lambda\cdot\frac{1}{1-\lambda}\left\lparen\frac{1-\lambda}{\lambda}b+b-\lambda^{i}\left\lparen b-(1-\lambda)a_{0}\right\rparen\right\rparen

\displaystyle=\lambda\frac{1}{1-\lambda}\left\lparen b-\lambda^{i}\left\lparen b-(1-\lambda)a_{0}\right\rparen\right\rparen+b.

∎

By applying the above lemma on the logarithm of the number of rows after the
i
th recursive application of row sampling, where
b
corresponds to
\log(d\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}((\log n)/\epsilon)))
, we obtain the following algorithm and theorem:

Algorithm 11

Recursive Huber subspace embedding

input:
Matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, weights
\mathbf{w}
(defaulted to
\mathbf{1}_{n}
).

output:
Weights
\mathbf{w}^{\prime\prime}
.

1:

\beta\leftarrow 3-2\sqrt{2}

2:

if

\nnz(\mathbf{w})\leq O(d^{1+\beta}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(\log(n/\delta)/\epsilon))

then

3:

return

\mathbf{w}

4:

end

if

5:

Obtain weights
\mathbf{w}^{\prime}
by running Algorithm
10
on
\mathbf{A}
with weights
\mathbf{w}

6:

Obtain weights
\mathbf{w}^{\prime\prime}
by recursively running Algorithm
11
on
\mathbf{A}
with weights
\mathbf{w}^{\prime}

7:

return

\mathbf{w}^{\prime\prime}

Theorem 6.13

(Huber Subspace Embedding)
.

There is an algorithm (Algorithm
11
) which, with probability
1-\delta
, computes a set of weights
\mathbf{w}\geq\mathbf{1}_{n}
with

\nnz(\mathbf{w})\leq O(d^{4-2\sqrt{2}}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(\log\frac{n}{\delta},\epsilon^{-1})

such that
\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}^{2}=(1\pm\epsilon)\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{H}^{2}
for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and runs in time

O\left\lparen\left[\nnz(\mathbf{A})(\log^{2}n)(\log\log n)+d^{\omega\cdot(1+1/\beta)}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}\left\lparen\log n,\epsilon^{-1}\right\rparen\right]\log\frac{1}{\delta}\right\rparen.

Proof.

We apply Lemma
6.11
for
O(\log\log n)
recursive steps with
\delta=O(1/\log\log n)
so that we have a sequence of upper bounds
m_{i}
on the number of rows such that, for
\beta=3-2\sqrt{2}
,

m_{i}=O\left\lparen m_{i-1}^{\beta/(\beta+1)}d\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}\left\lparen\frac{\log(n/\delta)}{\epsilon}\right\rparen\right\rparen.

Note then that
a_{i}\coloneqq\log m_{i}
satisfies

a_{i}=\frac{\beta}{\beta+1}a_{i-1}+b

for

b=\log\left[O\left\lparen d\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}\left\lparen\frac{\log(n/\delta)}{\epsilon}\right\rparen\right\rparen\right].

The closed form solution for this is given in Lemma
6.12
. Note that if
i
satisfies

\left\lparen\frac{\beta}{\beta+1}\right\rparen^{i}\left\lparen b+\frac{1}{\beta+1}a_{0}\right\rparen\leq\frac{1}{\log b}

then

a_{i}=(1+\beta)b\pm\frac{1}{\log b}

and thus the number of rows is at most

m_{i}=\exp(a_{i})=O(\exp(b)^{1+\beta})=O(d^{1+\beta}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}((\log n)/\epsilon))=O(d^{4-2\sqrt{2}}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}((\log n)/\epsilon)).

This only requires
i
at most
O(\log(b+a_{0}))=O(\log\log n)
iterations.
∎

6.5.1
Running Time Trade-offs

The above running time is quite a large polynomial in
d
; for the current matrix multiplication exponent of
\omega\approx 2.37286
, the exponent of
d
is

\omega\cdot\left\lparen 1+\frac{1}{\beta}\right\rparen\approx 16.20290.

However, by a further multi-step algorithm, we can further reduce the running time significantly, in a similar manner to Theorem
5.6
. First note that by always only computing sensitivities which sum to at most
d^{\omega}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}\log n
, we can achieve roughly
\nnz(\mathbf{A})+d^{\omega}
time to reduce the number of rows to roughly
n^{\prime}=d^{\omega(1+\beta)}
with the same proof, where we solve the recursion of reducing the dimension from
n
to
n^{\beta/(1+\beta)}d^{\omega}
at each step. We can then spend roughly
\nnz(\mathbf{A})+n^{\prime}d^{\omega}/d=\nnz(\mathbf{A})+d^{\omega(2+\beta)-1}
in Theorem
4.9
to fully reduce the dimension to
d^{1+\beta}
, where the exponent in
d
is now only

\omega\cdot\left\lparen 2+\beta\right\rparen-1\approx 4.14663.

This running time may still be undesirable, if one is willing to sacrifice in the dimension reduction bound; for example, this does not beat the running time
\nnz(\mathbf{A})+d^{4}
in
[
GPV21
]
, even though the dimension reduction bound is significantly better. If we wish for a running time that is strictly
\nnz(\mathbf{A})+d^{\omega}
, up to polylogarithmic factors, then the previous argument shows that we obtain a dimension reduction bound of

\omega\cdot(1+\beta)\approx 2.77998

rows, which indeed slightly improves over their bound of
d^{3}
rows in the same running time.

To find an intermediate trade-off by balancing the running time and row count, let
C\in[1,\omega]
be a parameter. Assume we have already reduced to
n=d^{\omega(1+\beta)}
rows in
\nnz(\mathbf{A})+d^{\omega}
time. Then, we can spend an additional

\frac{nd^{\omega}}{d^{C}}=d^{\omega(2+\beta)-C}

time to reduce to
d^{C(1+\beta)}
rows. One interesting choice is to balance the running time to be equal to
d
times the row count, which gives
C=\frac{\omega(2+\beta)-1}{2+\beta}\approx 1.91236
which allows for reduction to
d^{C}
rows in
d^{C+1}
time.

6.6
Active Regression Algorithms

As noted before, Theorem
6.13
gives constant factor solutions in the active regression setting, by Lemma
5.1
. We now discuss how to build on this result to obtain a relative error solution, using the more refined techniques of Section
3.2
.

Our main result of this section is the following:

See
1.5

Algorithm 12

We first obtain an analog of Lemmas
3.5
and
3.6
, mirroring some of the logic from Lemma
5.5
.

Lemma 6.14
.

Let
\mathbf{w}\geq\mathbf{1}_{n}
be a set of weights. Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
. Let
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be such that
\left\lVert\mathbf{z}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}\leq O(\mathsf{OPT})
, where

\mathsf{OPT}\coloneqq\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{z}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}.

Let
\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{H,\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{A})\geq\mathbf{s}_{i}^{H,\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{A})
be an upper bound on the Huber sensitivities. Let
\epsilon>0
and consider a subset

\mathcal{B}\subseteq\left\{i\in[n]:\mathbf{w}_{i}H(\mathbf{z}_{i})\geq\frac{\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{H,\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{A})}{\epsilon^{2}}\mathsf{OPT}^{2}\right\}

and let
\bar{\mathbf{z}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be equal to
\mathbf{z}
, but with all entries in
\mathcal{B}
set to
0
. Then, for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}\leq O(\mathsf{OPT})
,

\left\lvert\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}^{2}-\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{z}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}^{2}-\left\lVert\mathbf{z}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}^{2}\right\rvert=O(\epsilon)\mathsf{OPT}^{2}.

Proof.

For any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}\leq O(\mathsf{OPT})
. Then, for any
i\in\mathcal{B}
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{w}_{i}H([\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}](i))

\displaystyle\leq\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{H,\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{A})\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}^{2}

(15)

\displaystyle\leq\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{H,\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{A})O(\mathsf{OPT}^{2})

\displaystyle\leq O(\epsilon^{2})\cdot\mathbf{w}_{i}H(\mathbf{z}_{i}).

so

\displaystyle\mathbf{w}_{i}H([\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}](i)-\mathbf{z}(i))-\mathbf{w}_{i}H([\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}](i)-\bar{\mathbf{z}}(i))

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\mathbf{w}_{i}H([\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}](i)-\mathbf{z}(i))-\mathbf{w}_{i}H([\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}](i))

\displaystyle i\in\mathcal{B}

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\mathbf{w}_{i}\left\lparen H^{1/2}(\mathbf{z}(i))\pm H^{1/2}([\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}](i))\right\rparen^{2}-\mathbf{w}_{i}H([\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}](i))

see Lemma
C.4

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle(1\pm O(\epsilon))\mathbf{w}_{i}H(\mathbf{z}_{i})

Equation (
15
)

Thus,

\displaystyle\left\lvert\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{z}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}^{2}-\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}^{2}-\left\lVert\mathbf{z}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}^{2}\right\rvert

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\left\lvert\sum_{i\in\mathcal{B}}(1\pm O(\epsilon))\mathbf{w}_{i}H(\mathbf{z}_{i})-\mathbf{w}_{i}H(\mathbf{z}_{i})\right\rvert

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\left\lvert\sum_{i\in\mathcal{B}}O(\epsilon)\mathbf{w}_{i}H(\mathbf{z}_{i})\right\rvert

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle O(\epsilon)\left\lVert\mathbf{z}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}^{2}\leq O(\epsilon)\left\lVert\mathbf{z}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}^{2}\leq O(\epsilon)\mathsf{OPT}^{2}.\qed

Next, we show that our Huber subspace embedding algorithm approximately preserves Huber norms
\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}^{2}
for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}=O(\mathsf{OPT})
.

Lemma 6.15
.

Consider the setting of Lemma
6.14
. Suppose that
\mathbf{w}^{\prime}
is obtained by performing one step of Algorithm
10
(with the
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(\log(n\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{\infty}/\delta)/\epsilon)
term in
m
possibly being a larger polynomial). Then, there exists a
\mathcal{B}
satisfying the requirements of Lemma
6.14
(see Equation (
20
)) such that, with probability at least
1-\delta
, for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}=O(\mathsf{OPT})
,

\left\lvert\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}^{2}-\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}^{2}\right\rvert=O(\epsilon)\cdot\mathsf{OPT}^{2}.

(16)

Proof.

We will make a careful modification of our Huber rounding technique of Section
6.4
. To adapt the rounding to the active setting, we will need to modify the proof for the
[
BLM89
]
net construction rather than using them as a black box.

Note that we only need to provide Huber norm preservation guarantees for a single scale of Huber radius
\rho=O(\mathsf{OPT})
for this lemma, as opposed to
O(\log n)
scales for the Huber subspace embedding result. Furthermore, by Lemma
C.11
, it suffices to show the approximation guarantee for every
\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{N}\cup(\mathcal{N}-\bar{\mathbf{z}})
, where
\mathcal{N}
is an
O(\epsilon^{2}\rho)
-cover of
\mathcal{B}_{\rho}^{H,\mathbf{w}}
. We have already shown in Theorem
6.13
the preservation of
\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}^{2}
for every
\mathbf{y}
in
\mathcal{N}
, so we focus on showing the preservation of
\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}^{2}
for every
\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{N}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}
, which is similar but requires a slightly more involved argument to handle the
\bar{\mathbf{z}}
translation.

We start off with the following claim, which is the active version of Lemma
6.7
.

Claim 6.16
.

Let
\mathcal{I}\subset[1,2]
be the set in the statement of Corollary
6.3
. Let
\kappa\geq 1
be a distortion parameter. Suppose that
\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{H}(\mathbf{A})\geq\mathbf{s}_{i}^{H}(\mathbf{A})
is an upper bound on the Huber sensitivities such that

\kappa\mathbf{w}_{i}^{p}(\mathbf{A})\leq\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{H}(\mathbf{A})

(17)

for every
p\in\mathcal{I}
. Let
\Delta>0
be a scale parameter and let
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a target vector satisfying

H(\mathbf{v}(i))\leq\frac{\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{H}(\mathbf{A})}{\epsilon^{4}}\Delta^{2}

for each
i\in[n]
.
Let
\mathbf{r}=\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{v}\in(\mathcal{N}-\mathbf{v})
for for
\mathcal{N}
an
O(\epsilon^{2}\rho)
-cover of
\mathcal{B}_{\rho}^{H}
for
\rho=O(\Delta)
. Suppose that
\mathbf{y}
satisfies

\frac{\min_{p\in\mathcal{I}}\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}}{\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H}^{2}}\leq\kappa.

Then, there exists a
p\in\mathcal{I}
,
\ell=O((\log d)/\epsilon^{2})
, and a rounding
\mathbf{r}^{\prime}=\mathbf{e}+\sum_{k=0}^{\ell}\mathbf{d}_{k}
such that:

•

\left\lVert\mathbf{r}^{\prime}-\mathbf{r}\right\rVert_{H}\leq O(\epsilon)\Delta

•

H\left\lparen\left\lvert\mathbf{d}_{k}(i)\right\rvert\right\rparen\leq O(1)\frac{\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{H}(\mathbf{A})\cdot(1+\epsilon^{2})^{pk}}{\epsilon^{4}\cdot d}\Delta^{2}

•

\mathbf{e},\mathbf{d}_{0},\mathbf{d}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{d}_{\ell}
have disjoint supports

•

\mathbf{e}
is a single fixed vector with
H(\lvert\mathbf{e}(i)\rvert)\leq O(1)\frac{\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{H}(\mathbf{A})}{\epsilon^{4}\cdot d}\Delta^{2}

•

each
\mathbf{d}_{k}
is drawn from a set of vectors
\mathcal{D}_{k}
with size at most

\log\left\lvert\mathcal{D}_{k}\right\rvert\leq O(1)\frac{d}{\epsilon^{2(1+p)}(1+\epsilon^{2})^{pk}}\log\frac{n}{\epsilon}.

Proof.

Let
p\in\mathcal{I}
be the
p
achieving the minimum in Corollary
6.3
for
\mathbf{y}
. We will now define
\ell
nets
\mathcal{N}_{k}
for
k\in[\ell]
as follows. Recall the following result from
[
BLM89
]
and
[
SZ01
]
:

Lemma 6.17
.

Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
and
1\leq p<\infty
. Let
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
be the diagonal matrix with

\mathbf{W}_{i,i}\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}\left\lparen\frac{\mathbf{w}_{i}^{p}(\mathbf{A})}{d}+\frac{1}{n}.\right\rparen

Let
\mathcal{B}_{p}\coloneqq\left\{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}:\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{p}\leq 1\right\}
. Then for any
t>0
, there is a net
\mathcal{N}_{\infty}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that, for any
\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{B}_{p}
, there exists a
\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{N}_{\infty}
with
\left\lVert\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq t
and

\log\left\lvert\mathcal{N}_{\infty}\right\rvert\leq c(p)\frac{d}{t^{2\land p}}\log n

where
c(p)
is a constant depending only on
p

[
BLM89
, Corollary 4.7 and Proposition 7.2]
.

We use the following definition of a sequence of nets from
[
BLM89
]
.

Claim 6.18

(Index sets
[
BLM89
]
)
.

Let
\epsilon^{\prime}>0
. For
k\in[\ell]
, let
\mathcal{N}_{k}
be the net from Lemma
6.17
with
t=\epsilon^{\prime}(1+\epsilon^{\prime})^{k}/3
. If
t\leq 1
, we take
\mathcal{N}_{k}=\mathcal{N}
. Now for each
\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{N}
and
k\in[\ell]
, let
\mathbf{f}_{k,\mathbf{y}}\in\mathcal{N}_{k}
satisfy

\left\lVert\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}(\mathbf{f}_{k,\mathbf{y}}-\mathbf{y})\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\epsilon^{\prime}(1+\epsilon^{\prime})^{k}/3

as given by Lemma
6.17
. Define the index sets

\displaystyle C_{k,\mathbf{y}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{i\in[n]:|\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{f}_{k,\mathbf{y}}(i)|\geq(1+\epsilon^{\prime})^{k-1}\right\}

\displaystyle D_{k,\mathbf{y}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq C_{k,\mathbf{y}}\setminus\bigcup_{k^{\prime}>k}C_{k^{\prime},\mathbf{y}}

\displaystyle D_{0,\mathbf{y}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq[n]\setminus\bigcup_{k\geq 1}C_{k,\mathbf{y}}

Then, for each
k
we have
\log|\mathcal{N}_{k}|=O\left(\frac{d\log n}{(\epsilon^{\prime}(1+\epsilon^{\prime})^{k})^{2\land p}}\right)
, and for every
i\in D_{k,\mathbf{y}}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbf{w}_{i}^{p}(\mathbf{A})^{1/p}\cdot(1+\epsilon^{\prime})^{k-2}}{d^{1/p}}\leq|\mathbf{y}(i)|\leq\frac{\mathbf{w}_{i}^{p}(\mathbf{A})^{1/p}\cdot(1+\epsilon^{\prime})^{k+2}}{d^{1/p}}.

(18)

Proof.

Note that since
\lVert\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}(\mathbf{f}_{k,\mathbf{y}}-\mathbf{y})\rVert_{\infty}\leq\epsilon^{\prime}(1+\epsilon^{\prime})^{k}/3
, if
i\in C_{k,\mathbf{y}}
for
1\leq k\leq\ell
,

\displaystyle|\mathbf{y}(i)|

\displaystyle\geq|\mathbf{f}_{k,\mathbf{y}}(i)|-\frac{\mathbf{w}^{p}_{i}(\mathbf{A})^{1/p}\cdot\epsilon^{\prime}(1+\epsilon^{\prime})^{k}/3}{d^{1/p}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\mathbf{w}^{p}_{i}(\mathbf{A})^{1/p}}{d^{1/p}}\cdot\left[(1+\epsilon^{\prime})^{k-1}-\epsilon^{\prime}(1+\epsilon^{\prime})^{k}/3\right]

(By definition, if
i\in C_{k,\mathbf{y}}
,
|\mathbf{f}_{k,\mathbf{y}}(i)|\geq\frac{\mathbf{w}^{p}_{i}(\mathbf{A})^{1/p}\cdot(1+\epsilon^{\prime})^{k-1}}{d^{1/p}}
.)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\mathbf{w}^{p}_{i}(\mathbf{A})^{1/p}\cdot(1+\epsilon^{\prime})^{k-2}}{d^{1/p}}.

If
i\notin C_{k,\mathbf{y}}
we analogously have
|\mathbf{y}(i)|\leq\frac{\mathbf{w}_{i}^{p}(\mathbf{A})^{1/p}\cdot(1+\epsilon^{\prime})^{k+2}}{d^{1/p}}
.
Thus, for
i\in D_{k,\mathbf{y}}=C_{k,\mathbf{y}}\setminus\bigcup_{k^{\prime}>k}C_{k^{\prime},\mathbf{y}}
for
1\leq k<\ell
, Equation (
18
) holds. Equation (
18
) also holds for
k=\ell
via Lemma
2.5
– since
\lVert\mathbf{y}\rVert_{p}\leq 1
,
|\mathbf{y}(i)|\leq d^{\max(0,1/2-1/p)}\cdot\mathbf{w}_{i}^{p}(\mathbf{A})^{1/p}
for all
i
. And so vacuously, since for
\ell=\log_{1+\epsilon^{\prime}}d^{\max(1/p,1/2)}
,
(1+\epsilon^{\prime})^{\ell+2}\geq d^{\max(1/p,1/2)}
, we have
|\mathbf{y}(i)|\leq\frac{\mathbf{w}_{i}^{p}(\mathbf{A})^{1/p}\cdot(1+\epsilon^{\prime})^{\ell+2}}{d^{1/p}}
.
∎

We now apply Claim
6.18
with
\epsilon^{\prime}=\epsilon^{2}
to obtain the nets
\mathcal{N}_{k}
. We then define index sets for the target vector
\mathbf{v}
as follows:

Claim 6.19

(Huber index sets for
\mathbf{v}
)
.

Define the following sets:

\displaystyle B_{k,\mathbf{y}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{i\in D_{k,\mathbf{y}}:H(\mathbf{v}(i))\leq\frac{\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{H}(\mathbf{A})\cdot(1+\epsilon^{2})^{p(k+2)}}{\epsilon^{4}\cdot d}\Delta^{2}\right\}

(
k\in[\ell]\cup\{0\}
)

\displaystyle H_{k}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{i\in[n]:\frac{\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{H}(\mathbf{A})\cdot(1+\epsilon^{2})^{p(k+1)}}{\epsilon^{4}\cdot d}\Delta^{2}<H(\mathbf{v}(i))\leq\frac{\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{H}(\mathbf{A})\cdot(1+\epsilon^{2})^{p(k+2)}}{\epsilon^{4}\cdot d}\Delta^{2}\right\}

(
k\in[\ell]
)

\displaystyle H_{B}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{i\in[n]:H(\mathbf{v}(i))>\frac{\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{H}(\mathbf{A})\cdot(1+\epsilon^{2})^{p(\ell+2)}}{\epsilon^{4}\cdot d}\Delta^{2}\right\}

\displaystyle G_{k,\mathbf{y}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq H_{k}\setminus\bigcup_{k^{\prime}\geq k}C_{k^{\prime},\mathbf{y}}

(
k\in[\ell]
)

Then,
B_{0,\mathbf{y}},\ldots,B_{\ell,\mathbf{y}},G_{1,\mathbf{y}},\ldots,G_{\ell,\mathbf{y}},H_{B}
form a partition of
[n]
.

Proof.

Define
H_{0}=\{i:H(\mathbf{v}(i))\leq\frac{\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{H}(\mathbf{A})\cdot(1+\epsilon^{2})^{2p}}{\epsilon^{4}\cdot d}\Delta^{2}\}
, so that
H_{B}\cup\bigcup_{k=0}^{\ell}H_{k}=[n]
is a partition the range of all possible
H(\mathbf{v}(i))
values. Furthermore, every
i\in H_{0}
lies in some
B_{k,\mathbf{y}}
.

At a high level, our argument will be as follows.
H_{k}
can be furthered partitioned into the indices that lie in some
\bigcup_{k^{\prime}\geq k}C_{k,\mathbf{y}}
, or those that are not in any
\bigcup_{k^{\prime}\geq k}C_{k^{\prime},\mathbf{y}}
. Those that lie in some
\bigcup_{k^{\prime}\geq k}C_{k,\mathbf{y}}
are then regrouped into the
B_{k,\mathbf{y}}
, which is also a valid partition by definition of the
D_{k,\mathbf{y}}
.

Any
i\in H_{k}\cap\left(\bigcup_{k^{\prime}\geq k}C_{k^{\prime},\mathbf{y}}\right)
has
H(\mathbf{v}(i))\leq\frac{\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{H}(\mathbf{A})\cdot(1+\epsilon^{2})^{p(k+2)}}{\epsilon^{4}\cdot d}\Delta^{2}
and thus must lie in
B_{k^{\prime},\mathbf{y}}
for some
k^{\prime}\geq k
. Thus,
G_{k,\mathbf{y}}\cup(\bigcup_{k^{\prime}\geq k}B_{k^{\prime},\mathbf{y}})\supseteq H_{k}
and so overall
(\bigcup_{k=1}^{\ell}G_{k,\mathbf{y}}\cup B_{k,\mathbf{y}})\cup B_{0,\mathbf{y}}\cup H_{B}=[n]
.

Now clearly, the
H_{k}
and in turn the
G_{k,\mathbf{y}}
are disjoint.
H_{B}
is disjoint from all
H_{k}
and hence all
G_{k,\mathbf{y}}
. It is also disjoint from all
B_{k,\mathbf{y}}
since the
H(|\mathbf{v}(i)|)
values in
H_{B}
are too large to be assigned to
B_{k,\mathbf{y}}
, even for
k=\ell
. The
D_{k,\mathbf{y}}
, and in turn the
B_{k,\mathbf{y}}
, are also disjoint by construction. Further,
\bigcup_{k^{\prime}\geq k}B_{k^{\prime},\mathbf{y}}\subseteq\bigcup_{k^{\prime}\geq k}D_{k^{\prime},\mathbf{y}}=\bigcup_{k^{\prime}\geq k}C_{k^{\prime},\mathbf{y}}
, and thus
G_{k,\mathbf{y}}
is disjoint from
B_{k^{\prime},\mathbf{y}}
for all
k^{\prime}\geq k
. Also, for
k\geq 1
, being in
G_{k,\mathbf{y}}
requires
H(|\mathbf{v}(i)|)>\frac{\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{H}(\mathbf{A})\cdot(1+\epsilon^{2})^{p(k+1)}}{\epsilon^{4}\cdot d}\Delta^{2}
, while being in
B_{k^{\prime},\mathbf{y}}
for
k^{\prime}<k
requires
H(\mathbf{v}(i))\leq\frac{\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{H}(\mathbf{A})\cdot(1+\epsilon^{2})^{p(k+1)}}{\epsilon^{4}\cdot d}\Delta^{2}
. Thus
G_{k,\mathbf{y}}
is disjoint from
B_{k^{\prime},\mathbf{y}}
for all
k^{\prime}<k
. Overall,
G_{k,\mathbf{y}}
is disjoint from all
B_{k^{\prime},\mathbf{y}}
. Thus,
B_{0,\mathbf{y}},\ldots,B_{\ell,\mathbf{y}},G_{1,\mathbf{y}},\ldots,G_{\ell,\mathbf{y}},H_{B}
are all mutually disjoint and partition
[n]
.
∎

Using the index sets of Claim
6.19
, we next show how to round
\mathbf{r}=\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{v}
to a nearby
\mathbf{r}^{\prime}
using the partition of
[n]
defined above.

Claim 6.20

(
\ell_{\infty}
Error Bound)
.

Define the vectors
\mathbf{r}^{\prime}
,
\mathbf{e}
, and
\mathbf{d}_{k}
with

\mathbf{r}^{\prime}=\mathbf{e}+\sum_{k=0}^{\ell}\mathbf{d}_{k}

as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbf{d}_{0}(i)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbf{y}(i)-\mathbf{v}(i)

\displaystyle i\in B_{0,\mathbf{y}}

(19)

\displaystyle\mathbf{d}_{k}(i)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{(1+\epsilon^{2})^{k}\cdot\mathbf{w}_{i}^{p}(\mathbf{A})^{1/p}}{d^{1/p}}-\mathbf{v}(i)

\displaystyle k\in[\ell],i\in B_{{k,\mathbf{y}}}

\displaystyle\mathbf{d}_{k}(i)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbf{v}(i)

\displaystyle i\in G_{k,\mathbf{y}}

\displaystyle\mathbf{d}_{k}(i)

\displaystyle\coloneqq 0

\displaystyle\text{otherwise}

\displaystyle\mathbf{e}_{i}(i)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbf{v}(i)

\displaystyle i\in H_{B}

\displaystyle\mathbf{e}_{i}(i)

\displaystyle\coloneqq 0

\displaystyle\text{otherwise}

Then

|\mathbf{r}(i)-\mathbf{r}^{\prime}(i)|\leq\epsilon^{2}(|\mathbf{y}(i)|+|\mathbf{v}(i)|).

Proof.

For
i\in B_{0,\mathbf{y}}
we have
\mathbf{r}(i)=\mathbf{r}^{\prime}(i)
so the claim trivially holds. For
i\in B_{k,\mathbf{y}}
for
k\geq 1
, by (
18
),

\left|\mathbf{y}(i)-\frac{(1+\epsilon^{2})^{k}\cdot\mathbf{w}_{i}^{p}(\mathbf{A})^{1/p}}{d^{1/p}}\right|\leq O(\epsilon^{2})\cdot|\mathbf{y}(i)|.

Thus by triangle inequality,
|\mathbf{r}(i)-\mathbf{r}^{\prime}(i)|\leq O(\epsilon^{2})\cdot|\mathbf{y}(i)|
. So the claim holds after adjusting constants on
\epsilon
. Finally, for
i\in G_{k,\mathbf{y}}
we have

\displaystyle H(\left\lvert\mathbf{y}(i)\right\rvert)

\displaystyle\leq\left\lvert\mathbf{y}(i)\right\rvert^{p}

(
p\in[1,2]
)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathbf{w}_{i}^{p}(\mathbf{A})\cdot(1+\epsilon^{2})^{p(k+2)}}{d}\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}

(
i\notin\bigcup_{k^{\prime}\geq k}C_{k^{\prime},\mathbf{y}}
)

\displaystyle\leq\kappa\frac{\mathbf{w}_{i}^{p}(\mathbf{A})\cdot(1+\epsilon^{2})^{p(k+2)}}{d}\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{H}(\mathbf{A})\cdot(1+\epsilon^{2})^{p(k+2)}}{d}\cdot O(\Delta^{2})

(Equation (
17
))

\displaystyle\leq O(\epsilon^{4})H(\mathbf{v}(i))

(
i\in H_{k}
)

and similarly for
i\in H_{B}
.
∎

We then have that

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}H(\left\lvert\mathbf{r}^{\prime}(i)-\mathbf{r}(i)\right\rvert)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}H(\epsilon^{2}(\lvert\mathbf{y}(i)\rvert+\left\lvert\mathbf{v}(i)\right\rvert))

(monotonicity)

\displaystyle\leq O(\epsilon^{2})\sum_{i=1}^{n}H(\lvert\mathbf{y}(i)\rvert+\left\lvert\mathbf{v}(i)\right\rvert)

(at least linear growth)

\displaystyle=O(\epsilon^{2})(\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H}+\left\lVert\mathbf{v}\right\rVert_{H})^{2}=O(\epsilon^{2})\Delta^{2}

(triangle inequality)

so
\left\lVert\mathbf{r}^{\prime}-\mathbf{r}\right\rVert_{H}\leq O(\epsilon)\Delta
. The disjointness of the
\mathbf{e}
and
\mathbf{d}_{k}
as well as the net size bounds carry over directly. Finally, the bounds on the coordinates of
\mathbf{d}_{k}
follow from

\displaystyle H(\lvert\mathbf{d}_{k}(i)\rvert)

\displaystyle\leq\left\lvert\mathbf{d}_{k}(i)\right\rvert^{p}

(
p\in[1,2]
)

\displaystyle\leq O(1)(\lvert\mathbf{y}(i)\rvert^{p}+\lvert\mathbf{v}(i)\rvert^{p})

\displaystyle\leq O(1)\left[\frac{\mathbf{w}_{i}^{p}(\mathbf{A})}{d}(1+\epsilon^{2})^{pk}\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}+\frac{\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{H}(\mathbf{A})\cdot(1+\epsilon^{2})^{p(k+2)}}{\epsilon^{4}\cdot d}\Delta^{2}\right]

(
i\in B_{k,\mathbf{y}}
)

\displaystyle\leq O(1)\left[\kappa\frac{\mathbf{w}_{i}^{p}(\mathbf{A})}{d}(1+\epsilon^{2})^{pk}\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H}^{2}+\frac{\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{H}(\mathbf{A})\cdot(1+\epsilon^{2})^{p(k+2)}}{\epsilon^{4}\cdot d}\Delta^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=O(1)\frac{\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{H}(\mathbf{A})\cdot(1+\epsilon^{2})^{pk}}{\epsilon^{4}\cdot d}\Delta^{2}

for
i\in B_{k,\mathbf{y}}
, and similarly for other cases as well as for
\mathbf{e}
.

This completes the proof of Claim
6.16
.
∎

We return to proving Lemma
6.15
. Recall that we reduced our task to proving the approximation guarantee Equation (
16
) for all vectors
\mathbf{r}^{\prime}
belonging to an
O(\epsilon^{2}\rho)
-cover over the set
\mathcal{B}_{\rho}^{H}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}
. We will do so using the cover given by Claim
6.16
, with
\epsilon
set to
\epsilon^{2}
. Furthermore, we will set the Huber sensitivity upper bounds
\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{H}(\mathbf{A})
to be at least
\kappa\sum_{p\in\mathcal{I}}\tilde{\mathbf{w}}_{i}^{p}(\mathbf{A})
. This indeed satisfies the hypothesis (
17
) of Claim
6.16
. Then, performing a Bernstein bound gives the active version of Lemma
6.8
.

Claim 6.21
.

Consider the setting of Claim
6.16
. Let
m=d\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}((\log(n/\delta))/\epsilon)
. Let
\mathbf{w}\geq\mathbf{1}_{n}
be a set of weights such that

\frac{\max_{i\in[n]}\mathbf{w}_{i}}{\min_{i\in[n]}\mathbf{w}_{i}}\leq 2

and let
w=\min_{i\in[n]}\mathbf{w}_{i}
. Let
\mathbf{w}^{\prime}
be chosen randomly so that

\mathbf{w}_{i}^{\prime}\coloneqq\begin{cases}\mathbf{w}_{i}/\mathbf{p}_{i}&\text{w.p. $\mathbf{p}_{i}$}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

where

\mathbf{p}_{i}\geq\min\left\{1,m\cdot\frac{\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{H}(\mathbf{A})}{d}\right\}.

Let

S_{\kappa}\coloneqq\left\{\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{B}_{\rho}^{H}-\mathbf{v}:\frac{\min_{p\in\mathcal{I}}\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}}{\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H}^{2}}\leq\kappa\right\}

Then with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}=(1\pm\epsilon)\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H}

for all
\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{N}\cap S_{\kappa}
, where
\mathcal{N}
is an
\epsilon^{2}\rho
-net over
\mathcal{B}_{\rho}^{H}-\mathbf{v}
given by Claim
6.16
, by setting
\epsilon
in the lemma to
\epsilon^{2}
.

Proof.

The proof follows that of Lemma
6.8
almost exactly, with minimal changes. The only necessary change is that the coordinate upper bounds of Claim
6.16
are now proportional to
\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{H}(\mathbf{A})
rather than
\mathbf{w}_{i}^{p}(\mathbf{A})
. However, this is not a problem, since we are sampling with these probabilities, and thus does not change the proof. Note also that the sensitivity upper bounds incur extra
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(\epsilon)
factors, which changes the
m
by
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(\epsilon)
factors.
∎

Next, we use Claim
6.21
to obtain an active version of Lemma
6.9
.

Claim 6.22
.

Consider the setting of Claim
6.21
. Let
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
\left\lVert\mathbf{z}\right\rVert_{H}=O(\Delta)
. Let
\gamma
be as defined in Lemma
6.9
and let
\kappa=1/\gamma
. Let
\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{H}(\mathbf{A})
be a Huber sensitivity upper bound and let

T\subseteq\left\{i\in[n]:\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{H}(\mathbf{A})\leq\gamma\right\}.

We now define a second set of Huber sensitivity upper bounds
\overline{\mathbf{s}_{i}}^{H}(\mathbf{A})
via

\overline{\mathbf{s}_{i}}^{H}(\mathbf{A})\coloneqq\begin{cases}1&\text{if $i\in\overline{T}$}\\
\kappa\sum_{p\in\mathcal{I}}\tilde{\mathbf{w}}_{i}^{p}(\mathbf{A})+\tilde{\mathbf{w}}_{i}^{p}(\mathbf{A}|_{T})&\text{if $i\in T$}\end{cases}

Then consider a superset

\mathcal{B}\supseteq\left\{i\in[n]:H(\mathbf{z}_{i})\geq\frac{\overline{\mathbf{s}_{i}}^{H}(\mathbf{A})}{\epsilon^{4}}\Delta^{2}\right\}

and let
\bar{\mathbf{z}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be equal to
\mathbf{z}
, but with all entries in
\mathcal{B}
set to
0
. Let
\mathbf{p}
be the sampling probabilities given by Lemma
6.8
for
\mathbf{A}
and let
\mathbf{q}
be the sampling probabilities given by Claim
6.21
for
\mathbf{A}\mid_{T}
. We then consider sampling probabilities
\mathbf{r}
such that

\mathbf{r}_{i}\coloneqq\begin{cases}1&\text{if $i\in\overline{T}$}\\
\min\left\{\mathbf{p}_{i}+\mathbf{q}_{i},1\right\}&\text{if $i\in T$}\end{cases}

Let
\mathbf{w}\geq\mathbf{1}_{n}
be a set of weights such that

\frac{\max_{i\in[n]}\mathbf{w}_{i}}{\min_{i\in[n]}\mathbf{w}_{i}}\leq 2

and let
w=\min_{i\in[n]}\mathbf{w}_{i}
. Let
\mathbf{w}^{\prime}
be chosen randomly so that

\mathbf{w}_{i}^{\prime}\coloneqq\begin{cases}\mathbf{w}_{i}/\mathbf{r}_{i}&\text{w.p. $\mathbf{r}_{i}$}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

Then,

\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}=\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}\pm\epsilon w\rho.

for all
\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{B}_{\rho}^{H}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}
with probability at least
1-\delta
. Furthermore,

\displaystyle\E\nnz(\mathbf{w}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=O(\kappa m\log n)=O\left\lparen\kappa d\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}\left\lparen\frac{\log n}{\epsilon}\right\rparen\log\frac{1}{\delta}\right\rparen

\displaystyle\nnz(\mathbf{w}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=O\left\lparen\E\nnz(\mathbf{w}^{\prime})\right\rparen

with probability at least
1-\delta
, and
\left\lVert\mathbf{w}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq O(n)\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{\infty}
.

Proof.

We may check that
\bar{\mathbf{z}}
satisfies the hypothesis for
\mathbf{v}
in Claim
6.16
, both for
\mathbf{A}
and for
\mathbf{A}|_{T}
. After noticing this fact, we simply follow the proof of Lemma
6.9
, combined with Claim
6.21
.
∎

Finally, we adapt Lemma
6.10
to the following claim:

Claim 6.23
.

Let
\mathbf{w}\geq\mathbf{1}_{n}
be a set of weights and for each
j\in[\left\lceil\log_{2}(\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{\infty})\right\rceil+1]
define the sets

T_{j}\coloneqq\left\{i\in[n]:\mathbf{w}_{i}\in[2^{j-1},2^{j})\right\}.

Let
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
\left\lVert\mathbf{z}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}=O(\mathsf{OPT})
. Now define a set of weighted Huber sensitivity upper bounds
\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{H,\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{A})
via

\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{H,\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{A})\coloneqq 2\cdot\overline{\mathbf{s}_{i}}^{H}(\mathbf{A}|_{T_{j}})

for each
i\in T_{j}
, where
\overline{\mathbf{s}_{i}}^{H}(\mathbf{A}|_{T_{j}})
is as defined in Claim
6.22
. Then consider the set

\mathcal{B}=\left\{i\in[n]:\mathbf{w}_{i}H(\mathbf{z}_{i})\geq\frac{\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{H,\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{A})}{\epsilon^{4}}\mathsf{OPT}^{2}\right\}

(20)

and let
\bar{\mathbf{z}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be equal to
\mathbf{z}
, but with all entries in
\mathcal{B}
set to
0
. Let
\mathbf{w}^{\prime}
be obtained by applying Claim
6.22
on each
\mathbf{A}|_{T_{j}}
with weights
\mathbf{w}|_{T_{j}}
. Then,

\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}=\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}\pm\epsilon\cdot\mathsf{OPT}

for all
\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{B}_{\rho}^{H}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}
with probability at least
1-\delta
. Furthermore, for
\beta=3-2\sqrt{2}
and
\kappa=n^{\beta/(\beta+1)}
,

\displaystyle\E\nnz(\mathbf{w}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=O\left\lparen\kappa d\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}\left\lparen\frac{\log(n\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{\infty}/\delta)}{\epsilon}\right\rparen\right\rparen

\displaystyle\nnz(\mathbf{w}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=O\left\lparen\E\nnz(\mathbf{w}^{\prime})\right\rparen

with probability at least
1-\delta
.

Proof.

First note that
\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{H,\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{A})
are valid sensitivity upper bounds, since for any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\frac{\mathbf{w}_{i}H([\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}](i))}{\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}^{2}}\leq\frac{\mathbf{w}_{i}H([\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}](i))}{2^{j-1}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}|_{T_{j}}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{H}^{2}}\leq 2\frac{H([\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}](i))}{\left\lVert\mathbf{A}|_{T_{j}}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{H}^{2}}\leq 2\cdot\overline{\mathbf{s}_{i}}^{H}(\mathbf{A}|_{T_{j}}).

Since
\mathbf{w}_{i}\geq 1
, we have that

\left\{i\in T_{j}:H(\mathbf{z}_{i})\geq\frac{\overline{\mathbf{s}_{i}}^{H}(\mathbf{A}|_{T_{j}})}{\epsilon^{4}}\mathsf{OPT}^{2}\right\}\subseteq\mathcal{B}

for each
j
. Thus, restricting
\bar{\mathbf{z}}
to
T_{j}
satisfies the hypotheses of Claim
6.22
. We then set
\Delta=\mathsf{OPT}/2^{j-1}
and apply Claim
6.22
so that for all
\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{B}_{\rho}^{H,\mathbf{w}}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}
with
\rho=O(\mathsf{OPT})
,

\left\lVert\mathbf{y}|_{T_{j}}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}=\left\lVert\mathbf{y}|_{T_{j}}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}\pm\epsilon\cdot\mathsf{OPT}

or

\left\lVert\mathbf{y}|_{T_{j}}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}^{2}=\left\lVert\mathbf{y}|_{T_{j}}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}^{2}+\epsilon^{2}\cdot\mathsf{OPT}^{2}\pm 2\epsilon\cdot\mathsf{OPT}\left\lVert\mathbf{y}|_{T_{j}}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}.

Union bounding and summing over the
j
gives us that

\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}^{2}=\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}^{2}\pm\Theta(\epsilon\log\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{\infty})\mathsf{OPT}^{2}

with probability at least
1-\Theta(\delta\log\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{\infty})
. Rescaling
\epsilon
and
\delta
by
\Theta(\log\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{\infty})
gives the claim.
∎

This completes the proof of Lemma
6.15
.
∎

Given Lemma
6.15
, we now prove the active regression guarantees for a single step of the recursion.

Lemma 6.24
.

Consider the setting of Lemma
6.15
. Let
\bar{\mathbf{x}}
satisfy

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\bar{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{z}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}\leq(1+\epsilon)\min_{\mathbf{x}}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{z}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}.

Then, with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\bar{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{z}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}\leq(1+O(\epsilon/\delta))\min_{\mathbf{x}}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{z}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}.

Proof.

We follow the proof of Theorem
3.4
. By Markov’s inequality, we have that

\left\lVert\mathbf{z}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}^{2}=O(1/\delta)\left\lVert\mathbf{z}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}^{2}

with probability at least
1-\delta
. Let
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}
have
\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}=O(\mathsf{OPT})
. Let
\bar{\mathbf{z}}
be as defined in Lemma
6.15
.
Lemma
6.14
, we then have that

\displaystyle\left\lvert\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}^{2}-\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{z}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}^{2}-\left\lVert\mathbf{z}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}^{2}\right\rvert=O(\epsilon)\mathsf{OPT}^{2}

\displaystyle\left\lvert\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}^{2}-\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{z}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}^{2}-\left\lVert\mathbf{z}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}^{2}\right\rvert=O(\epsilon/\delta)\mathsf{OPT}^{2}.

Furthermore, Lemma
6.15
guarantees that

\left\lvert\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}^{2}-\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}^{2}\right\rvert=O(\epsilon)\cdot\mathsf{OPT}^{2}.

Then by the triangle inequality,

\left\lvert\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{z}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}^{2}-\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{z}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}^{2}+(\left\lVert\mathbf{z}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}^{2}-\left\lVert\mathbf{z}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}^{2})\right\rvert\leq O(\epsilon/\delta)\mathsf{OPT}^{2}.

Let
C\coloneqq\left\lVert\mathbf{z}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}^{2}-\left\lVert\mathbf{z}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}^{2}
. Note that
\left\lvert C\right\rvert\leq O(1/\delta)\mathsf{OPT}^{2}
. Let
\mathbf{x}^{*}\coloneqq\arg\min_{\mathbf{x}}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{z}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}
. Then,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\bar{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{z}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\bar{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{z}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}^{2}+C+O(\epsilon/\delta)\mathsf{OPT}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(1+\epsilon)\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}-\mathbf{z}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}^{2}+C+O(\epsilon/\delta)\mathsf{OPT}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(1+\epsilon)(\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}-\mathbf{z}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}^{2}-C)+C+O(\epsilon/\delta)\mathsf{OPT}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}-\mathbf{z}\right\rVert_{H,\mathbf{w}}^{2}+O(\epsilon/\delta)\mathsf{OPT}^{2}.\qed

We can now prove Theorem
1.5
.

Proof of Theorem
1.5
.

By an analysis identical to Theorem
6.13
, the algorithm makes at most
O(\log\log n)
recursive calls. We then scale the failure rate
\delta
and accuracy
\epsilon
in
6.24
by
\Theta(1/\log\log n)
so that we can union bound over
O(\log\log n)
recursive calls, and so that the error incurred at each recursive call is at most
(1+O(\epsilon/\log\log n))
, with probability at least
99/100
. The total error over the
O(\log\log n)
recursive calls is then at most

(1+O(\epsilon/\log\log n))^{O(\log\log n)}\leq 1+O(\epsilon)

so we conclude as desired.
∎

7
Applications: Dimension Reduction for Gamma Functions

The work of
[
BCLL18
]
introduced the
\gamma_{p}
functions, which are also known as smoothed
p
-norms and mixed
\ell_{2}
-
\ell_{p}
norms:

Definition 7.1

(
\gamma_{p}
functions, Definition 3.1,
[
AKPS19
]
)
.

For
t\geq 0
and
p\geq 1
, define

\gamma_{p}(t,x)=\begin{cases}\frac{p}{2}t^{p-2}x^{2}&\text{if $\lvert x\rvert\leq t$}\\
\left\lvert x\right\rvert^{p}+\left\lparen\frac{p}{2}-1\right\rparen t^{p}&\text{if $\lvert x\rvert>t$}\end{cases}

The
\gamma_{p}
functions have found many applications in fast algorithms for
\ell_{p}
regression and related problems
[
BCLL18
,
AKPS19
,
ABKS21
,
GPV21
]
. Recall also the simpler
\ell_{2}
-
\ell_{p}
loss defined in Lemma
1.11
, which is simply
M_{p}(x)=\min\{\lvert x\rvert^{2},\lvert x\rvert^{p}\}
. Now note that
\gamma_{p}(t,x)=\Theta(t^{p})M_{p}(x/t)
. Thus, for the purpose of bounding the associated sensitivities up to constant factors, we may replace our discussion of
\gamma_{p}
by
M_{p}
, for simplicity.

We now state our result for dimension reduction for
\gamma_{p}
functions, which is analogous to our Huber subspace embedding result in Theorem
6.13
.

Definition 7.2
.

For
p\geq 1
, thresholds
\mathbf{t}\geq\mathbf{1}_{n}
, and weights
\mathbf{w}\geq\mathbf{1}_{n}
, define the weighted
\gamma_{p}
-norm of
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
as

\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{\gamma_{p}(\mathbf{t},\cdot),\mathbf{w}}\coloneqq\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{w}_{i}\cdot\gamma_{p}(\mathbf{t}_{i},\mathbf{y}_{i})\right]^{1/p}.

If
\mathbf{w}=\mathbf{1}_{n}
. We simply write
\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{\gamma_{p}(\mathbf{t},\cdot)}
. Note that while we refer to this as a norm by abuse of notation, it does not satisfy the properties of a norm.

Theorem 7.3

(
\gamma_{p}
Subspace Embedding)
.

Let
p\in(1,2)
and let
\beta
be as defined in Lemma
1.11
. Let
\mathbf{t}\geq\mathbf{1}_{n}
. There is an algorithm which, with probability
1-\delta
, computes a set of weights
\mathbf{w}\geq\mathbf{1}_{n}
with

\nnz(\mathbf{w})\leq O\left\lparen d^{1+\beta}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}\left\lparen\log\frac{n}{\delta},\epsilon^{-1},\log\left\lVert\mathbf{t}\right\rVert_{\infty}\right\rparen\right\rparen

such that
\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{\gamma_{p}(\mathbf{t},\cdot),\mathbf{w}}^{2}=(1\pm\epsilon)\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{\gamma_{p}(\mathbf{t},\cdot)}^{2}
for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and runs in time

O\left\lparen\left[\nnz(\mathbf{A})+d^{\omega\cdot(1+1/\beta)}\right]\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}\left\lparen\log n,\log\frac{1}{\delta},\epsilon^{-1},\log\left\lVert\mathbf{t}\right\rVert_{\infty}\right\rparen\right\rparen.

Proof.

Because our proof is a simple extension of our Huber subspace embedding proof (Theorem
6.13
), we only outline the proof and necessary changes from the Huber result.

We first bucket the coordinates of
\mathbf{t}
into
O(\log\left\lVert\mathbf{t}\right\rVert_{\infty})
buckets
\left\{i\in[n]:2^{j-1}\leq\mathbf{t}_{i}<2^{j}\right\}
. Note that in typical applications,
\left\lVert\mathbf{t}\right\rVert_{\infty}
is roughly
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(n)
(see, e.g., the paragraph following Lemma 5.5 of
[
AKPS19
]
). A similar bucketing trick is used in
[
GPV21
]
. Once we have done this, the
\gamma_{p}
functions are within constant factors of each other, and thus we may focus on sensitivity sampling with a fixed threshold
t
, which we WLOG assume is
1
, and just refer to the corresponding loss as
\gamma_{p}
. Note that even with a constant factor variation in the
\gamma_{p}(t_{i},\cdot)
function, we can still get
(1+\epsilon)
approximations around the mean by sampling (see, e.g., how we handle weights differing by constant factors in Lemma
6.8
).

We first go over one step of the recursive sampling procedure, which reduces the number of rows from roughly
n
to
n^{\beta/(1+\beta)}d
. Let
\gamma
satisfy
1/\gamma=(\gamma n)^{\beta}
, or
\gamma=n^{-\beta/(1+\beta)}
. Now consider the coordinates
i\in[n]
with
\gamma_{p}
-sensitivities at least
\gamma
, that is,

S\coloneqq\left\{i\in[n]:\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\gamma_{p}}(\mathbf{A})\geq\gamma\right\}

where

\mathbf{s}_{i}^{\gamma_{p}}(\mathbf{A})\coloneqq\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\gamma_{p}([\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}](i))}{\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{\gamma_{p}}^{p}}=\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\gamma_{p}([\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}](i))}{\sum_{i^{\prime}=1}^{n}\gamma_{p}([\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}](i^{\prime}))}=\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\gamma_{p}(1,[\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}](i))}{\sum_{i^{\prime}=1}^{n}\gamma_{p}(1,[\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}](i^{\prime}))}.

As done in Lemma
6.11
, we may find a superset
S^{\prime}\supseteq S
of such coordinates in time at most

O\left\lparen\left[\nnz(\mathbf{A})+n^{1/(1+\beta)}d^{\omega}\right]\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}\left\lparen\log n,\log\frac{1}{\delta}\right\rparen\right\rparen\leq O\left\lparen\left[\nnz(\mathbf{A})+d^{\omega\cdot(1+1/\beta)}\right]\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}\left\lparen\log n,\log\frac{1}{\delta}\right\rparen\right\rparen

Since the sensitivity upper bounds sum to at most
O(d\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}\log(n/\delta))
, we have that

\left\lvert S^{\prime}\right\rvert\leq O(\gamma^{-1}d\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}\log(n/\delta)).

Now by Lemma
1.11
, any
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
either has distortion at most
\gamma
in the ratio between
\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{\gamma_{p}}^{p}
and
\min_{q\in[p,2]}\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{q}^{q}
, or the same ratio when restricted to coordinates outside of
S^{\prime}
. This means that the sum of Lewis weights in the range
[p,2]
(appropriately discretized by increments of
1/\log n
) bounds the
\gamma_{p}
-sensitivities up to a factor of
\gamma
either on all the coordinates or restricted outside of the set
S^{\prime}
, which means that the net arguments for Lewis weights via
[
BLM89
]
yields a net bound of shows that we can preserve all
\gamma_{p}
norms up to
(1\pm\epsilon)
factors with
O(\gamma^{-1}d\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(\log(n/\delta),\epsilon^{-1}))=O(n^{\beta/(1+\beta)}d\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(\log(n/\delta),\epsilon^{-1}))
samples.

Finally, via a recursive analysis similar to that done in Theorem
6.13
, we can repeat until we reach the desired number of rows in
O(\log\log n)
iterations.
∎

Although the running time as stated is quite large, one can further optimize this in a similar manner as discussed in Section
6.5.1
. For example, we can reduce to
d^{C}
dimensions in time
d^{C+1}
, where
C\approx 1.91236
, even for
p
near
1
, and the trade-offs only improve as
p\to 2
.

8
Applications: Active Regression for the Tukey Loss

An important loss function in practice is the
Tukey loss
:

Definition 8.1

(Tukey Loss Function)
.

For any
\tau\geq 0
, the Tukey loss function is given by:

\displaystyle T(x)=\begin{cases}\frac{\tau^{2}}{6}[1-(1-x^{2}/\tau^{2})^{3}]\quad|x|\leq\tau\\
\frac{\tau^{2}}{6}\quad\mathrm{otherwise}\end{cases}

For simplicity throughout, we assume that
\tau=1
. For a vector
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
we denote
\lVert\mathbf{y}\rVert_{M}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}M(\mathbf{y}(i))
. For a set of non-negative weights
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we let
\lVert\mathbf{y}\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{w}(i)\cdot M(\mathbf{y}(i))
.

Note that our theorem for general
M
-estimators does not capture Tukey loss, due to our assumption of at least polynomial growth, whereas the Tukey loss is flat for
\left\lvert x\right\rvert\geq\tau
. In this section, we show that nonetheless, our active regression techniques apply to this loss as well.

Our results will in fact hold for a more general class of Tukey-like loss functions defined in Assumption 1 of
[
CWW19
]
. This class includes the
\ell_{p}
Tukey losses, which we focus on in our proofs for simplicity.

Definition 8.2

(
\ell_{p}
Tukey Loss Function)
.

For any
\tau\geq 0
, the
\ell_{p}
Tukey loss function is given by:

\displaystyle M(x)=\begin{cases}|x|^{p}\quad|x|\leq\tau\\
\tau^{p}\quad\mathrm{otherwise}\end{cases}

For simplicity throughout, we assume that
\tau=1
.

We can check that for
\tau=1
and
p=2
, the loss in Definition
8.1
is equal to the loss in Definition
8.2
up to constants, so identical bounds will hold for it.

8.1
Preliminaries

We next state a few basic results regarding the
\ell_{p}
Tukey loss function, shown in
[
CWW19
]
. Mostly, these are analogs of the triangle inequality, which must be shown since
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{M}
is not a norm.

Lemma 8.3

(Tukey Triangle Inequality 1)
.

There is some fixed constant
c
(depending on
p
) such that, for any
a,b\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and any non-negative weight vector
w\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

\lVert\mathbf{a}+\mathbf{b}\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}\leq c\cdot(\lVert\mathbf{a}\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}+\lVert\mathbf{b}\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}).

Proof.

This simply uses the fact that
M(a)=|a|^{p}
when
|a|\leq 1
and
M(a)=1
otherwise.
∎

Lemma 8.4

(Tukey Triangle Inequality 2)
.

There is some fixed constant
c
(depending on
p
) such that, for any
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
with
|a|\leq\epsilon|b|
,

(1-c\epsilon)M(b)\leq M(a+b)\leq(1+c\epsilon)M(b).

From Lemma
8.4
we can also prove the following, which is used in Lemma
5.3
:

Lemma 8.5
.

There is some fixed constant
c
such that, for any
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
with
M(a)\leq\epsilon^{p}M(b)
,

(1-c\epsilon)M(b)\leq M(a+b)\leq(1+c\epsilon)M(b).

Proof.

If
M(a)\leq\epsilon^{p}M(b)
, then since
M(a)=|a|^{p}
when
|a|\leq 1
and
M(a)=1
otherwise, we must have
|a|=O(\epsilon)|b|
. The bound then follows from Lemma
8.4
.
∎

Relatedly we have,

Lemma 8.6

(Tukey Triangle Inequality 3 – Lemma 3.3. of
[
CWW19
]
)
.

There is some fixed constant
c
(depending on
p
) such that, for any
\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and non-negative weight vector
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
\lVert\mathbf{a}\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}\leq\epsilon^{2p+1}\lVert\mathbf{b}\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}
,

(1-c\epsilon)\lVert\mathbf{b}\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}\leq\lVert\mathbf{a}+\mathbf{b}\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}\leq(1+c\epsilon)\lVert\mathbf{b}\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}.

8.2
Constant Factor Approximation

As with
\ell_{p}
regression, we start by using a subspace embedding result for Tukey regression to give a constant factor approximate regression algorithm. Unlike Theorem
2.11
for the
\ell_{p}
norm, our embedding will only hold for a finite net of vectors rather than the full column span of the sampled matrix. As in
[
CWW19
]
, we will later handle this assumption by assuming finite bit complexity of our inputs and outputs. See Section
8.3
.

Theorem 8.7

(Tukey Loss Subspace Embedding – Lemma 6.7 of
[
CWW19
]
)
.

For any
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d},\mathbf{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, there is an algorithm that outputs a weight vector
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that for any set finite set of vectors
\mathcal{N}
in the span of
[\mathbf{A},\mathbf{b}]
,
\mathbf{w}
only has
\tilde{O}(d^{\max(1,p/2)}\log(|\mathcal{N}|/\delta)/\epsilon^{2})
nonzero entries and with probability
\geq 1-\delta
, for all
\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{N}
,

(1-\epsilon)\lVert\mathbf{y}\rVert_{M}\leq\lVert\mathbf{y}\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}\leq(1+\epsilon)\lVert\mathbf{y}\rVert_{M}.

We observe that the sampling algorithm used to achieve Lemma 6.7 preserves the norm of any vector in expectation. Thus via Markov’s inequality we have:

Lemma 8.8
.

Let
\mathbf{w}
be the weight vector produced by Theorem
8.7
. Let
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be fixed.
Then with arbitrarily large constant probability,
\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}=O(1)\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\rVert_{M}
.

Proof.

We use the notation in Section 6.1 of
[
CWW19
]
.
Given a weight vector
\mathbf{w}
, one step of the recursive sampling algorithm of
[
CWW19
]
is to
assign probabilities
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
to each of the
n
rows of
\mathbf{A}
, respectively. For the purposes of this lemma, it does not
matter what these sampling probabilities are.
One then chooses a sampling and rescaling matrix
\mathbf{S}
according to this distribution, where if row
i
is sampled,
then its new weight
\mathbf{w}_{i}^{\prime}
is set to
\mathbf{w}_{i}/p_{i}
, otherwise its weight is set to
0
. Thus, for any fixed
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
which does not depend on
\mathbf{w}
:

\E[\|\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\|_{M,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}]=\|\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\|_{M,\mathbf{w}},

When the algorithm begins,
\mathbf{w}=\mathbf{1}_{n}
. It follows inductively that for the final weight vector
\mathbf{w}
, that for any fixed
\mathbf{x}
, we have:

\E[\|\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\|_{M,\mathbf{w}}]=\|\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\|_{M}.

The lemma follows by Markov’s inequality.
∎

Combining Theorem
8.7
and Lemma
8.8
, we have an analog to Theorem
3.2
for Tukey regression.

Theorem 8.9

(Constant Factor Approximation for Tukey Regression)
.

Let
\mathbf{w}
be a weight vector, and
suppose
\lVert\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{x}_{1}-\mathbf{x}_{2})\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}=(1\pm\epsilon_{0})\lVert\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{x}_{1}-\mathbf{x}_{2})\rVert_{M}
for
all
\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2}\in\mathcal{N}
, where
\mathcal{N}
is a fixed set of vectors. Let

\tilde{\mathbf{x}}=\argmin_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{N}}\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}
, and let

\mathbf{x}^{*}=\argmin_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{N}}\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\rVert_{M}
.
Suppose also that
\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}-\mathbf{b}\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}=O(1)\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}-\mathbf{b}\rVert_{M}
.
Then
\lVert\mathbf{A}\tilde{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{b}\rVert_{M}=O(1)\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}-\mathbf{b}\rVert_{M}
.

By Theorem
8.7
and Lemma
8.8
there is an algorithm producing
\mathbf{w}
satisfying the required subspace embedding and norm preservation bounds with just
\tilde{O}(d^{\max(1,p/2)}\log(|\mathcal{N}|/\delta)/\epsilon^{2})
non-zero entries. Thus, computing
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}
requires reading just this number of entries from
\mathbf{b}
.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\lVert\mathbf{A}\tilde{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{b}\rVert_{M}

\displaystyle=O(1)\cdot(\lVert\mathbf{A}\tilde{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}\rVert_{M}+\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}-\mathbf{b}\rVert_{M})

(Approximate triangle inequality, Lem.
8.3
)

\displaystyle\leq O(1)\cdot\left(\frac{1}{1-\epsilon_{0}}\cdot\lVert\mathbf{A}(\tilde{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{x}^{*})\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}+\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}-\mathbf{b}\rVert_{M}\right)

(Subspace embedding assumption, since
\tilde{\mathbf{x}},\mathbf{x}^{*}\in\mathcal{N}
)

\displaystyle\leq O(1)\cdot\left(\frac{1}{1-\epsilon_{0}}\cdot\left(\lVert\mathbf{A}\tilde{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{b}\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}+\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}-\mathbf{b}\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}\right)+\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}-\mathbf{b}\rVert_{M}\right)

(Approximate triangle inequality, Lem.
8.3
)

\displaystyle\leq O(1)\cdot\left(\frac{2}{1-\epsilon_{0}}\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}-\mathbf{b}\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}+\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}-\mathbf{b}\rVert_{M}\right)

(Optimality of
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}
for the sampled problem)

\displaystyle\leq O(1)\cdot\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}-\mathbf{b}\rVert_{M}.

(Assumption that
\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}-\mathbf{b}\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}=O(1)\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}-\mathbf{b}\rVert_{M}
)

∎

8.3
Net Argument

The constant factor approximation result of Theorem
8.9
allows us to approximately minimize the Tukey loss regression problem over any finite net of vectors
\mathcal{N}
.
As in
[
CWW19
]
,
we need to make an assumption for the net
\mathcal{N}
to have a small size. For simplicity, we adopt the
assumption stated after Assumption 2 in
[
CWW19
]
, that the entries of
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{b}
are
integers of absolute values at most
n^{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(d)}
. We can also assume

\mathbf{A}
has full rank, since removing linearly dependent columns can be done in a preprocessing stage.

Lemma 8.10
.

Assuming that
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{b}
have integer entries bounded by
n^{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(d)}
and
\mathbf{A}
has full column rank, if
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is such that
\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\rVert_{M}\leq C\cdot\min_{\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{z}-\mathbf{b}\rVert_{M}
for some
C\leq\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(n)
,
and we round the entries of
\mathbf{x}
to the nearest integer multiple of
1/n^{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(d)}
, obtaining
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
,
then
\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}-\mathbf{b}\rVert_{M}\leq C\cdot(1+1/n^{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(d)})\cdot\min_{\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{z}-\mathbf{b}\rVert_{M}
. Further, all entries
of
\mathbf{x}
are at most
n^{d^{\alpha}}
for a fixed constant
\alpha>0
.

Further, if
\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\rVert_{M}=0
, then necessarily
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}=\mathbf{x}
. Also, if
\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{z}-\mathbf{b}\rVert_{M}>0

for all
\mathbf{z}
, then
\min_{\mathbf{z}}\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{z}-\mathbf{b}\rVert_{M}\geq 1/n^{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(d)}
.

Proof.

First suppose that
\min_{\mathbf{z}}\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{z}-\mathbf{b}\rVert_{M}=0
. This implies that
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}
and thus
\mathbf{x}=(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A})^{-1}\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{b}
. By Cramer’s rule
and our integrality assumptions, it follows that the entries of
\mathbf{x}
are integer multiples of
1/n^{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(d)}

and bounded in absolute value by
n^{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(d)}
. Thus,
\mathbf{x}
is rounded to itself, showing that
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}=\mathbf{x}

and the lemma holds.

Otherwise, suppose that
\min_{\mathbf{z}}\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\rVert_{M}>0
. Then the claim
is that
\min_{\mathbf{z}}\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{z}-\mathbf{b}\rVert_{M}\geq 1/n^{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(d)}
. To see this,
note that the matrix
[\mathbf{A},\mathbf{b}]
has rank-
(d+1)
, since the columns of
\mathbf{A}
are linearly independent.
Since
[\mathbf{A},\mathbf{b}]
is an integer matrix, it follows from
Lemma 4.1 of
[
CW09
]

that its minimum singular value is at least
n^{-\textrm{poly}(d)}
.
Notice that
\min_{\mathbf{z}}\|\mathbf{A}\mathbf{z}-\mathbf{b}\|_{2}^{2}
is at least the squared minimum singular value of
[\mathbf{A},\mathbf{b}]
, since:

\displaystyle\min_{\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|\mathbf{A}\mathbf{z}-\mathbf{b}\|_{2}^{2}=\min_{\mathbf{z}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1}:\mathbf{z}^{\prime}(d+1)=-1}\lVert[\mathbf{A},\mathbf{b}]\mathbf{z}^{\prime}\rVert_{2}^{2}\geq\min_{\mathbf{z}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1}:\lVert\mathbf{z}^{\prime}\rVert\geq 1}\lVert[\mathbf{A},\mathbf{b}]\mathbf{z}^{\prime}\rVert_{2}^{2}.

The last equation is exactly the minimum squared singular value of
[\mathbf{A},\mathbf{b}]
.
Consequently, the
\ell_{2}
-regression
cost is lower bounded by
n^{-\textrm{poly}(d)}
, which implies the
\ell_{p}
-regression cost
is lower bounded by
n^{-\textrm{poly}(d)}
, and the Tukey loss is lower bounded by

\min(1,n^{-\textrm{poly}(d)})=n^{-\textrm{poly}(d)}
(since we assume
\tau=1
). In this
case, if we round
\mathbf{x}
to the nearest vector with entries that are integer multiples of
1/n^{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(d)}
,
then using that the entries of
\mathbf{A}
are bounded by
n^{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(d)}
, we still obtain a

C\cdot(1+1/n^{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(d)})
-approximate solution.

Finally, note that the entries in any
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(n)
-approximate solution
\mathbf{x}
cannot be larger than

n^{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(d)}
. Indeed, by Lemma 5.2 of
[
CWW19
]
and since parts 2 and 3 of
Assumption 2 of
[
CWW19
]
hold by our integrality assumption, it follows that part 1
of Assumption 2 of
[
CWW19
]
also holds, and so
\|\mathbf{x}\|_{2}\leq n^{d^{\alpha}}
for a fixed
constant
\alpha>0
.
∎

Consider the set
\mathcal{N}
of all vectors
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
that are integer multiples of

1/n^{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(d)}
and bounded in absolute value by
n^{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(d)}
, for a sufficiently large
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(d)
.
Then
|\mathcal{N}|\leq n^{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(d)}
and we have

Theorem 8.11
.

Suppose
\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}-\mathbf{b}\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}=(1\pm\epsilon)\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}-\mathbf{b}\rVert_{M}
for all
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}

for a weight vector
\mathbf{w}
with
\|\mathbf{w}\|_{\infty}\leq n^{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(d)}
.
Then
\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}=(1\pm\epsilon)\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\rVert_{M}
for all
\mathbf{x}
for which

\|\mathbf{x}\|_{2}\leq n^{d^{\alpha}}
for a fixed constant
\alpha>0
. Further,
any
C
-approximate minimizer to
\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\rVert_{M}
, for some
C\leq\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(n)
,
necessarily satisfies
\|\mathbf{x}\|_{2}\leq n^{d^{\alpha}}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
be the rounding in
\mathcal{N}
of such an
\mathbf{x}
, as in Lemma
8.10
.
If
\min_{\mathbf{x}}\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\rVert_{M}=0
, then necessarily
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}=\mathbf{x}
by Lemma
8.10
.
Note that since the columns of
\mathbf{A}
are linearly independent,
\mathbf{x}
is unique, and as in
the proof of Lemma
8.10
, we have
\|\mathbf{x}\|_{2}\leq n^{d^{\alpha}}
for a fixed
constant
\alpha>0
.

Otherwise, we can observe that
\|\mathbf{w}\|_{\infty}\cdot\|\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\|_{\infty}=1/n^{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(d)}
by our assumption that
\lVert\mathbf{w}\rVert_{\infty}\leq n^{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(d)}
and the fact that that the granularity of our net is
1/n^{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(d)}
for a sufficiently large
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(d)
. This gives
\lVert\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}\leq 1/n^{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(d)}
. Further,
\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}-\mathbf{b}\rVert_{M}\geq 1/n^{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(d)}
and
\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\rVert_{M}\geq 1/n^{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(d)}
by Lemma
8.10
. In turn,
\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}-\mathbf{b}\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}\geq 1/n^{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(d)}
by our subspace embedding assumption on
\mathbf{w}
. Combining these bounds with the approximate triangle inequality of Lemma
8.6
gives:

\displaystyle\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}

\displaystyle=\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}-\mathbf{b}+\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}

\displaystyle=(1\pm 1/n^{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(d)})\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}-\mathbf{b}\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}

(Lemma
8.6
)

\displaystyle=(1\pm O(\epsilon))\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}-\mathbf{b}\rVert_{M}

(Subspace embedding assumption on
\mathbf{w}
for
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}
)

\displaystyle=(1\pm O(\epsilon))\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}+\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}-\mathbf{x})\rVert_{M}

\displaystyle=(1\pm O(\epsilon))\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\rVert_{M}.

(Lemma
8.6
again)

∎

Theorem
8.11
implies that we can use Theorem
8.9
with our net
\mathcal{N}
,
so that if we obtain a constant factor solution to the sampled regression problem
\min_{\mathbf{x}}\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}
over
\mathbf{x}
with
\|\mathbf{x}\|_{2}\leq n^{d^{\alpha}}
, then we
obtain an initial constant factor solution to the original regression problem.

8.4
Relative Error Approximation

We now build on our constant factor approximation result and the net arguments above to give a relative error approximation result for Tukey regression, akin to Theorem
3.4
. We assume that
\lVert\mathbf{b}\rVert_{M}=O(\mathsf{OPT})
, which we can ensure by computing an initial constant factor approximation as in Algorithm
4
, using Theorem
8.11
combined with Theorem
8.9
.

Theorem 8.12

(Relative Error Tukey Regression)
.

Consider
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
,
\mathbf{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and
p\geq 1
. Let
\mathsf{OPT}=\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\rVert_{M}
where
M
is the
\ell_{p}
Tukey loss (Def.
8.2
) and assume that
\lVert\mathbf{b}\rVert_{M}=O(\mathsf{OPT})
. There is an algorithm that reads
\tilde{O}\left(\frac{d^{\max(1,p/2)+O(1)}}{\epsilon^{2+p}}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}\log(n)\right)
entries of
\mathbf{b}
and outputs
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}
satisfying
\lVert\mathbf{A}\tilde{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{b}\rVert_{M}\leq(1+\epsilon)\mathsf{OPT}
with probability
99/100
.

Proof.

Let
\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{M}(\mathbf{A})
be as computed by the
\ell_{p}
Tukey sensitivities as computed by Algorithm
7
and Theorem
4.9
. Note then that

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{M}(\mathbf{A})\leq O(d^{\max\{1,p/2\}}\log^{2}n).

Note that Lemmas
5.2
,
5.3
, and
5.4
all hold for the Tukey loss, where assumption (1) is satisfied in Lemma
5.3
due to Lemma
8.5
. Thus, as reasoned in Lemma
5.5
, we just need to choose an
m
that is sufficient to union bound over a net. For the Tukey loss, we set

m=\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(d)\log n}{\epsilon^{2+p}}

and apply a union bound over all vectors
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}
in a net
\mathcal{N}
with
|\mathcal{N}|\leq n^{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(d)}
gives that this bound holds with high probability simultaneously for all vectors in the net. Letting this net be as in Theorem
8.11
, this is enough to give that the bound holds for all
\mathbf{x}
of interest.

Note that the number of entries of
\mathbf{b}
that are read is, in expectation,

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{p}_{i}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}m\cdot\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{M}(\mathbf{A})=O\left\lparen d^{\max\{1+p/2\}+O(1)}\frac{\log^{3}n}{\epsilon^{2+p}}\right\rparen.

Thus, this is, up to constant factors, the number of entries read with probability at least
99/100
as well.
∎

9
Applications: Kronecker Product Regression

In the
q
-th order Kronecker product regression problem, one is
given matrices
\mathbf{A}_{1},\mathbf{A}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{A}_{q}
, where
\mathbf{A}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}\times d_{i}}
,
as well as a vector
\mathbf{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}n_{2}\cdots n_{q}}
, and
the goal is to obtain a solution to the problem:

\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}d_{2}\cdots d_{q}}}\|(\mathbf{A}_{1}\otimes\mathbf{A}_{2}\cdots\otimes\mathbf{A}_{q})x-\mathbf{b}\|_{p},

where
\mathbf{A}_{1}\otimes\mathbf{A}_{2}\cdots\otimes\mathbf{A}_{q}
is the

\mathbb{R}^{(n_{1}\times n_{2}\times\cdots\times n_{q})\mathbf{x}(d_{1}\times d_{2}\times\cdot\times d_{q})}
matrix
whose
((i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{q}),(j_{1},\ldots,j_{q}))
-th entry is equal to

(\mathbf{A}_{1})_{i_{1},j_{1}}\cdots(\mathbf{A}_{q})_{i_{q},j_{q}}
. Typically
\prod_{i=1}^{q}d_{i}
is much less
than
\prod_{i=1}^{q}n_{i}
, and the goal is to obtain algorithms that do not explicitly form

\mathbf{A}_{1}\otimes\mathbf{A}_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{A}_{q}
, which is too expensive.

Kronecker product regression is a special case of
\ell_{p}
-regression
when the design matrix is highly structured. Such matrices
arise in spline regression,
signal processing, and multivariate data fitting

[
VL92
,
VLP93
,
GVL13
]
.
Recent work
[
DSSW18
,
DJS+19
]
designed algorithms for
p\in[1,2]
and constant
q

which solve this
problem with constant probability up to a
(1\pm\epsilon)
-factor in

\tilde{O}(\sum_{i=1}^{q}\nnz(\mathbf{A}_{i})+\nnz(\mathbf{b})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(d_{i}/\epsilon))
time;
see Theorem 1.2 of
[
DJS+19
]
. While
\sum_{i=1}^{q}\nnz(\mathbf{A}_{i})
is bounded by

O(\sum_{i}n_{i}d_{i})
, the main drawback of these works is that
\nnz(\mathbf{b})
may
be as large as
n_{1}\times n_{2}\times\cdots\times n_{q}
, which is prohibitive.
Theorem
1.2
shows that for every real
p\geq 1
, only
\tilde{O}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{q}d_{i}^{\max(1,p/2)}\right)

samples are needed, and our runtime is polynomial in this quantity. This should
be contrasted with prior work which required
\nnz(\mathbf{b})
time for every
p\neq 2
,
where
\nnz(\mathbf{b})
can be as large as
\prod_{i=1}^{q}n_{i}
.

Before stating our theorem, we need to understand Lewis weights of the Kronecker product
of two matrices
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
. Recall from Definition
2.3
that the Lewis weights of an
n\times d
matrix
\mathbf{A}
are
the unique numbers
\mathbf{w}^{p}_{1}(\mathbf{A}),\ldots,\mathbf{w}^{p}_{n}(\mathbf{A})
which satisfy

\mathbf{w}_{i}^{p}(\mathbf{A})=\mathbf{s}_{i}^{2}(\mathbf{W}^{1/2-1/p}\mathbf{A})
, where
\mathbf{s}_{i}^{2}(\mathbf{W}^{1/2-1/p}\mathbf{A})
is the
i^{th}
leverage score of
\mathbf{W}^{1/2-1/p}\mathbf{A}
, and
\mathbf{W}
is a diagonal matrix with
i^{th}

diagonal entry equal to
\mathbf{w}_{i}^{p}(\mathbf{A})
. We can show that the Lewis weights of
\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B}
are just the products of those of
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
.

Lemma 9.1
.

Given matrices
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}
, the
(i,j)^{th}
Lewis weight of
\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B}
is given by

\mathbf{w}_{i,j}^{p}(\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B})=\mathbf{w}_{i}^{p}(\mathbf{A})\cdot\mathbf{w}_{j}^{p}(\mathbf{B}).

Proof.

let
\mathbf{W}_{\mathbf{A}}
and
\mathbf{W}_{\mathbf{B}}
denote the diagonal matrices with the Lewis weights of
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
respectively on their diagonals. To prove the lemma, we just need to verify that
\mathbf{w}_{i}^{p}(\mathbf{A})\cdot\mathbf{w}_{j}^{p}(\mathbf{A})=\mathbf{s}_{i,j}^{2}((\mathbf{W}_{\mathbf{A}}^{1/2-1/p}\mathbf{A})\otimes(\mathbf{W}_{\mathbf{B}}^{1/2-1/p}\mathbf{B}))
.
By Proposition 3.2 of
[
DJS+19
]
, the
(i,j)
-th row leverage score of the
Kronecker product of two matrices
\mathbf{C}
and
\mathbf{D}
is
\mathbf{s}_{i}^{2}(\mathbf{C})\cdot\mathbf{s}_{j}^{2}(\mathbf{D})
. For completeness: to see this,
recall the leverage scores are basis-invariant and thus a property of the column span of
\mathbf{C}\otimes\mathbf{D}
.
If
\mathbf{R}
and
\mathbf{S}
are square matrices for which
\mathbf{C}\mathbf{R}
has orthonormal columns and
\mathbf{D}\mathbf{S}
has orthonormal columns, then
one can verify that
(\mathbf{C}\otimes\mathbf{D})\cdot(\mathbf{R}\otimes\mathbf{S})
has orthonormal columns, which implies the statement.
Consequently,

\mathbf{s}_{i,j}^{2}((\mathbf{W}_{\mathbf{A}}^{1/2-1/p}\mathbf{A})\otimes(\mathbf{W}_{\mathbf{B}}^{1/2-1/p}\mathbf{B}))=\mathbf{s}_{i}^{2}(\mathbf{W}_{\mathbf{A}}^{1/2-1/p}\mathbf{A})\cdot\mathbf{s}_{j}^{2}(\mathbf{W}_{\mathbf{B}}^{1/2-1/p}\mathbf{B})=\mathbf{w}_{i}^{p}(\mathbf{A})\cdot\mathbf{w}_{i}^{p}(\mathbf{B}).

∎

Corollary 9.2
.

Given matrices
\mathbf{A}_{1},\ldots\mathbf{A}_{q}
,
the
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{q})
-th Lewis weight of
\mathbf{A}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{A}_{q}
is

\mathbf{w}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{q}}^{p}(\mathbf{A}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{A}_{q})=\mathbf{w}^{p}_{i_{1}}(\mathbf{A}_{1})\cdot\mathbf{w}^{p}_{i_{2}}(\mathbf{A}_{2})\cdot\ldots\cdot\mathbf{w}^{p}_{i_{q}}(\mathbf{A}_{q}).

Proof.

Follows from Lemma
9.1
and induction.
∎

The following theorem is notable, in that it is the first algorithm for Kronecker product regression,
for every
p\geq 1
, whose running time
does not depend on
\nnz(\mathbf{b})
.

Theorem 9.3
.

Let
q\geq 1
and
p\geq 1
be constant, and
\epsilon>0
. The Kronecker product regression
problem can be solved up to a
(1+\epsilon)
-factor with constant probability in

\tilde{O}(\sum_{i=1}^{q}\nnz(\mathbf{A}_{i})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(\prod_{i=1}^{q}d_{i}/\epsilon))
time.

Proof.

By Theorem 6.1 of
[
CP15
]
, in
\tilde{O}(\sum_{i=1}^{q}\nnz(\mathbf{A}_{i}))
time
we can compute
2
-approximations
\hat{u^{\ell}_{i}}
to all of the Lewis weights
of all of the input matrices
\mathbf{A}^{\ell}
, where
\{u^{\ell}_{i}\}_{i}
is the set of Lewis weights of
\mathbf{A}^{\ell}
. By Corollary

9.2
, for each
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{q})
, we have that
\hat{u^{1}_{i_{1}}}\cdots\hat{u^{q}_{i_{q}}}
is a
2^{q}=O(1)
-approximation
to the
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{q})
-th Lewis weight of
\mathbf{A}^{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{A}^{q}
. By Theorem
3.4
, it follows
that for constant
p
if we sample
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(\prod_{i=1}^{q}d_{i}/\epsilon)
rows of
\mathbf{A}^{1}\otimes\cdot\otimes\mathbf{A}^{q}
and
corresponding entries of
\mathbf{b}
, then we can solve for a
(1+\epsilon)
-approximate solution
\mathbf{x}
to the
\ell_{p}
-Kronecker
product regression problem in
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(\prod_{i=1}^{q}d_{i}/\epsilon)
time. To sample proportional to the

\hat{u^{1}_{i_{1}}}\cdots\hat{u^{q}_{i_{q}}}
values, we can first sample an
i_{1}
proportional to
\hat{u^{1}_{i_{1}}}
, then independently
sample an
i_{2}
proportional to
\hat{u^{2}_{i_{2}}}
, and so on. It is possible to build a data structure in
O(n)
time, one for
each
\mathbf{A}^{\ell}
, so that a single sample can be extracted in
O(1)
time
[
Wal74
,
Wal77
]
.
So the total time for the sampling is

O(n)+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(\prod_{i=1}^{q}d_{i}/\epsilon)
, and we can assume
\tilde{O}(\sum_{i=1}^{q}\nnz(\mathbf{A}_{i}))
is at least
n
(otherwise we
can throw away zero rows), and so the overall time is
\tilde{O}(\sum_{i=1}^{q}\nnz(\mathbf{A}_{i})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(\prod_{i=1}^{q}d_{i}/\epsilon))
.
∎

10
Applications: Subspace Embeddings for Orlicz Norms

In this section, we show that our sensitivity sampling techniques immediately carry over to
Orlicz norms
, which can be thought of as scale-invariant versions of
M
-estimators. Subspace embeddings, regression, and row sampling algorithms for Orlicz norms have recently been studied by
[
ALS+18
]
and
[
SWY+19
]
, respectively.

The row sampling algorithms for Orlicz norm regression in
[
SWY+19
]
proceeds by constructing a generalization of well-conditioned bases for Orlicz norms using an oblivious subspace embedding, and then sampling the rows proportionally to an importance distribution defined using the well-conditioned basis. Due to this indirect construction of importance distributions, the algorithm of
[
SWY+19
]
incurs large polynomial factors in
d
on the sample size (i.e., the number of rows in the subspace embedding). Specifically, their total sensitivity bound from Lemma 4 is
O(d\kappa^{2})
using a well-conditioned basis of distortion
\kappa
, where
\kappa
given by their Corollary 12 is
O(d^{5/2}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}\log n)
. Then, the subspace embedding they obtain in Lemma 7 has
O(d^{7}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(\log n,\epsilon^{-1}))
rows with constant probability. Furthermore, small oblivious subspace embeddings do not exist when the
M
estimator function
M
grows faster than quadratic
[
BO10
]
, and this constraint carries over to their construction as well (see Assumption 1 of
[
SWY+19
]
).

We overcome both of these problems by directly constructing an importance sampling distribution according to the Orlicz sensitivities (see Definition
10.3
) using our Theorem
4.9
, which removes the quadratic growth assumption, allowing for a polynomial growth bound of degree
p_{G}
, and significantly improves the dependence on
d
in the dimension of the subspace embedding. More specificially, in Theorem
10.1
, we obtain a
\tilde{O}(\nnz(\mathbf{A})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(d/\epsilon))
time algorithm that computes a weighted subset of
O(d^{2}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(\log n,\epsilon^{-1}))
rows that achieves a
(1\pm\epsilon)
distortion subspace embedding for
p_{G}\leq 2
, as well as
O(d^{p_{G}/2+1}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(\log n,\epsilon^{-1}))
rows for
p_{G}>2
.

Theorem 10.1
.

Let
G
satisfy the hypotheses of Corollary
10.4
and let
p_{G}
be the polynomial upper bound exponent for
G
. There is an algorithm which, with constant probability, computes a set of weights
\mathbf{w}^{\prime}
such that

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{G,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}=(1\pm\epsilon)\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{G}

for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
in time

O\left\lparen\nnz(\mathbf{A})\log^{3}n+\frac{(Td^{\max\{1,p_{G}/2\}}+T^{2})\log^{4}n}{\epsilon^{2}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}\right\rparen,

where constant factor
\ell_{p_{G}}
Lewis weight approximation for an
n\times d
matrix
\mathbf{B}
takes time
O(\nnz(\mathbf{b})+T))
time (see Theorem
2.7
). Furthermore,

\nnz(\mathbf{w}^{\prime})=O\left\lparen\frac{d^{\max\{2,p_{G}/2+1\}}\log^{3}n}{\epsilon^{2}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}\right\rparen

with constant probability.

10.1
Fast Approximation of Orlicz Sensitivities

First recall the definition of Orlicz norms.

Definition 10.2

(Orlicz Norms and Weighted Orlicz Norms
[
SWY+19
]
)
.

Let
G:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
be a nonnegative convex function with
G(0)=0
. Then for
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the Orlicz norm of
\mathbf{y}
is defined by

\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{G}=\inf\left\{t>0:\sum_{i=1}^{n}G(\left\lvert\mathbf{y}_{i}\right\rvert/t)\leq 1\right\}.

Given a set of nonnegative weights
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the weighted Orlicz norm of
\mathbf{y}
is defined by

\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{G,\mathbf{w}}=\inf\left\{t>0:\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{w}_{i}G(\left\lvert\mathbf{y}_{i}\right\rvert/t)\leq 1\right\}.

We can naturally define the sensitivty of Orlicz norms as follows.

Definition 10.3

(Orlicz Sensitivities and Weighted Orlicz Sensitivities)
.

Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
. We define the
i
th Orlicz sensitivity
by

\mathbf{s}_{i}^{G}(\mathbf{A})\coloneqq\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{G}\leq 1}G(\left\lvert[\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}](i)\right\rvert)=\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\neq 0}G\left\lparen\frac{\left\lvert[\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}](i)\right\rvert}{\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{G}}\right\rparen

Let
\mathbf{w}\geq\mathbf{1}_{n}
be a set of weights. We define the
i
th weighted Orlicz sensitivity
by

\mathbf{s}_{i}^{G,\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{A})\coloneqq\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{G}\leq 1}\mathbf{w}_{i}G(\left\lvert[\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}](i)\right\rvert)=\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\neq 0}\mathbf{w}_{i}G\left\lparen\frac{\left\lvert[\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}](i)\right\rvert}{\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{G}}\right\rparen

The Orlicz norm can be thought of as a scale invariant version of
M
-estimators, and is the gauge norm of the
M
-ball of radius
1
. By homogeneity of the Orlicz norm, we can then restrict our attention to radius
1
, where the Orlicz norm is equivalent to the corresponding
M
-norm. Thus, our results for
M
-estimators from earlier easily carry forth to Orlicz norms. For instance, the following corollary is a simple consequence of Theorem
4.9
:

Corollary 10.4

(Fast Approximation of Orlicz Sensitivities)
.

Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
. Let
G:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
