be an Orlicz function that is polynomially bounded above with degree
p_{G}
, and satisfies the requirements analogous to those for
M
in Theorem
4.9
. Let
1\leq\tau\leq n
be a parameter. Then, with probability at least
99/100
, Algorithm
7
computes upper bounds
\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{G}(\mathbf{A})
on Orlicz sensitivities
\mathbf{s}_{i}^{G}(\mathbf{A})
that sum to at most

\tilde{\mathcal{T}}^{G}(\mathbf{A})\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{G}(\mathbf{A})=O(d^{\max\{1,p_{G}/2\}}\log^{2}n+\tau).

If constant factor Lewis weight approximation takes for an
n\times d
matrix
\mathbf{B}
takes
O(\nnz(\mathbf{B})\log n+T)
time (see Theorem
2.7
), then the total running time is

O\left\lparen\nnz(\mathbf{A})\log^{3}n+\frac{nT}{\tau}\log n\right\rparen.

As with
M
-estimators, we can also extend these results to the weighted case. The following lemma is a version of Lemma
4.11
(or Lemma 39 in
[
CW15a
]
), but requires a slightly different proof due to the fact that Orlicz norms are not “entrywise” norms.

Lemma 10.5

(Sensitivity Bounds for Weighted Orlicz Norms)
.

Let
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
\mathbf{w}\geq\mathbf{1}^{n}
. Let
N\coloneqq\left\lceil\log_{2}(1+\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{\infty})\right\rceil
. For
j\in[N]
, let

T_{j}\coloneqq\left\{i\in[n]:2^{j-1}\leq\mathbf{w}_{i}<2^{j}\right\},

let
\mathbf{A}\mid_{T_{j}}
denote the restriction of
\mathbf{A}
to the rows of
T_{j}
, and let
G_{j}\coloneqq 2^{j-1}\cdot G
. Then,

\mathbf{s}_{i}^{G,\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{A})\leq 2\cdot\mathbf{s}_{i}^{G_{j}}(\mathbf{A}\mid_{T_{j}})

and

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{s}_{i}^{G,\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{A})\leq O\left\lparen N\cdot d^{\max\{1,p_{G}/2\}}\log n\right\rparen.

Proof.

The proof can be found in Appendix
D
.
∎

The following is the resulting corollary for efficient computation of upper bounds on weighted Orlicz sensitivities, analogous to Corollary
4.12
for
M
-estimators.

Corollary 10.6

(Fast Approximation of Weighted Orlicz Sensitivities)
.

Let
G
satisfy the hypotheses of Corollary
10.4
and let
p_{G}
be the polynomial upper bound exponent for
G
. Let
\mathbf{w}\geq\mathbf{1}_{n}
be a set of weights. Define
N
as in Lemma
10.5
. There is an algorithm that computes weighted Orlicz norm sensitivities that sum to at most
O(Nd^{\max\{1,p_{G}/2\}}\log^{2}n+N\tau)
in time

O\left\lparen\nnz(\mathbf{A})\log^{3}n+N\frac{nT}{\tau}\log n\right\rparen,

where
T
is such that constant factor
\ell_{p_{G}}
Lewis weight approximation for an
n\times d
matrix
\mathbf{B}
takes
O(\nnz(\mathbf{B})\log n+T)
time (see Theorem
2.7
).

Proof.

This is simply the result of applying Corollary
10.4
on the
N
matrices
\mathbf{A}\mid_{T_{j}}
as defined in Lemma
10.5
.
∎

With upper bounds on Orlicz sensitivities in hand, we immediately obtain subspace embeddings by sampling from them.

Lemma 10.7

(Subspace Embeddings for Orlicz Norms)
.

Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
and
\epsilon>0
. Let
G:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
be a convex Orlicz function satisfying the requirements analogous to those for
M
in Theorem
4.9
. Let
\mathbf{w}\geq\mathbf{1}_{n}
be a set of weights. Let
\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{G}(\mathbf{A})\geq\mathbf{s}_{i}^{G,\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{A})
be upper bounds on the weighted Orlicz sensitivities of
\mathbf{A}
(see Definition
10.3
) and let

\tilde{\mathcal{T}}^{G,\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{A})\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{G,\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{A}).

Let
m\geq m_{0}
be a parameter larger than some

m_{0}=O\left\lparen\frac{d}{\epsilon^{2}}\left\lparen\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}\right\rparen\left\lparen\log\frac{1}{\delta}\right\rparen\right\rparen.

Let
\mathbf{w}^{\prime}\geq\mathbf{1}_{n}
be a set of weights chosen randomly so that

\mathbf{w}_{i}^{\prime}\coloneqq\begin{cases}\mathbf{w}_{i}/\mathbf{p}_{i}&\text{w.p. $\mathbf{p}_{i}$}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

where

\mathbf{p}_{i}\coloneqq\min\left\{1,m\cdot\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{G,\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{A})\right\}

Then with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{G,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}=(1\pm\epsilon)\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{G,\mathbf{w}}

for all
\mathbf{y}\in\Span(\mathbf{A})
. Furthermore,

\E\nnz(\mathbf{w}^{\prime})\leq m\tilde{\mathcal{T}}^{G,\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{A}).

Proof.

The proof follows from the proof of Lemma 7 of
[
SWY+19
]
, replacing their sampling process with our sensitivity sampling. We only briefly outline the necessary changes, since the proof is almost identical to their proof, as well as other sensitivity Bernstein bounds done in this paper, such as Lemma
4.8
.

By Lemma
C.10
, it suffices to show that
\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{G,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}=(1\pm\epsilon)\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{G,\mathbf{w}}
for all
\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{N}
where
\mathcal{N}
is an
\epsilon
-net on the unit Orlicz sphere. Such a net can be chosen to have size at most
\log\left\lvert\mathcal{N}\right\rvert\leq O(d\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
by Lemma
C.7
.

It is shown in the proof of Lemma 7 of
[
SWY+19
]
that if

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{w}_{i}^{\prime}G(\left\lvert\mathbf{y}_{i}\right\rvert)\in\left[1-\frac{\epsilon}{4},1+\frac{\epsilon}{4}\right],

then
\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{G,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}\in[1-\epsilon/4,1+\epsilon/4]
as long as
G(0)=0
and
G
is convex. Then, the result follows from exactly the same sensitivity sampling and Bernstein bound calculation as done in Lemma
4.8
.
∎

10.2
Nearly Input Sparsity Time Algorithms

Lemma
10.7
immediately yields subspace embeddings of size
O(d^{\max\{2,p_{G}/2+1\}}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(\log n,\epsilon^{-1}))
by computing Orlicz sensitivity upper bounds as in Corollary
10.4
with
\tau=1
. However, this does not yield input sparsity time algorithms, and as done in previous sections (see, e.g., Theorem
5.6
), we can apply Theorem
4.9
in a two step process in order to obtain these subspace embeddings in nearly input sparsity time.

Proof of Theorem
10.1
.

We first obtain sensitivity upper bounds
\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{G}(\mathbf{A})
by Corollary
10.4
with
\tau=T
, so that they sum to

\tilde{\mathcal{T}}^{G}(\mathbf{A})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{G}(\mathbf{A})=O\left\lparen d^{\max\{1,p_{G}/2\}}\log^{2}n+T\right\rparen

and can be computed in time

O\left\lparen\nnz(\mathbf{A})\log^{3}n+n\log n\right\rparen=O\left\lparen\nnz(\mathbf{A})\log^{2}n\right\rparen.

Then by Lemma
10.7
, we can obtain a set of weights
\mathbf{w}
such that

\nnz(\mathbf{w})\leq O\left\lparen(d^{\max\{1,p_{G}/2\}}\log^{2}n+T)\cdot\frac{d}{\epsilon^{2}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}\right\rparen

with constant probability, and

\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{G,\mathbf{w}}=(1\pm\epsilon)\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{G}

for all
\mathbf{y}\in\Span(\mathbf{A})
. We then compute weighted Orlicz sensitivities
\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{G,\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{A})
for
\mathbf{w}
via Corollary
10.6
with
\tau=d
. Since the sensitivity upper bounds are at least
1/n
,
\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{\infty}=O(n)
so that the weighted Orlicz sensitivity upper bounds sum to at most

\tilde{\mathcal{T}}^{G}(\mathbf{A})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{G}(\mathbf{A})=O\left\lparen d^{\max\{1,p_{G}/2\}}\log^{3}n\right\rparen.

Furthermore, the number of nonzero entries rows is at most
\nnz(\mathbf{w})
, so these can be computed in time at most

O\left\lparen\nnz(\mathbf{A})\log^{3}n+\frac{(Td^{\max\{1,p_{G}/2\}}+T^{2})\log^{4}n}{\epsilon^{2}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}\right\rparen,

Again applying Lemma
10.7
, we can obtain a random set of weights
\mathbf{w}^{\prime}
such that, with constant probability,

\nnz(\mathbf{w}^{\prime})\leq O\left\lparen d^{\max\{1,p_{G}/2\}}\log^{3}n\cdot\frac{d}{\epsilon^{2}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}\right\rparen=O\left\lparen\frac{d^{\max\{2,p_{G}/2+1\}}\log^{3}n}{\epsilon^{2}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}\right\rparen

and

\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{G,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}=(1\pm\epsilon)\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{G,\mathbf{w}}=(1\pm\epsilon)^{2}\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{G}

for all
\mathbf{y}\in\Span(\mathbf{A})
. Rescaling
\epsilon
by constant factors gives the desired conclusion.
∎

11
Applications: Robust Subspace Approximation

By using our improvements in sensitivity sampling, we are able to obtain improvements to the robust subspace approximation algorithms of
[
CW15a
]
.

In the subspace approximation problem, we are given a matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, which we think of as a set of
n
points in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Our task is then to compute a
d\times d
rank
k
projection matrix
\mathbf{X}
that minimizes

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{X}-\mathbf{A}\right\rVert_{M},

where here, the
M
-norm of an
n\times d
matrix
\mathbf{M}
is defined as

\left\lVert\mathbf{M}\right\rVert_{M}=\left\lparen\sum_{i=1}^{n}M(\left\lVert\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{M}\right\rVert_{2})\right\rparen^{1/p_{M}}

or equivalently, the vector
M
-norm for the vector of
\ell_{2}
row norms of
\mathbf{M}
. We also define the weighted version

\left\lVert\mathbf{M}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}=\left\lparen\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{w}_{i}M(\left\lVert\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{M}\right\rVert_{2})\right\rparen^{1/p_{M}}

We consider approximation algorithms to the above problem, so that we seek a rank
k

\mathbf{X}
such that

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{X}-\mathbf{A}\right\rVert_{M}\leq(1+\epsilon)\min_{\rank(\mathbf{Y})=k}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{Y}-\mathbf{A}\right\rVert_{M}.

For this problem,
[
CW15a
]
obtained an algorithm with parameters depending on
K=(\log n)^{O(\log k)}
, running in time

O\left\lparen\nnz(\mathbf{A})\log n+(n+d)\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(K/\epsilon)\right\rparen

to reduce the problem to an instance of size
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(K/\epsilon)\times\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(K/\epsilon)
. Furthermore, their algorithm makes use of an oblivious subspace embedding, which is only applicable to
M
-estimators with growth bounded by a quadratic, i.e.,
p_{M}\leq 2
. By using our improved sensitivity bounds of Section
4
, we match the guarantees of their robust subspace approximation and regression algorithms for
\ell_{p}
, up to
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(\log(n))
factors, for any
p_{M}
. That is, we obtain the following improved theorem:

Theorem 11.1
.

Let
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{M}
be an
M
-norm. There is an algorithm which computes matrices
\mathbf{T}
,
\mathbf{S}
, and
\mathbf{U}
, and a set of weights
\mathbf{w}
such that

\min_{\rank(\mathbf{Y})=k}\left\lVert\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{S}^{\top}-(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{U})\mathbf{Y}(\mathbf{U}\mathbf{S}^{\top})\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}\leq(1+\epsilon)\min_{\rank(\mathbf{Y})=k}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{Y}\right\rVert_{M}.

Furthermore,
\mathbf{T}
and
\mathbf{S}
only have
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(k(\log n)/\epsilon)
rows and
\mathbf{U}
only has
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(k(\log n)/\epsilon)
columns, and the matrices
\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{S}^{\top}
,
\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{U}
, and
\mathbf{U}\mathbf{S}^{\top}
can be formed in

O\left\lparen[\nnz(\mathbf{A})+(n+d)\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(k/\epsilon)]\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(\log n)\right\rparen

time.

We now briefly sketch out the changes, leaving many of the details to the
[
CW15a
]
paper, as the algorithm and analysis is essentially identical to their algorithms for
\ell_{p}
losses. The approach of
[
CW15a
]
, like many other coreset algorithms, begins with an initial crude relative error approximation, which is later refined down to
(1+\epsilon)
relative error.

11.1
Crude Relative Error Approximation

In this section, we derive the analogues of the
ConstApprox
algorithm, which is Algorithm 4 and Theorem 47 of
[
CW15a
]
. This computes a
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(k\log n)
-dimensional subspace, such that projection to this subspace is within a
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(k\log n)
factor of optimal.

The first step is to reduce the number of columns of
\mathbf{A}
via standard
\ell_{2}
dimension reduction techniques, as formalized by the following theorem.

Theorem 11.2

(Theorem 32 of
[
CW15a
]
)
.

Let
M:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
be increasing and polynomially bounded with degree
p
(Definition
4.1
). Let
\mathbf{R}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times d}
be an
OSNAP
sparse embedding
[
NN13
]
with sparsity parameter
s
. Then, there is
s=O(p^{3}/\epsilon)
and
m=O(k^{2}/\epsilon^{O(p)})
such that with constant probability,

\min_{\rank(\mathbf{X})=k}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{R}^{\top}\mathbf{X}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{R}^{\top}\right\rVert_{M}\leq(1+\epsilon)\min_{\rank(\mathbf{Y})=k}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{Y}-\mathbf{A}\right\rVert_{M}.

By applying the above theorem with
\epsilon
set to a constant, we make work instead with
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{R}
, which only has
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(k)
columns. We may then apply our improved sensitivity bounds to efficiently reduce the number of rows for a crude bicriteria guarantee.

Algorithm 13

ConstApprox
(\mathbf{A},k)

input:
Matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, rank parameter
k

output:
Projection
\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top}

1:

Let
\mathbf{R}
be a sparse embedding matrix from Theorem
11.2

2:

Compute
M
-sensitivity upper bounds
\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{M}(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{R})
by Theorem
4.9
with
\tau=\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(k)

3:

Let
r
be sufficiently large in
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(k)

4:

Let
\mathbf{w}_{i}
be
1/\mathbf{p}_{i}
w.p.
\mathbf{p}_{i}
and
0
otherwise, for
\mathbf{p}_{i}\coloneqq\min\{1,r\cdot\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{i}^{M}(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{R})\}

5:

return

\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top}
, where
\mathbf{U}^{\top}
is an orthonormal basis for the row space of

Lemma 11.3

(Improvement to Theorem 47 of
[
CW15a
]
)
.

With constant probability, the matrix
\mathbf{U}
output by Algorithm
13
has

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}\leq\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(k)\min_{\rank(\mathbf{Y})=k}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{Y}\right\rVert_{M}

The running time is

O\left\lparen[\nnz(\mathbf{A})+(n+d)\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(k)]\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}\log(n)\right\rparen

with high probability.

Proof.

The proof is nearly identical to the
\ell_{p}
case of Theorem 47 of
[
CW15a
]
. Instead of using their Lemma 39 to compute sensitivity upper bounds that sum to approximately
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(k)\sqrt{n}
, we use our Theorem
4.9
to compute sensitivity upper bounds that sum to
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(k\log n)
. Then, we only require one round of sampling in order to reduce the number of rows to
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(k\log n)
, and thus results in guarantees analogous to their
\ell_{p}
loss case, up to a
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}\log(n)
factor.
∎

Note that
[
CW15a
]
only achieve a much larger distortion of
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(k)^{O(\log\log n)}=(\log n)^{O(\log k)}
rather than
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(k\log n)
, due to their recursive application of sensitivity sampling for a depth of
O(\log\log n)
, which each incurs a distortion of
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(k)
.

11.2
Refinement to
(1+\epsilon)
-Approximations

Using the crude
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(k)
approximation, the next step in the
[
CW15a
]
algorithm is to adapt a result of
[
DV07
]
to obtain a
d\times\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(k(\log n)/\epsilon)
matrix
\mathbf{U}
with orthonormal columns such that

\min_{\rank(\mathbf{Y})=k}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{U}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}\leq(1+\epsilon)\min_{\rank(\mathbf{Y})=k}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{Y}\right\rVert_{M}.

This is the
DimReduce
algorithm described as Algorithm 2 of
[
CW15a
]
as well as Theorem 46.

Theorem 11.4

(Theorem 46 of
[
CW15a
]
)
.

Let
K>0
and let
\hat{\mathbf{X}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
be a
m
-dimensional projection such that

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{I}-\hat{\mathbf{X}})\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}\leq K\min_{\rank(\mathbf{Y})=k}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{Y})\right\rVert_{M}.

Then, there is an algorithm that returns
\mathbf{U}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times(m+K\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(k/\epsilon))}
such that

\min_{\rank(\mathbf{Y})=k}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{U}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}\leq(1+\epsilon)\min_{\rank(\mathbf{Y})=k}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{Y}\right\rVert_{M}.

This problem can then by sketched from the left by sparse embedding
\mathbf{S}
with
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(k(\log n)/\epsilon)
rows so that we are left with solving the problem

\min_{\rank(\mathbf{Y})=k}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{S}^{\top}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{S}^{\top}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}.

Finally, the last step is to conduct another row sampling process to reduce the number of rows
n
to a
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(k(\log n)/\epsilon)
-sized instance. For this step, we again use our improved sensitivity sampling lemma as in Theorem
4.9
. However, there is an additional challenge in this setting in that we cannot afford to compute the matrix
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{U}
explicitly, since this requires
\nnz(\mathbf{A})\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(k(\log n)/\epsilon)
time to compute. The solution to this problem described in
[
CW15a
]
is to use an oblivious sparse
\ell_{p}
subspace embedding
\boldsymbol{\Pi}
and then to compute a QR decomposition of
\boldsymbol{\Pi}\mathbf{A}[\mathbf{S}^{\top}~\mathbf{U}]=\mathbf{Q}\mathbf{R}
in order to obtain a well-conditioned basis
\mathbf{U}=\mathbf{A}[\mathbf{S}^{\top}~\mathbf{U}]\mathbf{R}^{-1}
. Then, the row norms of
\mathbf{U}
, which can be used as approximate sensitivity upper bounds, are estimated by multiplying by a Gaussian matrix
\mathbf{G}
with
O(1)
columns, so that
\mathbf{U}\mathbf{G}=\mathbf{A}([\mathbf{S}^{\top}~\mathbf{U}]\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{G})
can be computed efficiently in
O(\nnz(\mathbf{A}))
time. However, this approach has a drawback, in that the use of an oblivious subspace embedding restricts the range of applicable growth upper bounds
p_{M}
to at most
2
.

Instead, as observed in
[
SW19
]
, we make use of the fact that Lewis weight computation can be done efficiently as long as matrix vector products can be computed efficiently, so that
\nnz(\mathbf{A})
dependencies can approximately be replaced by
T(\mathbf{A})
dependencies, where
T(\mathbf{A})
denotes the time required to compute a matrix vector product. Thus, for computing the Lewis weights of
\mathbf{A}[\mathbf{S}^{\top}~\mathbf{U}]
, we can approximately replace all
\nnz(\mathbf{A})
terms with
\nnz(\mathbf{A})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(k(\log n)/\epsilon)
, by multiplying the product one matrix at a time. This is implicit in the proof of Theorem 3 of
[
SW19
]
.

Thus, following the
Approx
algorithm described as Algorithm 7 with their sensitivity bound replaced by our sensitivity bound of Theorem
4.9
, we may find a set of weights
\mathbf{w}^{\prime}
such that

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{S}^{\top}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{S}^{\top}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}=(1\pm\epsilon)\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{S}^{\top}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{S}^{\top}\right\rVert_{M,\mathbf{w}}.

for all
\mathbf{Y}
with
\rank(\mathbf{Y})=k
, with
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(k(\log n)/\epsilon)
nonzero entries. After this step, all of the matrices involved are of size
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(k(\log n)/\epsilon)\times\mathop{\mathrm{}}{poly}(k(\log n)/\epsilon)
, as desired.

12
Lower Bounds

We now present various lower bounds on active sampling for
\ell_{p}
regression. Our first result obtains a nearly optimal lower bound of
\Omega(d/\epsilon^{2})
for
p\in(0,1)
, which matches our upper bound of Theorem
1.2
up to logarithmic factors. Our lower bound is similar to Theorem 5.1 of
[
CD21
]
, and is based on distinguishing biased coin flips. We also show that the same instance gives a lower bound
\Omega(d/\epsilon)
in the range of
p\in(1,2)
, which is also tight with our upper bound in Theorem
1.2
. For
d=1
, we note that the active
\ell_{p}
regression problem is equivalent to the
\ell_{p}
power means
problem. Thus, these results also improve upon a query complexity lower bound for this problem by
[
CSS21
]
, which shows a lower bound of
\Omega(\epsilon^{1-p})
in one dimension. However, this lower bound is useful for us for
p>2
, which shows that our Theorem
1.2
is off by a factor of
\epsilon
in the
\epsilon
dependence.

Our next result shows that
\Omega(d^{\max(1,p/2)})
samples are required to solve Problem
1.1
for the
\ell_{p}
norm for any
p\geq 1
to a constant approximation factor. This show that Algorithm
4
is optimal in its dependence on
d
, up to
polylogarithmic factors (see Theorem
1.2
).

Finally, our last lower bound concerns algorithms which solve the
\ell_{p}
-regression problem
\min_{\mathbf{x}}\|\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\|_{p}
up to a constant factor in a specific way. Namely, say an algorithm
is a
sampling-and-reweighting
algorithm if, given an
n\times d
input matrix
\mathbf{A}
, the algorithm first reads
\mathbf{A}
and then decides on a subset
S
of
s
entries of
\mathbf{b}
to read in an arbitrary way. The algorithm also decides on a diagonal rescaling matrix
\mathbf{D}\in\mathbb{R}^{s\times s}
–
\mathbf{D}
may be arbitrary, except that we require that if two rows of
\mathbf{A}
are identical and are both sampled in
S
, they are given the same weight in
\mathbf{D}
. We also assume that the number
s
of samples is a function of
d,\epsilon,
and the failure probability
\delta
, and is independent of
n
. These assumptions hold for all importance-based sampling methods for subspace preservation.

After deciding on
S
and
\mathbf{D}
, the algorithm then reads
the entries in
\mathbf{b}
indexed by the set
S
, denoted
\mathbf{b}_{S}
, and sets

\mathbf{x}^{\prime}=\textrm{argmin}_{x}\|\mathbf{D}\mathbf{A}_{S}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{D}\mathbf{b}_{S}\|_{p}
, where
\mathbf{A}_{S}

is the subset of rows of
\mathbf{A}
corresponding to the entries in
\mathbf{b}_{S}
. We show that any sampling-and-reweighting algorithm which
fails with probability at most
\delta
, necessarily takes
|S|=\Omega(1/\delta^{p-1})
samples.
Moreover, this remains true even if
\|\mathbf{b}\|_{p}=O(1)\cdot\min_{\mathbf{x}}\|\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\|_{p}
.

We stress that our main algorithms
are not
sampling-and-reweighting algorithms due to the success probability boosting steps of Section
3.1.1
which ensure that
\|\mathbf{b}\|_{p}=O(1)\cdot\min_{\mathbf{x}}\|\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\|_{p}
and
\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{b}\rVert_{p}=O(\lVert\mathbf{b}\rVert_{p})
with probability at least
1-\delta
. With these steps, our approach
achieves a
O(\log 1/\delta)
dependence overall, an exponential improvement over what is possible by simple sampling-and-reweighting algorithms. We also remark that our lower bound becomes vacuous when
p=1
, which is required – Chen and Dereziński
[
CD21
]
as well as Parulekar, Parulekar, and Price
[
PPP21
]
achieve
O(\log(1/\delta))
dependence with simple sampling-and-reweighting for
\ell_{1}
regression.

12.1
Lower Bounds for
p\in(0,1)

We first show an
\Omega(d/\epsilon^{2})
lower bound for
p\in(0,1)
, which is tight up to logarithmic factors. The idea is essentially the same as the lower bound of
[
CD21
]
. We use Yao’s minimax principle to restrict our attention to deterministic algorithms which must succeed with high probability over a random distribution over input instances.

We first recall the result of
[
CD21
]
, which provides a generic reduction from
d
-dimensional lower bounds to
1
-dimensional lower bounds via a padding argument. Although
[
CD21
]
prove a theorem online in the case of
\ell_{1}
, the following result is an easy generalization that is implicit from their proof:

Theorem 12.1

(Theorem 5.1,
[
CD21
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{D}_{0}
and
\mathcal{D}_{1}
be two distributions over label vectors
\mathbf{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
such that distinguishing between
\mathbf{b}\sim\mathcal{D}_{0}
and
\mathbf{b}\sim\mathcal{D}_{1}
with probability at least
2/3
requires at least
q
queries to
\mathbf{b}
in expectation, for any deterministic algorithm. Furthermore, suppose that there exists
\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
such that, with probability at least
99/100
,
\mathcal{D}_{0}
and
\mathcal{D}_{1}
can be distinguished by
\tilde{x}\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\left\lVert\mathbf{a}\tilde{x}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}\leq(1+\epsilon)\min_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\left\lVert\mathbf{a}x-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}.

Finally, suppose that there exist
R>0
and
c\geq 1
such that
\min_{x}\left\lVert\mathbf{a}x-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}\in[R,cR]
with probability at least
99/100
for
\mathbf{b}\sim\frac{1}{2}(\mathcal{D}_{0}+\mathcal{D}_{1})
. Then, there exists an
md\times d
matrix
\mathbf{A}
and a distribution
\mathcal{D}
over label vectors
\mathbf{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{md}
such that any deterministic algorithm which outputs
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that

\Pr\left\{\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\tilde{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}\leq\left\lparen 1+\frac{\epsilon}{200c}\right\rparen\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}\right\}\geq\frac{99}{100}

must make at least
\Omega(dq)
queries to
\mathbf{b}
in expectation.

Thus, it suffices to show a
1
-dimensional lower bound which suits the hypotheses of Theorem
12.1
. Our hard input distribution will be the same as that of
[
CD21
, Theorem 5.1]
.

Theorem 12.2
.

Let
0<p<1
be a constant. Let
\epsilon>0
be sufficiently small and let
n=100\left\lceil\epsilon^{-2}\right\rceil
. Let
\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be the all ones vector. Let
\mathcal{D}_{0}
be the distribution over binary vectors
\mathbf{b}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
which independently draws each coordinate as a Bernoulli with bias
1/2+\epsilon
and let
\mathcal{D}_{1}
be the distribution which independently draws each coordinate as a Bernoulli with bias
1/2-\epsilon
. Then, any
\tilde{x}
such that

\left\lVert\mathbf{a}\tilde{x}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}\leq(1+\epsilon)\min_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\left\lVert\mathbf{a}x-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}

distinguishes whether
\mathbf{b}\sim\mathcal{D}_{0}
or
\mathbf{b}\sim\mathcal{D}_{1}
with probability at least
99/100
.

Proof.

Note that the optimal
x^{*}
minimizing
\left\lVert\mathbf{a}x-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}
over
x\in\mathbb{R}
must lie in
[0,1]
. Indeed, if
x<0
, then
-x
has a strictly lower cost than
x
, and if
x>1
, then
x=1
has a strictly lower cost. Thus, the objective function can be written as

\left\lVert\mathbf{a}x-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}=(n-r)\cdot x^{p}+r\cdot(1-x)^{p}

(21)

where
r
is the number of ones in
\mathbf{b}
. As noted by
[
CD21
]
, note that
r\in[(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\epsilon}{2})n,(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{3\epsilon}{2})n]
with probability at least
99/100
if
\mathbf{b}\sim\mathcal{D}_{0}
, and similarly,
r\in[(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\epsilon}{2})n,(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{3\epsilon}{2})n]
with probability at least
99/100
if
\mathbf{b}\sim\mathcal{D}_{1}
. Let this event be denoted as
\mathcal{E}
, and condition on this event. Write this as
r=n/2+a\epsilon n
for some
a\in[1/2,3/2]
if
\mathbf{b}\sim\mathcal{D}_{0}
and
a\in[-3/2,-1/2]
if
\mathbf{b}\sim\mathcal{D}_{1}
.

Optimal Solutions.

We will first compute the optimal cost. Since
x\mapsto x^{p}
for
p\in(0,1)
is nonconvex, we have three candidate solutions for the optimum: the endpoints
x=0
,
x=1
, and the unique stationary point

x=\frac{1}{1+(n/r-1)^{1/(p-1)}}

of Equation (
21
). It can easily be seen that the costs for
x=0
and
x=1
are
r
and
n-r
, respectively. Now assume that
\mathbf{b}\sim\mathcal{D}_{0}
, since the other case follows symmetrically. Then,

(n/r-1)^{1/(p-1)}=\left[\frac{1}{1/2+a\epsilon}-1\right]^{1/(p-1)}=\left[\frac{1/2+a\epsilon}{1/2-a\epsilon}\right]^{1/(1-p)}=1+O(\epsilon)

so

x=\frac{1}{2+O(\epsilon)}=\frac{1}{2}-O(\epsilon).

The objective cost is thus

\displaystyle(n-r)\cdot x^{p}+r\cdot(1-x)^{p}

\displaystyle=\left\lparen\frac{1}{2}-a\epsilon\right\rparen n\cdot\left\lparen\frac{1}{2}-O(\epsilon)\right\rparen^{p}+\left\lparen\frac{1}{2}+a\epsilon\right\rparen n\cdot\left\lparen\frac{1}{2}+O(\epsilon)\right\rparen^{p}

\displaystyle=\frac{n}{2^{p}}\left[\left\lparen\frac{1}{2}-a\epsilon\right\rparen(1-O(\epsilon))^{p}+\left\lparen\frac{1}{2}+a\epsilon\right\rparen(1+O(\epsilon))^{p}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{n}{2^{p}}(1\pm O(\epsilon)).

Since
p<1
, this has cost worse than
r
or
n-r
, so the optimal solution is
n-r
, conditioned on
\mathcal{E}
. Likewise, if
\mathbf{b}\sim\mathcal{D}_{1}
, then the optimal solution is
x=0
with cost
r
.

Suboptimal Solutions.

We now show that, given a nearly optimal solution
\tilde{x}
, we can determine whether
\mathbf{b}
is drawn from
\mathcal{D}_{0}
or
\mathcal{D}_{1}
with high probability by testing whether
\tilde{x}\in[1/2,1]
or
\tilde{x}\in[0,1/2]
. Again, assume by symmetry that
\mathbf{b}\sim\mathcal{D}_{0}
. Suppose that
x\in[0,1/2]
. If
x>1/2-O(\epsilon)
, then as calculated above,
x
is not even an
\alpha
-factor solution for some constant
\alpha
. Otherwise, we have that

x\leq\frac{1}{2}-O(\epsilon)=\frac{1}{1+(n/r-1)^{1/(p-1)}}

which rearranges to

p\cdot(n-r)x^{p-1}-pr\cdot(1-x)^{p-1}>0

which means that the objective is increasing on this interval. Thus, for
x\in[0,1/2]
, the smallest that the cost can be is
r
, which is a factor of
(1+\epsilon)
larger than
n-r
. Thus, a
(1+\epsilon)
-factor approximation must distinguish between
\mathbf{b}
drawn from
\mathcal{D}_{0}
and
\mathcal{D}_{1}
.
∎

As discussed in
[
CD21
]
, the distributions of Theorem
12.2
require at least
\Omega(\epsilon^{-2})
queries to distinguish, by standard arguments. Then, by combining Theorems
12.1
and
12.2
, we arrive at the following:

Theorem 12.3
.

Let
p\in(0,1)
be a constant. Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
and let
\mathbf{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Suppose that with probability at least
99/100
, an algorithm
\mathcal{A}
returns
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\tilde{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}\leq(1+\epsilon)\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}.

Then,
\mathcal{A}
queries
\Omega(d/\epsilon^{2})
entries of
\mathbf{b}
in expectation.

12.2
Lower Bounds for
p\in(1,2)

In the range of
p\in(1,2)
, we analyze the same lower bound instance as in Theorem
12.2
. However, the nature of the objective function changes in this parameter regime, and our lower bound weakens to
\Omega(d/\epsilon)
. In particular, the value of the endpoints
x=0
and
x=1
stay at
r
and
n-r
, but the value of the stationary point, which is near
x=1/2
and has a value of around
n/2^{p}
, becomes significantly better than the endpoint solutions. This causes a phase transition in the lower bound that we are able to achieve with this method.

We now present our
1
-dimensional lower bound of
\Omega(\epsilon^{-1})
for
p\in(1,2)
. For easy reuse of our calculations from Theorem
12.2
, we state this result as a lower bound of
\Omega(\epsilon^{-2})
for any algorithm achieving an
O(\epsilon^{2})
-approximation. This can be reparameterized to an
\Omega(\epsilon^{-1})
lower bound for
O(\epsilon)
-approximations.

Theorem 12.4
.

Let
1<p<2
be a constant. Let
\epsilon>0
be sufficiently small and let
n=100\left\lceil\epsilon^{-2}\right\rceil
. Let
\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be the all ones vector. Let
\mathcal{D}_{0}
be the distribution over binary vectors
\mathbf{b}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
which independently draws each coordinate as a Bernoulli with bias
1/2+\epsilon
and let
\mathcal{D}_{1}
be the distribution which independently draws each coordinate as a Bernoulli with bias
1/2-\epsilon
. Then, there exists a constant
c
such that any
\tilde{x}
such that

\left\lVert\mathbf{a}\tilde{x}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}\leq(1+c\cdot\epsilon^{2})\min_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\left\lVert\mathbf{a}x-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}

distinguishes whether
\mathbf{b}\sim\mathcal{D}_{0}
or
\mathbf{b}\sim\mathcal{D}_{1}
with probability at least
99/100
.

Proof.

Many of our calculations from Theorem
12.2
directly carry over. Recall our notation of setting
r
to be the number of ones in
\mathbf{b}
, which is
r=n/2+a\epsilon n
for
a\in[1/2,3/2]
if
\mathbf{b}\sim\mathcal{D}_{0}
and
a\in[-3/2,-1/2]
if
\mathbf{b}\sim\mathcal{D}_{1}
. Recall also that the unique stationary point, which is the optimum now by convexity, is

x=\frac{1}{1+(n/r-1)^{1/(p-1)}}.

Optimal Solutions.

We now calculate the value of the optimum. We carry out calculations for
\mathbf{b}\sim\mathcal{D}_{0}
since
\mathbf{b}\sim\mathcal{D}_{1}
gives symmetric results. Note first that

\displaystyle(n/r-1)^{1/(p-1)}

\displaystyle=\left[\frac{1}{1/2+a\epsilon}-1\right]^{1/(p-1)}=\left[\frac{1-2a\epsilon}{1+2a\epsilon}\right]^{1/(p-1)}=\left[(1-2a\epsilon)\cdot\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}(-2a\epsilon)^{i}\right]^{1/(p-1)}

\displaystyle=\left[(1-2a\epsilon)-(2a\epsilon)(1-2a\epsilon)+(2a\epsilon)^{2}(1-2a\epsilon)+O(\epsilon^{3})\right]^{1/(p-1)}

\displaystyle=\left[1-4a\epsilon+2(2a\epsilon)^{2}+O(\epsilon^{3})\right]^{1/(p-1)}

\displaystyle=1-\frac{4a}{p-1}\epsilon+\frac{8a^{2}}{p-1}\epsilon^{2}+O(\epsilon^{3})

so

x=\frac{1}{2}\left\lparen 1+\frac{2a}{p-1}\epsilon-\frac{4a^{2}}{p-1}\epsilon^{2}+O(\epsilon^{3})\right\rparen.

Then, the objective value at this
x
is

\displaystyle\frac{n}{2^{p}}\left[\left\lparen\frac{1}{2}-a\epsilon\right\rparen\left\lparen 1+\frac{2a}{p-1}\epsilon-\frac{4a^{2}}{p-1}\epsilon^{2}+O(\epsilon^{3})\right\rparen^{p}+\left\lparen\frac{1}{2}+a\epsilon\right\rparen\left\lparen 1-\frac{2a}{p-1}\epsilon+\frac{4a^{2}}{p-1}\epsilon^{2}+O(\epsilon^{3})\right\rparen^{p}\right]

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\frac{n}{2^{p}}\left[\left\lparen\frac{1}{2}-a\epsilon\right\rparen\left\lparen 1+\frac{2pa}{p-1}\epsilon-\frac{4pa^{2}}{p-1}\epsilon^{2}+O(\epsilon^{3})\right\rparen+\left\lparen\frac{1}{2}+a\epsilon\right\rparen\left\lparen 1-\frac{2pa}{p-1}\epsilon+\frac{4pa^{2}}{p-1}\epsilon^{2}+O(\epsilon^{3})\right\rparen\right]

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\frac{n}{2^{p}}\left[1+\left\lparen\frac{1}{2}-a\epsilon\right\rparen\left\lparen\frac{2pa}{p-1}\epsilon-\frac{4pa^{2}}{p-1}\epsilon^{2}+O(\epsilon^{3})\right\rparen+\left\lparen\frac{1}{2}+a\epsilon\right\rparen\left\lparen-\frac{2pa}{p-1}\epsilon+\frac{4pa^{2}}{p-1}\epsilon^{2}+O(\epsilon^{3})\right\rparen\right]

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\frac{n}{2^{p}}\left[1-\frac{4pa^{2}}{p-1}\epsilon^{2}+O(\epsilon^{3})\right].

If
\mathbf{b}\sim\mathcal{D}_{1}
, then we have that

x=\frac{1}{2}\left\lparen 1-\frac{2a}{p-1}\epsilon+\frac{4a^{2}}{p-1}\epsilon^{2}+O(\epsilon^{3})\right\rparen

with the same objective value.

Suboptimal Solutions.

We now show that, given a nearly optimal solution
\tilde{x}
, we can determine whether
\mathbf{b}
is drawn from
\mathcal{D}_{0}
or
\mathcal{D}_{1}
with high probability by testing whether
\tilde{x}\in[1/2,1]
or
\tilde{x}\in[0,1/2]
. Again, assume by symmetry that
\mathbf{b}\sim\mathcal{D}_{0}
. Suppose that
x\in[0,1/2]
. Then,

x\leq\frac{1}{2}<\frac{1}{1+(n/r-1)^{1/(p-1)}}

which rearranges to

p(n-r)\cdot x^{p-1}-pr\cdot(1-x)^{p-1}<0

which means that the objective is decreasing on this interval. Thus, for
x\in[0,1/2]
, the smallest that the cost can be is
x=1/2
, which gives a value of

(n-r)\cdot(1/2)^{p}+r\cdot(1/2)^{p}=\frac{n}{2^{p}},

which is a factor of
1-\Theta(\epsilon^{2})
larger than the optimal solution. Thus, a
(1+\Theta(\epsilon^{2}))
-approximate solution can distinguish between
\mathcal{D}_{0}
and
\mathcal{D}_{1}
.
∎

Then, by combining Theorems
12.1
and
12.4
, we arrive at the following:

Theorem 12.5
.

Let
p\in(1,2)
be a constant. Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
and let
\mathbf{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Suppose that with probability at least
99/100
, an algorithm
\mathcal{A}
returns
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\tilde{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}\leq(1+\epsilon)\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}.

Then,
\mathcal{A}
queries
\Omega(d/\epsilon)
entries of
\mathbf{b}
in expectation.

12.3
Lower Bounds for
p>2

In the range of
p>2
, a lower bound of
\Omega(\epsilon^{1-p})
follows from Theorem 3 of
[
CSS21
]
, which shows that in the
1
-dimensional
\ell_{p}
power mean problem, which is equivalent to
1
-dimensional active
\ell_{p}
regression,
\Omega(\epsilon^{1-p})
queries are necessary to obtain
(1+\epsilon)
-approximate solutions. This matches our
\epsilon
dependence in the upper bound by at most
\epsilon
. For
d
dependence, it is clear that solving Problem
1.1
to any non-trivial relative error approximation factor for any norm requires at least
d
samples, as otherwise, we would have non-zero error in the case that
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}
. Here, we show that
\Omega(d^{p/2})
samples are required to solve the problem for any
\ell_{p}
norm with
p\geq 2
. In combination, this shows that the
\tilde{O}(d^{\max(1,p/2)})
bound of Theorem
1.2
is tight up to polylogarithmic factors.
We use the following standard fact from coding theory.

Lemma 12.6

(
[
PTB12
]
)
.

For any real
q\geq 1
and
d=2^{k}-1
for some integer
k
, there exists a set

\mathbf{S}\subset\{-1,1\}^{d}
and a constant
C_{q}
depending on
q
which satisfy

1.

|S|=d^{q}

2.

For any
s,t\in S
such that
s\neq t
,
|\langle s,t\rangle|\leq C_{q}\sqrt{d}
.

Using Lemma
12.6
with
q=p/2
, we can find
r=d^{p/2}
vectors
\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{r}

for which
\|x_{i}\|_{2}^{2}=d
and
|\langle\mathbf{x}_{i},\mathbf{x}_{j}\rangle|\leq C\sqrt{d}
for all
i\neq j
, for a constant
C>0
. We work with a fixed subset of
s=C^{\prime}\cdot d^{p/2}
of these vectors, for
a sufficiently small constant
C^{\prime}>0
. Let these
s
vectors form the rows
a_{1},\ldots,a_{s}
of
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{s\times d}
. We choose a uniformly random index
I\in[s]
and set
\mathbf{b}_{I}=d
, while we set

\mathbf{b}_{j}=0
for all
j\neq I
. The claim is that any solution to the
\ell_{p}
-regression problem
can be used to predict the index
I
. However, by standard information-theoretic arguments,
any algorithm for predicting
I
with constant
probability requires reading
\Omega(s)=\Omega(d^{p/2})
entries of
\mathbf{b}
.

To see the claim, consider a
(1+\epsilon)
-approximate solution vector
\mathbf{x}
to

\min_{\mathbf{x}}\|\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\|_{p}
, where
\epsilon>0
is less than a sufficiently small constant.
Suppose
|\langle a_{j},\mathbf{x}\rangle|\geq d/2
for some
j\neq I
.
Then the regression cost is at least
(d/2)^{p}
, since this is the cost on the
j
-th coordinate
alone. Alternatively, suppose
|\langle a_{I},\mathbf{x}\rangle|\leq d/2
.
Then the regression cost is at least
(d/2)^{p}
, since this is the cost on the
I
-th coordinate
alone. On the other hand, consider the solution vector
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}=a_{I}
.
Then the regression cost is at most

(s-1)\cdot(C\sqrt{d})^{p}\leq d^{p}\cdot C^{p}\cdot C^{\prime}.

Since
C^{\prime}>0
can be made an arbitrarily small constant (while suffering a constant factor
in the lower bound on the number of entries of
\mathbf{b}
read),
the regression cost is a constant factor smaller than
(d/2)^{p}
.

Hence, any
(1+\epsilon)
-approximate solution vector
\mathbf{x}
, for
\epsilon>0
less
than a small enough constant, must be such that
|\langle a_{I},\mathbf{x}\rangle|\geq d/2
and

|\langle a_{j},\mathbf{x}\rangle|<d/2
for all
j\neq I
. Thus,
from the solution vector
\mathbf{x}
, one can determine the value of
I
. We thus have:

Theorem 12.7
.

Let
p\geq 2
and
\epsilon>0
be less than a sufficiently small constant. Any algorithm which outputs
a
(1+\epsilon)
-approximate solution
\mathbf{x}
to the
\ell_{p}
-regression problem
\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\|_{p}^{p}

with constant probability requires reading
\Omega(d^{p/2})
entries of
\mathbf{b}
.

Lemma
12.6
has been used to obtain lower bounds for the stronger subspace
sketch problem
[
LWW20
]
, which is related, though does not immediately give a lower bound for regression.

12.4
A
1/\delta^{p-1}
Lower Bound for Sampling-and-Reweighting Algorithms

We next show that sampling-and-reweighting algorithms for
\ell_{p}
regression must pay a polynomial dependence in the failure probability
\delta
, contrasting with the logarithmic dependence achieved by our approach.

Theorem 12.8
.

Let
p>1
. Any sampling-and-reweighting algorithm which, with probability at least
1-\delta
,
outputs a
(1+\epsilon)
-approximate solution
\mathbf{x}

to the
\ell_{p}
-regression problem, for
\epsilon>0
less than a sufficiently small constant,
requires reading
\Omega(1/\delta^{p-1})
entries of
\mathbf{b}
.

Proof.

In our hard instance we will have
d=1
and require a sufficiently fine constant factor approximation
with failure probability
\delta
. Suppose, with these parameters, that there is an algorithm reading

s
entries. We set
n=s/\delta
, and will show that the algorithm cannot output
a constant factor approximation to the
\ell_{p}
-regression problem with probability at least

1-\delta
.

Let
\mathbf{A}
be a single column of
n

1
s. Since the entries of
\mathbf{A}
are indistinguishable from each other, we can assume without loss of generality that the sampling-and-reweighting algorithm samples entries uniformly at random. By assumption, since the rows of
\mathbf{A}
are all identical, the algorithm reweights the sampled rows uniformly (equivalently assigns weight
1
to each sampled entry). We choose
\mathbf{b}=\mathbf{e}_{I}
for a random standard basis vector
\mathbf{e}_{I}
.
For the optimal
x
, necessarily
0\leq x\leq 1
since if
x<0
, replacing
x
with
-x

would give lower cost. Similarly, if
x>1
, then replacing
x
with
1
would give lower cost.
Then the cost is
(1-x)^{p}+(n-1)x^{p}
. This is convex and differentiable for
p>1
, and is minimized when
the derivative is
0
. Differentiating, the optimal
x
satisfies

-p(1-x)^{p-1}+p(n-1)x^{p-1}=0
, or

(1-x)^{p-1}=(n-1)x^{p-1}
. Taking
(p-1)
-th roots,

1-x=(n-1)^{1/(p-1)}x
, or
x=1/(1+(n-1)^{1/(p-1)})
. The optimal cost is therefore

\left(1-\frac{1}{1+(n-1)^{1/(p-1)}}\right)^{p}+\frac{n-1}{(1+(n-1)^{1/(p-1)})^{p}}.

For
n=\omega(1)
, this is

\Theta(1+n^{1-p/(p-1)})=O(1)
for any constant
p>1
.

On the other hand, given that
n=s/\delta
, with probability at least
\delta
, the algorithm’s sample includes
\mathbf{b}_{I}
.
If this is the case, for the sampled problem, the cost is

(1-x)^{p-1}+(s-1)x^{p}
for a given
x
. Setting the derivative to
0
,
we now have that the optimal
x^{\prime}
for the sampled problem is:

x^{\prime}=\frac{1}{1+(s-1)^{1/(p-1)}}
.
Computing the cost of using
x^{\prime}
for the original problem, our cost of using
x^{\prime}
is

\left(1-\frac{1}{(1+(s-1)^{1/(p-1)})^{p}}\right)+\frac{(n-1)}{\left(1+(s-1)^{1/(p-1)}\right)^{p}}.

The cost is at least the second term, which for
n=\omega(1)
and
s=\delta n=\omega(1)
is

\Theta((s/\delta)/s^{p/(p-1)})
. This term must be
O(1)
to be an

O(1)
-approximation, by our above calculation of the optimal cost. Hence,

s^{p/(p-1)-1}=\Omega(1/\delta)
, or
s^{1/(p-1)}=\Omega(1/\delta)
, or

s=\Omega(1/\delta^{p-1})
.
∎

13
